for all
\alpha\in\Delta^{\operatorname{iso}}
.

In general,
\Delta_{\lambda}
can be constructed by the following procedure:

—

we set
X_{0}:=\overline{R}_{\lambda}
;

—

for odd
i>0
we take
X_{i+1}
to be the union of
X_{i}
, the sets

s_{\alpha}X_{i}
where

\alpha\in X_{i}\cap\Delta^{\operatorname{an}}
;

—

for even
i>0
we take
X_{i+1}
to be the union of
X_{i}
and the sets

\{\alpha\pm\beta\}\cap\Delta
for
\alpha,\beta\in X_{i}
satisfying

(\alpha,\alpha)=0\not=(\alpha,\beta)
.

Then
X_{0}\subset X_{1}\subset\ldots
and
X_{i}=-X_{i}
. Taking

X:=\bigcup_{i=0}^{\infty}X_{i}
we obtain

\Delta_{\lambda}=X\cup\{2\alpha|\ \alpha\in X\ \text{ and }2\alpha\in\Delta\}.

This implies the uniqueness of
\Delta_{\lambda}

and the inclusion

\Delta_{\lambda}\subset\mathbb{Z}\overline{R}_{\lambda}
.

4.1.4.

Lemma.

(i)

If
\alpha\in\Delta_{\lambda}\cap\Delta_{\overline{0}}

is such that
\alpha/2\not\in\Delta
and
(\gamma,\gamma)\in\mathbb{Z}(\alpha,\alpha)

for all
\gamma\in\overline{R}_{\lambda}
, then
\alpha\in\overline{R}_{\lambda}
.

(ii)

For any
\gamma\in\Delta_{\lambda}\setminus R_{\lambda}
there exists

\alpha\in R_{\lambda}
such that
(\gamma,\alpha)\not=0
.

(iii)

For any non-isotropic
\gamma\in\Delta_{\lambda}\setminus R_{\lambda}
there exists isotropic
\alpha\in\Delta_{\lambda}

such that
(\alpha,\gamma)\not=0
.

Proof.

For (i) normalise
(-,-)
by the condition
(\alpha,\alpha)=2
. Then

(\lambda,\gamma)\in\mathbb{Z}
for all
\gamma\in\overline{R}_{\lambda}
. Since
\Delta_{\lambda}\subset\mathbb{Z}R_{\lambda}
this gives

(\lambda,\gamma)\in\mathbb{Z}
for all
\gamma\in\Delta_{\lambda}
.
In particular,
(\lambda,\alpha)\in\mathbb{Z}
. Since
\alpha/2\not\in\Delta

we have
\alpha\in\overline{\Delta}^{\operatorname{re}}
. Therefore

\alpha\in\overline{\Delta}^{\operatorname{re}}_{\overline{0}}
and

\frac{2(\lambda,\alpha)}{(\alpha,\alpha)}=(\lambda,\alpha)\in\mathbb{Z}
. Hence

\alpha\in\overline{R}_{\lambda}
as required.

For (ii)
observe that
(\gamma,R_{\lambda})=0
implies

(\gamma,\Delta_{\lambda})=0
(since
\Delta_{\lambda}
is spanned by
R_{\lambda}
).
Hence for (ii), (iii)
it is enough to verify the existence of
\alpha\in\Delta_{\lambda}

satisfying
(\gamma,\alpha)\not=0
such that
\alpha
is isotropic if
\gamma
is anisotropic.
We retain notation of
\lx@sectionsign

4.1.3
. Take
\gamma\in\Delta_{\lambda}\setminus R_{\lambda}
. Without loss of generality we can assume that
\gamma\in X
, so

\gamma\in X_{i+1}\setminus X_{i}
for some
i\geq 0
.
We proceed by induction on
i
.

If
i
is even, then
\gamma\in X_{i+1}\setminus X_{i}

implies the existence of
\alpha,\beta\in X_{i}

such that
\gamma\in\{\alpha\pm\beta\}
and
(\alpha,\alpha)=0\not=(\alpha,\beta)
.
Then
(\gamma,\alpha)\not=0
which implies the required assertions
for the case when
i
is even (in particular, for
i=0
).

Consider the case when
i
is odd.
Then there exists
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\in X_{i}
such that
(\beta_{1},\beta_{1})
,
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})\not=0
and

\gamma=s_{\beta_{1}}\beta_{2}
. Then
(\gamma,\beta_{1})\not=0
and
this completes the proof of (ii).
For (iii) we assume that
\gamma
is anisotropic.
Then
\beta_{2}
is anisotorpic as well.
If
\Delta_{\lambda}
contains an isotropic root
\alpha_{2}

such that
(\beta_{2},\alpha_{2})\not=0
, then
(\gamma,\alpha)\not=0
for

\alpha:=s_{\beta_{1}}\alpha_{2}
and

\alpha\in\Delta_{\lambda}
is isotropic, since

\beta_{2},\alpha_{2}\in\Delta_{\lambda}
and
\alpha_{2}
is isotropic.
Consider the remaining case when

\Delta_{\lambda}
does not contain an isotropic root
\alpha_{2}

such that
(\beta_{2},\alpha_{2})\not=0
. By induction hypothesis this implies
\beta_{2}\in R_{\lambda}
.
Since
\gamma\not\in R_{\lambda}
, this forces

\beta_{1}\not\in R_{\lambda}
. By induction hypothesis, there exists isotropic
\alpha\in\Delta_{\lambda}

such that
(\beta_{1},\alpha)\not=0
. By above,
(\beta_{2},\alpha)=0
. Therefore

(\gamma,\alpha)=(s_{\beta_{1}}\beta_{2},\alpha)\not=0
(since
\gamma\not=\beta_{2}
).
This completes the proof of (iii).
∎

4.1.5.

Lemma.

Suppose that
Z\subset\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}
satisfies properties (b)–(d) in
\lx@sectionsign

4.1.1

(with
\Delta_{\lambda}
replaced by
Z
) and
Z
contains two odd real roots
\alpha,\beta
such that

(\beta,\beta)=0
,
(\alpha,\alpha)\not=0
,

\langle\beta,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle<0
.
Then

(\mathbb{R}\alpha+\mathbb{R}\beta)\cap Z=\{\pm\alpha,\pm\beta,\pm 2\alpha,\pm(\alpha+\beta),\pm(2\alpha+\beta)\}=(\mathbb{R}\alpha+\mathbb{R}\beta)\cap\Delta^{\operatorname{re}},

i.e.
(\mathbb{R}\alpha+\mathbb{R}\beta)\cap Z=(\mathbb{R}\alpha+\mathbb{R}\beta)\cap\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}

forms the root system

B(1|1)

3
3

3

The statement does not immediately follow from Serganova’s
classification of generalized root systems in
[
47
]
since we do not assume that

(\mathbb{R}\alpha+\mathbb{R}\beta)\cap\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}
is finite.
and
\{\alpha,\beta\}

is its base.

Proof.

Since
\beta
is an isotropic real root,
\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}
is of type (Fin) or (Aff).

Renormalise the bilinear form by taking
(\alpha,\alpha)=1
. Then

b:=-(\alpha,\beta)>0
and, by (
2.4
),

b\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
.
Recall that
\mathfrak{g}_{\pm\alpha}
generate a subalgebra isomorphic to
\mathfrak{osp}(1|2)
. Since
\beta
and
s_{\alpha}\beta=\beta+2b\alpha
are real roots,
one has
\alpha+\beta,2\alpha+\beta\in\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}
. One has

(\alpha+\beta,\alpha+\beta)=1-2b\not=0
,
so
\alpha+\beta
is a real root and

\frac{2(\alpha+\beta,\beta)}{(\alpha+\beta,\alpha+\beta)}\in\mathbb{Z}
that is

\frac{b}{2b-1}\in\mathbb{Z}
. Since
b
is a positive integer,
this implies
b=1
, so
s_{\alpha}\beta=\beta+2\alpha
.
Using properties (b)–(d)
we obtain

(\mathbb{R}\alpha+\mathbb{R}\beta)\cap Z\supset Y\ \ \text{ for }Y:=\{\pm\alpha,\pm\beta,\pm 2\alpha,\pm(\alpha+\beta),\pm(2\alpha+\beta)\}.

Let us show that
(\mathbb{R}\alpha+\mathbb{R}\beta)\cap\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}=Y
.

Set
\delta_{1}:=\alpha
,
\varepsilon_{1}:=\alpha+\beta
. Note that

\delta_{1}
is an odd root and
\varepsilon_{1}
is an even root.
Using
b=1
we get
(\delta_{1},\varepsilon_{1})=0
,
(\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{1})=-1
.

Suppose the contrary, that
(\mathbb{R}\alpha+\mathbb{R}\beta)\cap\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}\not=Y
.
Take
\gamma:=(x\varepsilon_{1}+y\delta_{1})\in(\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}\setminus Y)
.
Then
s_{\varepsilon_{1}}\gamma
,
s_{\delta_{1}}\gamma\in\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}
, so

(\pm x\varepsilon_{1}\pm y\delta_{1})\in\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}
. Without loss
of generality we can (and will) assume that
x,y\geq 0
.
If
x=0
, then
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}\alpha
, so
\gamma\in\{\delta_{1},2\delta_{1}\}\subset Y
.
Similarly, if
y=x
, then
\gamma=\varepsilon_{1}+\delta_{1}\in Y
. Therefore

x>0
,
y\geq 0
and
x\not=y
. Then
(\gamma,\gamma)\not=0
and the
conditions

\langle\gamma,\varepsilon_{1}^{\vee}\rangle,\langle\varepsilon_{1},\gamma^{\vee}\rangle,\langle\delta_{1},\gamma^{\vee}\rangle\in\mathbb{Z},\ \ \langle\gamma,\delta_{1}^{\vee}\rangle\in 2\mathbb{Z}

give
2x,y\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\frac{2x}{x^{2}-y^{2}},\frac{2y}{x^{2}-y^{2}}\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Therefore
2x\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
,
y\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
x\not=y
and

\frac{2}{x+y}\in\mathbb{Z}
. This gives

(x,y)\in\{(\frac{1}{2},0),(1,0),(2,0)\}.

In particular,
(\frac{\varepsilon_{1}}{2}+\delta_{1})\not\in\Delta
(in this case

x=\frac{1}{2}
,
y=1
).
If
(x,y)=(\frac{1}{2},0)
, then
\gamma=\frac{\varepsilon_{1}}{2}
, and

\delta_{1}
,
\varepsilon_{1}+\delta_{1}\in\Delta
forces

\frac{\varepsilon_{1}}{2}+\delta_{1}\in\Delta
, a contradiction.
If
(x,y)=(1,0)
, then
\gamma=\varepsilon_{1}\in Y
. If

(x,y)=(2,0)
, then
\gamma=2\varepsilon_{1}\not\in\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}

since
\varepsilon_{1}
is an even real root. Hence

(\mathbb{R}\alpha+\mathbb{R}\beta)\cap\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}=Y
. This completes the proof.
∎

4.1.6.

Corollary.

If
\beta\in\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}
is such that

2\beta\in\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}
, then
\beta\in\Delta_{\lambda}

if and only if
2\beta\in\Delta_{\lambda}
.

Proof.

If
\beta\in\Delta_{\lambda}
, then
2\beta\in\Delta_{\lambda}
by property (d)
of
\Delta_{\lambda}
.

Suppose the contrary, that
\beta\in\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}
is such that

\beta\not\in\Delta_{\lambda}
and
2\beta\in\Delta_{\lambda}
.
Since
2\beta
is a real root,

\beta
is an odd non-isotropic root.
If
2\beta\in R_{\lambda}
, then, by definition of
R_{\lambda}
, we have
\beta\in\overline{R}_{\lambda}
,
a contradiction. Therefore
2\beta\not\in R_{\lambda}
.
By Lemma
4.1.4
(iii),
\Delta_{\lambda}
contains an isotropic root
\alpha
such that

\langle\alpha,(2\beta)^{\vee}\rangle<0
.
Then, by Lemma
4.1.5
,

\beta,\alpha
form a base of the root subsystem

\Delta_{\lambda}\cap(\mathbb{Z}\beta+\mathbb{Z}\alpha)

which is of type
B(1|1)
, and
(\alpha+\beta,\alpha+\beta)=-(\beta,\beta)
.

Since
\alpha
is isotropic,
\Delta
is of type (Fin) or (Aff).
From the Kac and van de Leur classifications
[
24
]
and
[
49
]
we know that each odd
non-isotropic root in
\Delta
is a short root. Renormalise the bilinear form
(-,-)

in such a way that
(\beta,\beta)=2
. Since
\beta
is a short root,

(\gamma,\gamma)\in 2\mathbb{Z}
for all
\gamma\in\Delta
,
so
(\lambda,\gamma)\in\mathbb{Z}
for all
\gamma\in\overline{R}_{\lambda}
which implies

(\lambda,\gamma)\in\mathbb{Z}
for all
\gamma\in\Delta_{\lambda}
.
By above,
(\alpha+\beta)
is an even root with

(\alpha+\beta,\alpha+\beta)=-2
. Hence

\alpha+\beta,\alpha\in\Delta_{\lambda}
with

(\alpha,\alpha)=0\not=(\alpha,\beta)
.
Property (c) in
\lx@sectionsign

4.1.1

gives
\beta\in\Delta_{\lambda}
, a contradiction.
∎

4.1.7.

Lemma.

If
\alpha
is a real isotropic root and
\nu\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}
is such that

(\nu,\alpha)=0
, then
\Delta_{\nu}=\Delta_{\nu+\alpha}
.

Proof.

Let us verify that
\overline{R}_{\nu}\subset\Delta_{\nu+\alpha}
. Take
\beta\in\overline{R}_{\nu}
.

If
(\beta,\alpha)=0
, then
(\nu,\beta)=(\nu+\alpha,\beta)
, so

\beta\in\overline{R}_{\nu+\alpha}
. If

\beta
is anisotropic, then, by (
2.4
),

\langle\alpha,\beta^{\vee}\rangle
is integral
and is even if
\beta
is odd; thus

\beta\in\overline{R}_{\nu+\alpha}
. This gives

(4.3)

\{\beta\in\overline{R}_{\nu}|\ (\beta,\alpha)=0\}\subset\overline{R}_{\nu+\alpha},\ \ \ \ (\overline{R}_{\nu}\cap{\Delta}^{\operatorname{an}})\subset\overline{R}_{\nu+\alpha}.

Now take
\beta\in(\overline{R}_{\nu}\cap\Delta^{\operatorname{iso}})
such that
(\beta,\alpha)\not=0
.
By
\lx@sectionsign

4.1.1
(c), the set

\Delta_{\nu}\cap\{\alpha\pm\beta\}
is non-empty.
We assume that
(\alpha+\beta)\in\Delta_{\nu}

(otherwise we substitute

\beta
by
-\beta
).
Note that
\alpha+\beta
is a real even root (since
(\alpha+\beta,\alpha+\beta)=2(\alpha,\beta)\not=0
).
By above,
(\nu,\alpha)=(\nu,\beta)=0
, so

(\nu,\alpha+\beta)=0
.

Assume that
(\alpha+\beta)/2\not\in\Delta
. Then

(\alpha+\beta)\in(\overline{R}_{\nu}\cap{\Delta}^{\operatorname{an}})
, so

(\alpha+\beta)\in\overline{R}_{\nu+\alpha}

by (
4.3
). Therefore
\overline{R}_{\nu+\alpha}
contains both
\alpha

and
\alpha+\beta
. Since
(\alpha,\alpha+\beta)=(\alpha,\beta)\not=0
,

\lx@sectionsign

4.1.1
(c) gives

\beta\in\Delta_{\nu+\alpha}
as required.

Now consider the remaining case
\alpha+\beta=2\gamma
for
\gamma\in\Delta
.
Then
\gamma
is an odd non-isotropic root,
\alpha

is an isotropic root and
(\gamma,\alpha)\not=0
. By Lemma
4.1.5
,

\Delta\cap(\mathbb{R}\gamma+\mathbb{R}\alpha)

is the root system of type
B(1|1)
. By above,
\Delta
contains
\beta=\alpha-2\gamma
,
so, using the standard notation for
B(1|1)
,
we can assume
\gamma=\delta_{1}
and
\alpha=\delta_{1}+\varepsilon_{1}
.
We have
(\nu,\alpha)=(\nu,\beta)=0
, so
(\nu,\varepsilon_{1})=0
and

\langle\nu+\alpha,\varepsilon_{1}^{\vee}\rangle=\langle\delta_{1}+\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{1}^{\vee}\rangle=2.

Since
\varepsilon_{1}
is an even real root
and
\varepsilon_{1}/2\not\in\Delta
, we obtain

\varepsilon_{1}\in\overline{R}_{\nu+\alpha}
.
Using
\alpha=\delta_{1}+\varepsilon_{1}\in\overline{R}_{\nu+\alpha}
and
\lx@sectionsign

4.1.1
(b) we get
s_{\varepsilon_{1}}\alpha\in\Delta_{\nu+\alpha}
. One has

s_{\varepsilon_{1}}\alpha=\delta_{1}-\varepsilon_{1}=-\beta
.
Hence
\beta\in\Delta_{\nu+\alpha}

as required.

By above,
\overline{R}_{\nu}\subset\Delta_{\nu+\alpha}
. This gives
\Delta_{\nu}\subset\Delta_{\nu+\alpha}
.
Since
(\nu+\alpha,-\alpha)=0
we have
\Delta_{\nu+\alpha}\subset\Delta_{\nu}
.
This completes the proof.
∎

4.1.8.

Corollary.

If
L(\lambda_{1},\Sigma_{1})=L(\lambda,\Sigma)
for some
\Sigma_{1}\in\operatorname{Sp}

and
\rho,\rho_{1}
are the Weyl vectors of
\Sigma
and
\Sigma_{1}
respectively, then

\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}=\Delta_{\lambda_{1}+\rho_{1}}
.

Proof.

It is enough to verify the assertion for
\Sigma_{1}=r_{\alpha}\Sigma

where
\alpha\in(\Sigma\cap\Delta^{\operatorname{iso}})
.

If
(\lambda+\rho,\alpha)\not=0
, then
\lambda_{1}+\rho_{1}=\lambda+\rho
,
so
\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}=\Delta_{\lambda_{1}+\rho_{1}}
.

If
(\lambda+\rho,\alpha)=0
, then

\lambda_{1}+\rho_{1}=\lambda+\rho+\alpha
and the assertion follows from Lemma
4.1.7
.
∎

4.2.

Definition.
For an irreducible module
L\in\mathcal{O}_{\Sigma}(\mathfrak{g})

we set
\Delta_{L}:=\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}
where
\lambda
is the highest weight of
L
.
If
N\in{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{g})
is such that
\Delta_{L}
is the same for all
irreducible subquotients of
N
, we denote this set by
\Delta_{N}
; in this
case we say that
\Delta_{N}
is well defined.

4.2.1.

By Corollary
4.1.8
the set
\Delta_{L}
does not depend on
the choice of a base in the spine. In
\lx@sectionsign

4.6.6
we will see that
\Delta_{N}
is defined
for any indecomposable non-critical module
N
.

4.2.2.

By Theorem
B.6
, if
\mathfrak{g}(A,\tau)
is of type (Fin) or (Aff), but different from
\mathfrak{g}(A,\tau)\not=A(2m-1|2n-1)^{(2)}
,
and

L\in\mathcal{O}_{\Sigma}(\mathfrak{g})
is a non-critical irreducible module, then
there exists a symmetrizable Kac-Moody superalgebra
\mathfrak{g}(A^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})

such that

(a)

the sets
\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}(\mathfrak{g}(A^{\prime},\tau^{\prime}))
and
\Delta_{L}
are isometric; denote by

\psi:\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}(\mathfrak{g}(A^{\prime},\tau^{\prime}))\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\Delta_{L}
the isometry;

(b)

for any
\alpha^{\prime}\in\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}(\mathfrak{g}(A^{\prime},\tau^{\prime}))
,
the roots
\alpha^{\prime}
and
\psi(\alpha^{\prime})\in\Delta

have the same parity;

(c)

any indecomposable submatrix
A_{1}^{\prime}
of
A^{\prime}
is of type (Fin) or (Aff); moreover, for such
A^{\prime}

the restriction of
\psi
gives a monomorphism

\mathbb{C}\Delta(\mathfrak{g}(A^{\prime}_{1},\tau_{1}^{\prime}))\to\mathbb{C}\Delta
.

For
\mathfrak{g}(A,\tau)=A(2m-1|2n-1)^{(2)}
the similar assertion holds
if
\Delta_{L}
does not have an indecomposable component
of type
A(1|1)^{(2)}
.

For type (An) Proposition
B.8
implies the existence
of
\mathfrak{g}(A^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})
, where
A^{\prime}
can be of infinite size, and the isometry

\psi:\mathbb{C}\Delta(\mathfrak{g}(A^{\prime},\tau^{\prime}))\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{C}\Delta
satisfying (b).

From now on we always exclude the case when
\mathfrak{g}(A,\tau)=A(2m-1|2n-1)^{(2)}
and
\Delta_{L}
has an indecomposable component
of type
A(1|1)^{(2)}
.

Remark

If
\mathfrak{g}(A,\tau)
is of type (Aff) and
L
is critical, then
a similar result holds, but
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
might be a countable product
of Kac-Moody superalgebras, with almost all factors isomorphic to
\mathfrak{gl}(1|1)
,
see Lemma
B.6.5
.

4.2.3.
Example

Let
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sl}_{4}^{(1)}
and
L=L(\lambda)
for
\lambda:=\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2}+\frac{\varepsilon_{3}-\varepsilon_{4}}{2}
.
Then

\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}=\{\pm(\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2})+\mathbb{Z}\delta\}\cup\{\pm(\varepsilon_{3}-\varepsilon_{4})+\mathbb{Z}\delta\}

and
\mathfrak{g}(A^{\prime})=\mathfrak{sl}_{2}^{(1)}\times\mathfrak{sl}_{2}^{(1)}
. Let

\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{0}\}
,
\{\beta_{1},\beta_{0}\}
be the sets of simple roots for two copies
of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}^{(1)}
in
\mathfrak{g}(A^{\prime})
. The isometry
\psi
is given by

\psi(\alpha_{1}):=\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2},\ \ \psi(\alpha_{0})=\delta-(\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2}),\ \ \psi(\beta_{1})=\varepsilon_{3}-\varepsilon_{4},\ \ \psi(\beta_{0})=\delta-(\varepsilon_{3}-\varepsilon_{4}).

We see that the restriction of
\psi
induces a bijection (of sets) between

\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}(\mathfrak{g}(A^{\prime}))
and
\Delta_{L}
, but the restriction of
\psi
to
\Delta(\mathfrak{g}(A^{\prime}))
is not injective:
\psi
maps
the minimal imaginary roots in both copies of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}^{(1)}
to the minimal imaginary root of
\mathfrak{sl}_{4}^{(1)}
.

4.2.4.
Example

Take
\mathfrak{g}=A(1|3)^{(2)}

with
\Sigma=\{\delta-2\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{1}-\delta_{1},\delta_{1}-\delta_{2},\delta_{2}-\delta_{3},2\delta_{3}\}

and
\lambda=\Lambda_{0}+\frac{3}{4}\delta_{2}+\frac{1}{4}\delta_{3}
. Then
\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}=A(1|1)^{(2)}\times A_{1}^{(1)}\times A(1|1)^{(1)}
,where

A(1|1)^{(2)}=\{\mathbb{Z}\delta\pm\varepsilon_{1}\pm\delta_{1},\ 2\mathbb{Z}\delta\pm 2\delta_{1},\ (2\mathbb{Z}-1)\delta\pm 2\varepsilon_{1}\},

and two copies of
A_{1}^{(1)}
correspond to the subsystems

A_{1}^{(1)}=\{\mathbb{Z}\delta\pm\alpha\}

for
\alpha=\delta_{2}-\delta_{3}
and
\alpha=\delta_{2}+\delta_{3}
.

4.2.5.

Lemma.

Fix a base
\Sigma
and set
\Delta_{L}^{+}:=\Delta_{L}\cap\Delta^{+}
.

(i)

The root system
\Delta(\mathfrak{g}(A^{\prime},\tau^{\prime}))
admits a base
\Sigma^{\prime}
such that

\psi\bigl((\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}(\mathfrak{g}(A^{\prime},\tau^{\prime}))^{+}\bigr)=\Delta_{L}^{+}.

(ii)

The set
\Sigma_{L}:=\psi(\Sigma^{\prime})
coincides with the set of indecomposable elements in
\Delta_{L}^{+}

(i.e., the elements which can not be presented as a sum of several elements from
\Delta_{L}^{+}
).

Proof.

For type (An) (i) follows from
[
39
]
, Section 2; in this case

\Sigma_{L}=\{\alpha\in\Delta_{L}|\ s_{\alpha}\bigl(\Delta_{L}^{+}\setminus\{\alpha,2\alpha\}\bigr)\subset\Delta_{L}^{+}\},\ \ \ \Sigma^{\prime}:=\psi^{-1}(\Sigma_{L}).

Let us prove both statements for types (Fin) and (Aff).
It is enough to check the statements for each indecomposable component of
\mathfrak{g}(A^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})
.
Let
\Delta^{\prime}
be the root system of
such component. Write
\Sigma:=\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
and let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}

be
\mathbb{Q}
-linearly independent positive real numbers.
Define a homomorphism
\omega:\mathbb{Z}\Delta\to\mathbb{R}
by taking
\omega(\alpha_{i}):=a_{i}
.
Note that
\omega(\mu)\not=0
for all non-zero
\mu\in\mathbb{Q}\Delta
.
In particular,
\omega(\alpha)\not=0
for all
\alpha\in\Delta
and

\Delta^{+}\coprod\Delta^{-}
is the triangular decomposition corresponding to
\omega
in the sense
of
\lx@sectionsign

2.10
. Since
\Delta^{\prime}
lies in
\mathbb{Z}(\Delta^{\prime})^{\operatorname{re}}
we have

\psi(\Delta^{\prime})\subset\mathbb{Z}\Delta
and property (c) in
\lx@sectionsign

4.2.2
implies

(\omega\circ\psi)(\mu^{\prime})\not=0
for all
\mu^{\prime}\in\Delta^{\prime}
.
Therefore
\omega\circ\psi:\mathbb{Z}\Delta^{\prime}\to\mathbb{R}
determines triangular decomposition

\Delta^{\prime}=-(\Delta^{\prime})^{+}\coprod(\Delta^{\prime})^{+}

in the sense of
\lx@sectionsign

2.10
. If
\alpha^{\prime}\in(\Delta^{\prime})^{+}
, then

(\omega\circ\psi)(\alpha^{\prime})>0
that is
\psi(\alpha^{\prime})\in\Delta_{L}^{+}
.
Since
\Delta^{\prime}
is the root system of a Kac-Moody superalgebra
of type (Fin) or (Aff),
any triangular decomposition of
\Delta^{\prime}
admits a base (see
Theorem 0.4.3 in
[
15
]
).
This completes the proof of (i).
Now (ii) follows from (i) and the fact that
\Sigma^{\prime}
coincides with the set of indecomposable elements in

\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}(\mathfrak{g}(A^{\prime},\tau^{\prime}))^{+}
.
∎

4.2.6.

Let
\mathfrak{g}(A^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})
and
\psi
be as in
\lx@sectionsign

4.2.2
and set

\Delta^{\prime}:=\Delta(\mathfrak{g}(A^{\prime},\tau^{\prime}))
.
Recall that

\psi
gives a bijection
(\Delta^{\prime})^{\operatorname{re}}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\Delta_{L}
.
Using Lemma
4.2.5
(i),
we assign to each base
\Sigma_{1}
of
\Delta
a base of
\Delta^{\prime}
, which we denote by
\Upsilon(\Sigma_{1})
, which satisfies the property

\psi\bigl((\Delta^{\prime})^{\operatorname{re}}\cap\Delta^{+}(\Upsilon(\Sigma_{1}))\bigr)=\Delta_{L}\cap\Delta^{+}(\Sigma_{1}).

We claim that for each isotropic
\alpha\in\Sigma
we have

(4.4)

\Upsilon(r_{\alpha}\Sigma)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}\Upsilon(\Sigma)&&\text{ if }\ \alpha\not\in\psi(\Upsilon(\Sigma)),\\
r_{\psi^{-1}(\alpha)}\Upsilon(\Sigma)&&\text{ if }\ \alpha\in\psi(\Upsilon(\Sigma)).\\
\end{array}\right.

Indeed,
\Delta^{+}\setminus(\Delta^{+}(r_{\alpha}\Sigma))=\{\alpha\}

by (
2.2
). Setting
\Sigma^{\prime}:=\Upsilon(\Sigma)
we get

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\Delta^{+}((r_{\alpha}\Sigma^{\prime}))=\Delta^{+}(\Sigma^{\prime})&&\text{ if }\ \alpha\not\in\Delta_{\lambda+\rho},\\
\Delta^{+}\setminus(\Delta^{+}(r_{\alpha}\Sigma)^{\prime})=\Delta^{+}(\Sigma^{\prime})\cap\mathbb{Z}\psi^{-1}(\alpha)&&\text{ if }\ \alpha\in\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}.\end{array}

By Lemma
4.2.5
(ii), the condition

\alpha\not\in\Delta_{L}
is equivalent to the condition

\alpha\not\in\psi(\Sigma^{\prime})
. This implies (
4.4
).

Notice that, since
\psi
preserves the bilinear
form,
\psi^{-1}(\alpha)
is isotropic.
Denote by
\operatorname{Sp}^{\prime}
the spine of
\Sigma^{\prime}
. Using (
4.4
) we obtain the correspondence

\Upsilon:\operatorname{Sp}\to\operatorname{Sp}^{\prime}
. Viewing
\operatorname{Sp},\operatorname{Sp}^{\prime}

as graphs were vertices are the bases, and
the edges correspond to the isotropic reflexions

r_{\alpha}
, we obtain the following description of
\Upsilon
:

\Upsilon(\Sigma)=\Sigma^{\prime}
and for every edge

r_{\alpha}:\Sigma_{1}\to\Sigma_{2}
in
\operatorname{Sp}
we have
\Upsilon(\Sigma_{2}):=\Sigma^{\prime}_{2}

if
\operatorname{Sp}^{\prime}
contains the edge
r_{\psi^{-1}(\alpha)}:\Sigma^{\prime}_{1}\to\Sigma^{\prime}_{2}
and

\Upsilon(\Sigma_{2}):=\Sigma^{\prime}_{1}
otherwise.

For example, for
\mathfrak{sl}(2|1)
take
\Sigma=\{\alpha,\beta\}
where
\beta
is isotropic
and
\alpha
is anisotropic.
Take
\lambda+\rho=a\beta
for
a\not\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Then
\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}=\{\pm\beta\}
, so
\mathfrak{g}(A^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})\cong\mathfrak{gl}(1|1)

with
\Sigma^{\prime}=\{\beta^{\prime}\}
(
\beta^{\prime}
is isotropic). Then
\Upsilon:\operatorname{Sp}\to\operatorname{Sp}^{\prime}
takes the following form

\textstyle{\operatorname{Sp}}
\textstyle{\Sigma\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r_{\beta}}
\scriptstyle{\Upsilon}
\textstyle{\Sigma_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r_{\alpha+\beta}}
\scriptstyle{\Upsilon}
\textstyle{\Sigma_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Upsilon}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r_{\beta^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{\prime}_{1}}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{\prime}_{1}}

4.2.7.

One can similarly introduce the correspondence between the skeleton
\operatorname{Sk}

and the skeleton
\operatorname{Sk}^{\prime}
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime\prime}
be an indecomposable component of
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
.
Assume that
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime\prime}
is of type (Aff) and let

\delta^{\prime\prime}
be the minimal imaginary root of
\Delta^{+}(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime\prime})
. Then

\delta^{\prime\prime}
is a positive root for any triangular decomposition
appearing in
\operatorname{Sk}^{\prime}
, so
\psi(\delta^{\prime\prime})
lies in
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\Delta^{+}(\Sigma_{1})
for any

\Sigma_{1}\in\operatorname{Sk}
. By
[
11
]
, Proposition 7.6.1 this implies

\psi(\delta^{\prime\prime})\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\delta
.

4.3.
Module
L^{\prime}

Take
L=L(\lambda)
.
The following construction is a slight variation of the construction given in
[
14
]
.
Retain notation of
\lx@sectionsign

4.2.6
.
We set

\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}:=\mathfrak{g}(A^{\prime},\tau^{\prime}),\ \ \Delta^{\prime}:=\Delta(\mathfrak{g}(A^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})).

Recall that
\psi
gives a bijection
(\Delta^{\prime})^{\operatorname{re}}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\Delta_{L}
, see
\lx@sectionsign

4.2.2
. We set

(\Delta^{\prime})^{\perp}:=\{\mu^{\prime}\in(\mathfrak{h}^{\prime})^{*}|\ (\mu^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime})=0\}
and
let
\psi_{*}:\mathfrak{h}^{*}\to(\mathfrak{h}^{\prime})^{*}/(\Delta^{\prime})^{\perp}
be the linear map satisfying

(4.5)

(\psi_{*}(\nu),\mu^{\prime})=(\nu,\psi(\mu^{\prime}))\ \ \text{ for all }\mu^{\prime}\in\Delta^{\prime}.

4.3.1.

Take
\Sigma^{\prime}
as in Lemma
4.2.5
and let

\mathfrak{g}(A^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})=(\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})^{-}\oplus\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}\oplus(\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})^{+}
be the corresponding triangular decomposition of

\mathfrak{g}(A^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})
; let
\rho^{\prime}\in(\mathfrak{h}^{\prime})^{*}
be the corresponding
Weyl vector. Fix

\lambda^{\prime}\in(\mathfrak{h}^{\prime})^{*}
such that

(4.6)

(\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}):=(\lambda+\rho,\psi(\alpha^{\prime}))\ \text{ for all }\alpha^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{\prime}.

and set

(4.7)

L^{\prime}:=L_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}(\lambda^{\prime}).

Note that the choice of
\lambda^{\prime}
is not unique:
\lambda^{\prime\prime}
satisfies (
4.6
)
if and only if
\psi_{*}(\lambda^{\prime})=\psi_{*}(\lambda^{\prime\prime})
, or equivalently, if

L_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}(\lambda^{\prime\prime})
and
L^{\prime}
are isomorphic as
[\mathfrak{g}^{\prime},\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}]
-modules.

4.3.2.

Lemma.

(i)

For any
\mu^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}\Delta^{\prime}
we have

\psi(\overline{R}_{\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime}-\mu^{\prime}})\subset\overline{R}_{\lambda+\rho-\psi(\mu^{\prime})},\ \ \ \psi({\Delta}_{\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime}-\mu^{\prime}})\subset\Delta_{\lambda+\rho-\psi(\mu^{\prime})}.

(ii)

We have
\psi(\overline{R}_{\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime}})=\overline{R}_{\lambda+\rho}

and
\Delta_{L^{\prime}}=(\Delta^{\prime})^{\operatorname{re}}
.

Proof.

For any
\mu^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}\Delta^{\prime}
and
\beta^{\prime}\in\Delta^{\prime}

we have
(\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime}-\mu^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})=(\lambda+\rho-\psi(\mu^{\prime}),\psi(\beta^{\prime}))
by (
4.6
).
Since
\psi
preserves
(-,-)
and the parity, this implies

\psi(\overline{R}_{\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime}-\mu^{\prime}})\subset\overline{R}_{\lambda+\rho-\psi(\mu^{\prime})}
.
If
\mathfrak{g}
is of type (An), then
\Delta_{\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime}-\mu^{\prime}}=R_{\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime}-\mu^{\prime}}

and
\Delta_{\lambda+\rho-\mu}=R_{\lambda+\rho-\mu}
.
If
\mathfrak{g}
is of type (Fin) or (Aff), then, by definition,
\Delta_{\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime}-\mu^{\prime}}

is the minimal subset of
(\Delta^{\prime})^{\operatorname{re}}
which contains

\overline{R}_{\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime}-\mu^{\prime}}
and
satisfies properties (b)–(d) in
\lx@sectionsign

4.1.1
.
Therefore
\psi(\Delta_{\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime}-\mu^{\prime}})
is a subset of
\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}
which contains

\psi(\overline{R}_{\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime}-\mu^{\prime}})
and
satisfying properties (b)–(d). By above,
\psi(\overline{R}_{\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime}-\mu^{\prime}})\subset\overline{R}_{\lambda+\rho-\psi(\mu^{\prime})}
, so

\psi({\Delta}_{\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime}-\mu^{\prime}})\subset\Delta_{\lambda+\rho-\psi(\mu^{\prime})}
. This establishes (i).

For (ii) take any
\beta\in\Delta_{L}
. Then
\beta^{\prime}:=\psi^{-1}(\beta)\in(\Delta^{\prime})^{\operatorname{re}}

and
(\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})=(\lambda+\rho,\beta)
by (
4.6
). Since

\psi
preserves
(-,-)
and the parity, we obtain

\beta\in\overline{R}_{\lambda+\rho}
if and only if
\beta^{\prime}\in\overline{R}_{\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime}}
.
Therefore
\psi(\overline{R}_{\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime}})=\overline{R}_{\lambda+\rho}
.

If
\mathfrak{g}
is of type (An), then
\Delta_{L}=R_{\lambda+\rho}
and

\Delta_{L^{\prime}}=R_{\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime}}
which gives
\psi(\Delta_{L^{\prime}})=\Delta_{L}
and implies

\Delta_{L^{\prime}}=(\Delta^{\prime})^{\operatorname{re}}
.

If
\mathfrak{g}
is of type (Fin) or (Aff), then, by definition,
\Delta_{L^{\prime}}
is the minimal subset of
(\Delta^{\prime})^{\operatorname{re}}
which contains

\overline{R}_{\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime}}
and
satisfies properties (b)–(d) in
\lx@sectionsign

4.1.1
.
Therefore
\psi(\Delta_{L^{\prime}})\subset\Delta
contains

\overline{R}_{\lambda+\rho}
and
satisfies properties (b)–(d). Therefore

\psi(\Delta_{L^{\prime}})
contains
\Delta_{L}
(since
\Delta_{L}
is the minimal subset of
\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}
which contains

\overline{R}_{\lambda+\rho}
and
satisfies (b)–(d)). Since the restriction of
\psi
gives a bijection between

(\Delta^{\prime})^{\operatorname{re}}
and
\Delta_{L}
, we obtain
\Delta_{L^{\prime}}=(\Delta^{\prime})^{\operatorname{re}}
as required.
∎

4.3.3.

We let
\operatorname{Sp}^{\prime}
be the spine of
\Sigma^{\prime}
(if
\mathfrak{g}
is of type (An), then
\operatorname{Sp}=\{\Sigma\}
and

\operatorname{Sp}^{\prime}=\{\Sigma^{\prime}\}
). Let
b_{L}:\operatorname{Sp}\to\mathfrak{h}^{*}
,
b_{L^{\prime}}:\operatorname{Sp}^{\prime}\to(\mathfrak{h}^{\prime})^{*}
be the maps given by

b_{L}(\Sigma_{1}):=\operatorname{hwt}_{\Sigma_{1}}L,\ \ \ b_{L^{\prime}}(\Sigma^{\prime}_{1}):=\operatorname{hwt}_{\Sigma^{\prime}_{1}}L^{\prime},

see
\lx@sectionsign

2.12
for the notation (one has

L=L(\operatorname{hwt}_{\Sigma_{1}}L-\rho_{\Sigma_{1}},\Sigma_{1})
).
Taking
\Upsilon:\operatorname{Sp}\to\operatorname{Sp}^{\prime}
as in
\lx@sectionsign

4.2.6
and
\psi_{*}:(\mathfrak{h}^{\prime})^{*}\to(\mathfrak{h}^{\prime})^{*}/(\Delta^{\prime})^{\perp}

given by (
4.5
)
we obtain the following diagram:

(4.8)

\textstyle{\operatorname{Sp}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{L}}
\scriptstyle{\Upsilon}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{h}^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{*}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Sp}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{L^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{(\mathfrak{h}^{\prime})^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\mathfrak{h}^{\prime})^{*}/(\Delta^{\prime})^{\perp}}

4.4.

Lemma.

The diagram (
4.8
) is commutative.

Proof.

We have to verify that

(4.9)

(\operatorname{hwt}_{\Sigma^{\prime}_{1}}L^{\prime},\mu)=(\operatorname{hwt}_{\Sigma_{1}}L,\psi(\mu))\ \ \text{ for all }\ \ \mu\in\Delta^{\prime}.

For
\Sigma_{1}=\Sigma

the required formula follows from the constriuction of
L^{\prime}
, see (
4.6
).
By the definition of
\operatorname{Sp}
,
it is enough to verify that (
4.9
) holds for

\Sigma_{1}=r_{\alpha}\Sigma

with
\alpha\in(\Sigma\cap\Delta^{\operatorname{iso}})
.

Consider the case
(\operatorname{hwt}_{\Sigma}L,\alpha)=0
. Then

\alpha\in\overline{R}_{\lambda+\rho}
, so
\alpha\in\Delta_{L}
. Since

\alpha\in\Sigma
we have
\psi^{-1}(\alpha)\in\Sigma^{\prime}
by Lemma
4.2.5
(ii),
so (
4.4
) gives

\Sigma_{1}^{\prime}=r_{\psi^{-1}(\alpha)}\Sigma^{\prime}
. Formula (
4.9
) for
\Sigma

gives

(\operatorname{hwt}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}L^{\prime},\psi^{-1}(\alpha))=(\operatorname{hwt}_{\Sigma}L,\alpha)=0.

By (
2.7
) we obtain

\operatorname{hwt}_{\Sigma_{1}}L=\operatorname{hwt}_{\Sigma}L+\alpha
and
\operatorname{hwt}_{\Sigma^{\prime}_{1}}L^{\prime}=\operatorname{hwt}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}L^{\prime}+\psi^{-1}(\alpha)
.
Thus

(\operatorname{hwt}_{\Sigma^{\prime}_{1}}L^{\prime},\mu)=(\operatorname{hwt}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}L^{\prime}+\psi^{-1}(\alpha),\mu)=(\operatorname{hwt}_{\Sigma}L,\psi(\mu))+(\alpha,\psi(\mu))=(\operatorname{hwt}_{\Sigma_{1}}L,\psi(\mu))

as required.

Consider the case
(\operatorname{hwt}_{\Sigma}L,\alpha)\not=0
. By (
2.7
) we have

\operatorname{hwt}_{\Sigma_{1}}L=\operatorname{hwt}_{\Sigma}L
.
By (
4.4
),
one has either
\Sigma_{1}^{\prime}=\Sigma^{\prime}

or
\Sigma_{1}^{\prime}=r_{\psi^{-1}(\alpha)}\Sigma^{\prime}
and
\alpha\in\psi(\Sigma^{\prime})
.
In the latter case, using (
4.9
) for
\Sigma
, we get

(\operatorname{hwt}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}L^{\prime},\psi^{-1}(\alpha))=(\operatorname{hwt}_{\Sigma}L,\alpha)\not=0
,
so (
2.7
) gives

\operatorname{hwt}_{\Sigma^{\prime}_{1}}L^{\prime}=\operatorname{hwt}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}L^{\prime}
.
Hence in both cases
\operatorname{hwt}_{\Sigma_{1}}L=\operatorname{hwt}_{\Sigma}L
and
\operatorname{hwt}_{\Sigma^{\prime}_{1}}L^{\prime}=\operatorname{hwt}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}L^{\prime}
,
so formula (
4.9
) for
\Sigma_{1}
follows from the same formula for
\Sigma
.
∎

4.4.1.

The commutativity of the diagram (
4.8
) means that
the
[\mathfrak{g}^{\prime},\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}]
-module
L^{\prime}
defined in (
4.7
)
does not depend on the choice of a base:
if we apply the same procedure
to the pairs
(\lambda,\Sigma)
and
(\lambda_{1},\Sigma_{1})
such that
\Sigma_{1}\in\operatorname{Sp}
and

L=L(\lambda,\Sigma)=L(\lambda_{1},\Sigma_{1})
,
then the modules
L^{\prime}=L_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}(\lambda^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
and
L_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}(\lambda_{1}^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime}_{1})

are isomorphic as
[\mathfrak{g}^{\prime},\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}]
-modules.

4.5.

Definition.

Let
\sim
be the equivalence relation on the set of isomorphism classes of
irreducible modules in
\mathcal{O}_{\Sigma}(\mathfrak{g})

which is generated by the following relation:
L_{1}\sim L_{2}

if there exists
\Sigma_{1}\in\operatorname{Sp}
and
\alpha
such that

(4.10)

\alpha\in(\Sigma_{1}\cap\Delta^{\operatorname{an}}),\ \ \alpha\not\in\Delta_{\operatorname{hwt}_{\Sigma_{1}}L_{1}}\ ,\ \ \ \operatorname{hwt}_{\Sigma_{1}}L_{2}=s_{\alpha}(\operatorname{hwt}_{\Sigma_{1}}L_{1}).

4.5.1.

For
L=L(\lambda)
we will use the following notation:

\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{\lambda+\rho}:=\mathfrak{g}^{\prime},\ \ \psi_{\lambda+\rho}:=\psi,\ \ \Sigma^{\prime}_{\lambda+\rho}:=\Sigma^{\prime}\subset\Delta(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{\lambda+\rho}),\ \ \Sigma_{\lambda+\rho}:=\psi(\Sigma^{\prime})\subset\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}

where
\Sigma^{\prime}
is the base introduced in Lemma
4.2.5
.
We denote by
\lambda^{\prime}

the weight given by (
4.6
).

4.5.2.

Lemma.

If
\lambda+\rho=s_{\alpha}(\nu+\rho)
for
\alpha\in(\Sigma\cap\Delta^{\operatorname{an}})
such that

\alpha\not\in\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}
, then

(i)

the algebra
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{\nu+\rho}
can be identified
with
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}:=\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{\lambda+\rho}
in such a way that
\psi_{\nu+\rho}=s_{\alpha}\psi_{\lambda+\rho}
and

\Sigma^{\prime}_{\nu+\rho}=\Sigma^{\prime}_{\lambda+\rho}
;

(ii)

\Delta_{\nu+\rho}^{+}=s_{\alpha}\Sigma_{\lambda+\rho}^{+}
and

\Sigma_{\nu+\rho}=s_{\alpha}\Sigma_{\lambda+\rho}
;

(iii)

for this identification
L_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}(\nu^{\prime})
and
L_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}(\lambda^{\prime})
are isomorphic as
[\mathfrak{g}^{\prime},\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}]
-modules.

Proof.

One has

\overline{R}_{\nu+\rho}=s_{\alpha}\overline{R}_{\lambda+\rho}
, so
\Delta_{\nu+\rho}=s_{\alpha}\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}
.
This allows to identify

\mathfrak{g}_{\nu+\rho}
with
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}:=\mathfrak{g}_{\lambda+\rho}
by taking

\psi_{\nu+\rho}:=s_{\alpha}\circ\psi_{\lambda+\rho}
. This gives (i).

By Corollary
4.1.6
we have
2\alpha\not\in\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}
.
Since
\alpha\in\Sigma
and
\alpha,2\alpha\not\in\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}
,
the formula

\Delta_{\nu+\rho}=s_{\alpha}\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}
forces

\Delta^{+}_{\nu+\rho}=s_{\alpha}\Delta^{+}_{\lambda+\rho}
.
By Lemma
4.2.5
. the sets
\Sigma_{\lambda+\rho}
,
\Sigma_{\nu+\rho}
are
the sets of indecomposable elements in
\Delta^{+}_{\lambda+\rho}
,
\Delta^{+}_{\nu+\rho}
respectively. This gives

\Sigma_{\nu+\rho}=s_{\alpha}\Sigma_{\lambda+\rho}
and establishes (ii).
By definition (
4.6
) for any
\mu^{\prime}\in\Delta(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})
we have

\begin{array}[]{rl}(\nu^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})&=(\nu+\rho,\psi_{\nu+\rho}(\mu))=(\nu+\rho,s_{\alpha}\circ\psi_{\lambda+\rho}(\mu))=(s_{\alpha}(\nu+\rho),\psi_{\lambda+\rho}(\mu))\\
&=(\lambda+\rho,\psi_{\lambda+\rho}(\mu))=(\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime},\mu).\end{array}

Therefore
(\lambda^{\prime}-\nu^{\prime},\Delta(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}))=0
, so
L_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}(\lambda^{\prime})

and
L_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}(\nu^{\prime})
are isomorphic as
[\mathfrak{g}^{\prime},\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}]
-modules.
∎

Using
\lx@sectionsign

4.4.1
we obtain

4.5.3.

Corollary.

If
L(\nu)\sim L(\lambda)
, then

\mathfrak{g}_{\nu+\rho}
can be identified with
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}:=\mathfrak{g}_{\lambda+\rho}

in such a way that
L_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}(\nu^{\prime})
and
L_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}(\lambda^{\prime})
are isomorphic as
[\mathfrak{g}^{\prime},\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}]
-modules.

4.6.
Set
U(\lambda)

For
\lambda\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}
we denote by
U(\lambda)
the subset of
\mathfrak{h}^{*}
consisting of weights

\nu
such that
there exists a chain

\nu+\rho=\mu_{r}<\mu_{r-1}<\ldots<\mu_{0}=\lambda+\rho
,
where
\mu_{i+1}=\mu_{i}-m_{i}\gamma_{i}
for some

\gamma_{i}\in\overline{\Delta}^{\operatorname{re}}\cap\Delta^{+}
and
m_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}

such that
2(\mu_{i},\gamma_{i})=m_{i}(\gamma_{i},\gamma_{i})
,
where
m_{i}=1
is odd if
p(\gamma)=\overline{1}
,
m_{i}=1
if

\gamma
is isotropic. Note that

\mu_{i+1}=s_{\gamma_{i}}\mu_{i}
if
\gamma_{i}
is anisotropic.

4.6.1.

The following statement follows from
[
27
]
for Lie algebras
and
[
12
]
for Lie superalgebras.

4.6.2.

Proposition.

Consider the expansion

De^{\rho}\operatorname{ch}L(\lambda)=\sum\limits_{\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\Sigma}a_{\mu}e^{\lambda+\rho-\mu}
(with
a_{\mu}\in\mathbb{Z}
).
Assume that
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\Sigma
is such that

[M(\lambda):L(\lambda-\mu)]\not=0
or
a_{\mu}\not=0
. Then

(i)

If
\lambda
is non-critical, then
(\lambda-\mu)\in U(\lambda)
.

(ii)

If
\mathfrak{g}
is of type (Aff) and
\lambda
is critical, then

(\lambda-\mu)\in(U(\lambda)-\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\delta)
(where
\delta
is the minimal imaginary root).

4.6.3.

Lemma.

If
\nu\in U(\lambda)
, then

\Delta_{\nu+\rho}=\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}
and
(\lambda-\nu)
lies in
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}
.

Proof.

It is enough to verify the assertion for a chain of length one:

\nu+\rho=\mu_{1}<\mu_{0}=\lambda+\rho

(the general case follows by induction on the length of the chain).
For such a chain we have

\nu+\rho=\lambda+\rho-m\gamma
where

\gamma\in\overline{\Delta}^{\operatorname{re}}\cap\Delta^{+}
and
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}

is such that
2(\lambda+\rho,\gamma)=m(\gamma,\gamma)
,
and
m
is odd if
p(\gamma)=\overline{1}
,
m=1
if
\gamma
is isotropic.
Notice that
\gamma\in\overline{R}_{\lambda+\rho}
, so
(\lambda-\nu)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}
. Let us verify that
\Delta_{\nu+\rho}=\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}
.

If
\gamma
is anisotropic, then
\nu+\rho=s_{\gamma}(\lambda+\rho)
, so

\Delta_{\nu+\rho}=s_{\gamma}\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}=\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}

(since
\gamma\in\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}
).

Consider the case when
\gamma
is isotropic. Then

(\lambda+\rho,\gamma)=0
and

\nu=\lambda-\gamma
, so the formula
\Delta_{\nu+\rho}=\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}

follows from Lemma
4.1.7
.
∎

4.6.4.

Arguing as in
[
4
]
, Section 4 we obtain the following decomposition theorem.

Consider the equivalence relation
\lambda\approx\nu
on
\mathfrak{h}^{*}
which is generated
by
\nu\approx\lambda
if
\nu\in U(\lambda)
and

(\lambda-\delta)\approx\lambda
if
\mathfrak{g}
is of type (Aff) and
\lambda
is critical.

For any
N\in\mathcal{O}_{\Sigma}(\mathfrak{g})

there exists a unique set of submodules
\{M_{\chi}\}_{\chi\in A}
such that

\bullet

M=\oplus_{\chi\in A}M_{\chi}
;

\bullet

for each
\chi_{1},\chi_{2}\in A
and
\lambda,\nu
such that

[M_{\chi_{1}}:L(\lambda)]
,
[M_{\chi_{2}}:L(\nu)]\not=0

one has
\lambda\approx\nu
if and only if
\chi_{1}=\chi_{2}
.

If
N\in\mathcal{O}_{\Sigma}(\mathfrak{g})
is indecomposable, then
N=M_{\chi}
.

4.6.5.

Corollary.

If
N\in\mathcal{O}_{\Sigma}(\mathfrak{g})
is non-critical and indecomposable, then

[N:L(\lambda)],[N:L(\nu)]\not=0\ \ \Longrightarrow\ \ \Delta_{\lambda+\rho}=\Delta_{\nu+\rho},\ \ (\lambda-\nu)\in\mathbb{Z}\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}.

Proof.

By
\lx@sectionsign

4.6.4
we have
\lambda\approx\nu
.
By Lemma
4.6.3
this forces

\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}=\Delta_{\nu+\rho}
and

(\lambda-\nu)\in\mathbb{Z}\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}
.
∎

4.6.6.

Let
N\in\mathcal{O}_{\Sigma}(\mathfrak{g})
be a non-critical indecomposable module.
Using Corollary
4.6.5
we define
\Delta_{N}
to be
\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}

where
\lambda
is such that

[N:L(\lambda)]\not=0
.

Take
\nu\in U(\lambda)
and retain notation of
\lx@sectionsign

4.5.1
.
By Proposition
4.6.2
we can identify
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{\nu+\rho}
with
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}:=\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{\lambda+\rho}
;
moreover, we can choose
\nu^{\prime}
in such a way that
\nu^{\prime}\in U(\lambda^{\prime})

and
\psi(\lambda^{\prime}-\nu^{\prime})=\lambda-\nu
.

4.7.
Remark

The character formulas for arbitrary irreducible non-critical modules

L(\lambda)
over symmetrizable Kac-Moody algebras were established in
[
40
]
;
these formulas imply the following remarkable formula:

De^{\rho}\operatorname{ch}L=D^{\prime}e^{\rho^{\prime}}\operatorname{ch}L^{\prime}
, where
D,\rho
(resp.,
D^{\prime},\rho^{\prime}
) are the Weyl denominator and
the Weyl vector for
\Delta^{+}
(resp., for
(\Delta^{\prime})^{+}
).

More precisely, we denote by
\mathcal{R}_{0}(\Sigma)

the subalgebra of
\mathcal{R}(\Sigma)
consisting of

x\in\mathcal{R}(\Sigma)
satisfying
\operatorname{supp}(x)\subset-\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\Sigma
.
The linear monomorphism

\psi_{\lambda+\rho}:\mathbb{Z}\Sigma^{\prime}\to\mathbb{Z}\Sigma

induces a ring monomorphism
\mathcal{R}_{0}(\Sigma^{\prime})\to\mathcal{R}_{0}(\Sigma)

which we also denote by
\psi_{\lambda+\rho}
. The algebra

\mathcal{R}_{0}(\Sigma^{\prime})
contains

D^{\prime}
and
e^{-\lambda^{\prime}}\operatorname{ch}L^{\prime}
.
The formula
De^{\rho}\operatorname{ch}L=D^{\prime}e^{\rho^{\prime}}\operatorname{ch}L^{\prime}
means

(4.11)

De^{-\lambda}\operatorname{ch}L(\lambda)=\psi_{\lambda+\rho}\bigl(D^{\prime}e^{-\lambda^{\prime}}\operatorname{ch}L^{\prime}(\lambda^{\prime})\bigr).

For example, if a non-critical weight
\lambda

is such that
m:=\langle\lambda+\rho,\alpha\rangle\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}

for some
\alpha\in\Delta^{\operatorname{re}+}

and
\langle\lambda+\rho,\beta\rangle\not\in\mathbb{Z}

for all
\beta\in\Delta^{\operatorname{re}+}\setminus\{\alpha\}
,
then
\mathfrak{g}_{\lambda+\rho}\cong\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
,
\Sigma^{\prime}=\{\alpha^{\prime}\}

and
\psi_{\lambda+\rho}(\alpha^{\prime})=\alpha
.
In this case

De^{-\lambda}\operatorname{ch}L(\lambda)=\psi_{\lambda+\rho}\bigl(D^{\prime}e^{-\lambda^{\prime}}\operatorname{ch}L^{\prime}(\lambda^{\prime})\bigr)=\psi_{\lambda+\rho}(1-e^{-m\alpha^{\prime}})=1-e^{m\alpha}.

We conjecture that formula (
4.11
) holds for all symmetrizable Kac-Moody superalgebras, see
[
14
]
. In
[
7
]
, P. Fiebig established equivalence
of the corresponding blocks
in the categories
\mathcal{O}_{\Sigma}(\mathfrak{g})
and
\mathcal{O}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})

for an arbitrary symmetrizable Kac-Moody algebra
\mathfrak{g}
.

5.
Quasi-admissible modules

In this section
\mathfrak{g}
is an indecomposable symmetrizable
Kac-Moody superalgebra with a base
\Sigma
and
\mathfrak{g}\not=\mathfrak{gl}(1|1)
.
Recall that
(-,-)
is normalised in such a way that
(\alpha,\alpha)\in\mathbb{Q}_{>0}
for some
\alpha\in\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}
.
The action of
w\in W
on

\sum\limits_{\nu}m_{\nu}e^{\nu}

is always assumed to be the natural, i.e.

w(\sum\limits_{\nu}m_{\nu}e^{\nu}):=\sum\limits_{\nu}m_{\nu}e^{w\nu}
.

5.1.
Integrable and partially integrable modules

Let
\Sigma_{\operatorname{pr}}
be the set of principal roots (see
\lx@sectionsign

2.8.3
for definition).
In the case when
\mathfrak{g}=\dot{\mathfrak{g}}^{(1)}
is the non-twisted affinization of
\dot{\mathfrak{g}}
we let
\dot{\Delta}\subset\Delta
be the root subsystem of
\dot{\mathfrak{g}}
.
By Remark
2.8.2

\dot{\Sigma}_{\operatorname{pr}}:=\dot{\Delta}\cap{\Sigma}_{\operatorname{pr}}

is the set of principal roots of
\dot{\Delta}
.

5.1.1.

Definition.

For a subset
\pi\subset\Sigma_{\operatorname{pr}}
we call a
[\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}]
-module
N

\pi
-integrable
if
\mathfrak{g}_{\pm\alpha}
acts locally nilpotently
on
N
for each
\alpha\in\pi
.

5.1.2.

Lemma.

Let
W[\pi]
be the subgroup of
W
generated by the reflections
s_{\alpha}
with
\alpha\in\pi
.
If a
[\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}]
-module
N
is
\pi
-integrable, then
the root space
\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}

acts locally nilpotently on
N

for each
\alpha\in W[\pi](\pi)
.

Proof.

Take
\alpha\in\pi
.
We will prove that
\mathfrak{g}_{\pm w\alpha}
acts locally nilpotently on
N

by induction on the length of
w\in W[\pi]
.
If the length is zero, then, by definition,
\mathfrak{g}_{\pm\alpha}

acts locally nilpotently on
N
.
Otherwise for some
\beta\in\pi
we have
w=s_{\beta}w^{\prime}
, where
the length of
w^{\prime}
is less than the length of
w
. By induction,

\mathfrak{g}_{\pm s_{\beta}\alpha}
act locally nilpotently on
N
.
By
[
26
]
, Lemma 3.8, any
u\in\mathfrak{g}_{\pm w\alpha}
can be written as

u=(\exp\operatorname{ad}f)(\exp\operatorname{ad}(-e))(\exp\operatorname{ad}f)(u^{\prime})
for some

u^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{g}_{\pm w^{\prime}\alpha}
,
e\in\mathfrak{g}_{\beta}
and
f\in\mathfrak{g}_{-\beta}
. Let
E,F,U,U^{\prime}
be the images
of
e,f,u,u^{\prime}
in
\operatorname{End}(N)
. Then

\begin{array}[]{rl}U&=(\exp\operatorname{ad}F)(\exp\operatorname{ad}(-E))(\exp\operatorname{ad}F)(U^{\prime})\\
&=(\exp F)(\exp-E)(\exp F)U^{\prime}(\exp-F)(\exp E)(\exp-F),\end{array}

since
(\exp a)b(\exp-a)=\exp(\operatorname{ad}a)(b)
. For any
v\in N
we have

U^{s}(v)=(\exp F)(\exp-E)(\exp F)(U^{\prime})^{s}(\exp-F)(\exp E)(\exp-F)(v).

Since
E,F,U^{\prime}
acts locally nilpotenly,
U^{s}v=0
for
s>>0
, so
U
acts locally nilpotently.
∎

5.1.3.
Integrable modules

Since
\Delta^{\operatorname{an}}_{\overline{0}}=W(\Sigma_{\operatorname{pr}})
and
W=W[\Sigma_{\operatorname{pr}}]
, the
\Sigma_{\operatorname{pr}}
-integrable modules
are modules where
\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
acts locally nilpotently
for each
\alpha\in\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}
. Such modules are called
integrable
.

5.1.4.
Remark

If
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{g}(A,\tau)
is a symmetrizable infinite-dimensional Kac-Moody superalgebra, then the following conditions are equivalent
[
30
]
:

\bullet

the set
\Sigma_{\operatorname{pr}}
is linearly independent;

\bullet

the Dynkin diagram of
\Sigma_{\operatorname{pr}}
is connected;

\bullet

the subalgebra generated by vector space
\mathfrak{h}+\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\Sigma_{\operatorname{pr}}}\mathfrak{g}_{\pm\alpha}
is
a Kac-Moody algebra in the sense of
\lx@sectionsign

2.3.3
;

\bullet

there exists an irreducible integrable module in

\mathcal{O}_{\Sigma}(\mathfrak{g})
which is not one-dimensional;

\bullet

\mathfrak{g}
is anisotropic,
\mathfrak{sl}(1|n)^{(1)}
,
or
\mathfrak{osp}(2|2n)^{(1)}
.

5.1.5.

Remark.

Consider the case when
\mathfrak{g}
is affine
and the Dynkin diagram of
\Sigma_{\operatorname{pr}}
is not connected.
By above,
\mathcal{O}_{\Sigma}(\mathfrak{g})
does not contain irreducible integrable modules
which are not one-dimensional.
If we take
\pi
to be a maximal proper subset of
\Sigma_{\operatorname{pr}}

(i.e.,
\Sigma_{\operatorname{pr}}\setminus\pi
is of cardinality one), then

\mathcal{O}_{\Sigma}(\mathfrak{g})
contains interesting
\pi
-integrable modules.
For example, if
\mathfrak{g}=\dot{\mathfrak{g}}^{(1)}

and
\pi=\dot{\Sigma}_{\operatorname{pr}}\cup\pi^{\#}
, where

\pi^{\#}:=\{\alpha\in\Sigma_{\operatorname{pr}}|\ (\alpha,\alpha)>0\}
,
then, for
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, the irreducible
\pi
-integrable modules
in
\mathcal{O}_{\Sigma}(\mathfrak{g})^{k}
are irreducible modules in the

KL_{k}
-category studied in
[
2
]
.
Important examples of
\pi
-integrable modules are irreducible vacuum modules

L(k\Lambda_{0})
for suitable values of
k
.
The irreducible
\pi
-integrable modules in
\mathcal{O}_{\Sigma}(\mathfrak{g})

were classified in
[
31
]
.

5.1.6.

The simple affine vertex algebra
V_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
corresponding to
an anisotropic non-twisted affine superalgebra

\mathfrak{g}
and an integrable
\mathfrak{g}
-module

L(k\Lambda_{0})
,
see
[
38
]
(and
[
6
]
,
[
5
]
).
This result is based on the fact that in this case
the integrability of
L(k\Lambda_{0})

implies that
V_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
-modules are exactly

V^{k}(\mathfrak{g})
-modules which are integrable.

The proof of rationality of
V_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
,
given in
[
38
]
, Section 2.2, uses only the fact that the even part of the finite-dimensional Lie superalgebra
\dot{\mathfrak{g}}
to which
\mathfrak{g}
is associated, is simple.
It follows that the adjoint orbit of any non-zero element
a
in
\dot{\mathfrak{g}}_{\overline{0}}
spans it, hence
U(\dot{\mathfrak{g}})/(a^{k+1})

and
S(\dot{\mathfrak{g}})/(a^{k+1})
are finite-dimensional
(see
[
38
]
, Lemma 2.3).

5.1.7.

If
\mathfrak{g}
is a non-twisted affine superalgebra which is not
anisotropic, then
V_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
is not rational
for
k\not=0
, since the category of
V_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
-modules is not
completely reducible. However, for
\mathfrak{g}\not=D(2|1,a)^{(1)}
,
the
\pi^{\#}
-integrability of
L(k\Lambda_{0})

implies that
V_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
-modules are exactly
V^{k}(\mathfrak{g})
-modules
which are
\pi^{\#}
-integrable,
see
[
16
]
Theorem 5.3.1.

5.1.8.

Let
\pi
be a linearly independent subset of
\Sigma_{\operatorname{pr}}
. This means
that
\pi
is a proper subset of
\Sigma_{\operatorname{pr}}
if
\mathfrak{g}
is affine,
not anisotropic and not equal to
\mathfrak{sl}(1|n)^{(1)}
,
\mathfrak{osp}(2|2n)^{(1)}
,

D(2|1,a)^{(1)}
,
\pi
lies in
A_{1}^{(1)}\coprod A_{1}\coprod A_{1}

for
D(2|1,a)^{(1)}
, and
\pi
is an arbitrary subset of
\Sigma_{\operatorname{pr}}

in the rest of the cases.
We denote by
\mathfrak{g}_{\pi}
the subalgebra generated by the vector space
\mathfrak{h}+\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\pi}\mathfrak{g}_{\pm\alpha}
.
Since
\Sigma_{\operatorname{pr}}\subset\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}_{\overline{0}}
,
\mathfrak{g}_{\pi}

is a subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}_{\overline{0}}
.
Using the arguments
of
[
48
]
, Theorem 9.1 (see also
[
15
]
, 3.5)
it is easy to show that
\mathfrak{g}_{\pi}
is
a symmetrizable Kac-Moody algebra
(in the sense of
\lx@sectionsign

2.3.3
)
with the Cartan subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}
,
the set of simple roots
\pi
, and

\pi^{\vee}:=\{\alpha^{\vee}|\ \alpha\in\pi\}
.
This Kac-Moody algebra is indecomposable if
the Dynkin diagram of
\pi
is connected.
The restriction of
(-,-)
to
\mathfrak{g}_{\pi}

is a non-degenerate invariant bilinear form.
We set

\Delta_{\pi}:=\Delta(\mathfrak{g}_{\pi})^{\operatorname{re}}.

5.1.9.
Example

Take
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{osp}(3|2)^{(1)}
.
Then
\Sigma_{\operatorname{pr}}=\{\varepsilon_{1},\delta-\varepsilon_{1},2\delta_{1},\delta-2\delta_{1}\}
. If
\pi
is of cardinality three, then

\mathfrak{g}_{\pi}\cong\mathfrak{sl}_{2}^{(1)}\times\mathfrak{gl}_{2}
.

5.1.10.

If
\mathfrak{g}
is of type (Aff), then each indecomposable
component of
\mathfrak{g}_{\pi}
is of type (Fin) or (Aff).
If
\pi^{\#}=\{\alpha\in\Sigma_{\operatorname{pr}}|\ (\alpha,\alpha)>0\}

has a connected Dynkin diagram, then
\mathfrak{g}_{\pi^{\#}}

is an affine Kac-Moody algebra. For
\pi=\dot{\Sigma}_{\operatorname{pr}}\cup\pi^{\#}
,
which appeared in Remark
5.1.5
,
the algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{\pi}
is the direct product of
\mathfrak{g}_{\pi^{\#}}

and a reductive Lie algebra with the set
of simple roots
\{\alpha\in\dot{\Sigma}_{\operatorname{pr}}|\ (\alpha,\alpha)<0\}
.

5.1.11.
Remark

For any
N\in{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{g})
one has
\operatorname{Res}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathfrak{g}_{\pi}}N\in{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{g}_{\pi})
.
A
\mathfrak{g}
-module
N
is
\pi
-integrable if and only if

\operatorname{Res}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathfrak{g}_{\pi}}N
is
\mathfrak{g}_{\pi}
-integrable.

Note that for any
\pi
-integrable

L(\lambda)
we have

\Delta_{\pi}\subset{R}_{\lambda+\rho}
and

\langle\lambda,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
for each
\alpha\in\pi
.

We will use the following result (which can be deduced from
[
17
]
, but we include a proof for the sake of completeness).

5.1.12.

Lemma.

For any
N\in\mathcal{O}_{\Sigma}(\mathfrak{g})
and
\gamma\in\pi

the following conditions are equivalent:

(a)

\mathfrak{g}_{-\gamma}
acts locally nilpotently on
N
;

(b)

s_{\gamma}((e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}}-e^{-\frac{\gamma}{2}})\operatorname{ch}N)=-(e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}}-e^{-\frac{\gamma}{2}})\operatorname{ch}N
.

(c)

D_{\pi}e^{\rho_{\pi}}\operatorname{ch}N
is naturally
s_{\gamma}
-anti-invariant
(where
D_{\pi}
and
\rho_{\pi}
are respectively the Weyl denominator and the Weyl vector for

\Delta_{\pi}^{+}
).

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{t}
be the subalgebra which is generated by
\mathfrak{g}_{\pm\gamma}

and
\mathfrak{h}
. Then
\mathfrak{t}
is the direct product of a copy of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}

and a subalgebra of codimension
1
in
\mathfrak{h}
.
Set
M:=\operatorname{Res}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathfrak{t}}N
. By
\lx@sectionsign

2.10.3
(iv) we have

M\in\mathcal{O}_{\Sigma}(\mathfrak{t})
and

\operatorname{ch}N=\operatorname{ch}M=\sum\limits_{\mu\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}}[M:L_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mu)]\operatorname{ch}L_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mu).

Setting
P^{+}(\mathfrak{t}):=\{\mu|\ \dim L_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mu)<\infty\}
we have

(e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}}-e^{-\frac{\gamma}{2}})\operatorname{ch}L_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mu)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(e^{\mu+\frac{\gamma}{2}}-s_{\gamma}e^{\mu+\frac{\gamma}{2}})&\text{ if }\mu\in P^{+}(\mathfrak{t})\\
e^{\mu+\frac{\gamma}{2}}&\text{ if }\mu\not\in P^{+}(\mathfrak{t})\end{array}\right.

which gives

(e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}}-e^{-\frac{\gamma}{2}})\operatorname{ch}N=\sum\limits_{\mu\in P^{+}(\mathfrak{t})}[M:L_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mu)](e^{\mu+\frac{\gamma}{2}}-s_{\gamma}e^{\mu+\frac{\gamma}{2}})+\sum\limits_{\mu\not\in P^{+}(\mathfrak{t})}[M:L_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mu)]e^{\mu+\frac{\gamma}{2}}.

Therefore (b) is equivalent to

\sum\limits_{\mu\not\in P^{+}}[M:L_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mu)]s_{\gamma}e^{\mu+\frac{\gamma}{2}}=-\sum\limits_{\mu\not\in P^{+}}[M:L_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mu)]e^{\mu+\frac{\gamma}{2}}.

Observe that all coefficients in the left-hand side are non-negative and in
the right-hand side are non-positive. Thus (b) is equivalent to

[M:L_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mu)]=0
for all
\mu\not\in P^{+}
which means that
M
is a direct sum of
finite-dimensional
\mathfrak{t}
-modules. This establishes the equivalence (a)
\Longleftrightarrow
(b).

For the equivalence (b)
\Longleftrightarrow
(c) note that

\rho_{\pi}-\frac{\gamma}{2}
and
\Delta(\mathfrak{g}_{\pi})^{+}\setminus\{\gamma\}
are

s_{\gamma}
-invariant, so

(5.1)

\begin{array}[]{l}D_{\pi}e^{\rho_{\pi}}(e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}}-e^{-\frac{\gamma}{2}})^{-1}=e^{\rho_{\pi}-\frac{\gamma}{2}}\prod\limits_{\alpha\in\Delta(\mathfrak{g}_{\pi})^{+}\setminus\{\gamma\}}(1-e^{-\alpha})\ \text{ is $s_{\gamma}$-invariant}\\
D^{-1}_{\pi}e^{-\rho_{\pi}}(e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}}-e^{-\frac{\gamma}{2}})=e^{-\rho_{\pi}+\frac{\gamma}{2}}\prod\limits_{\alpha\in\Delta(\mathfrak{g}_{\pi})^{+}\setminus\{\gamma\}}(1-e^{-\alpha})^{-1}\ \text{ is $s_{\gamma}$-invariant}\end{array}

(see
[
14
]
, Section 2
on invariance of infinite products). This implies the equivalence (b)
\Longleftrightarrow
(c)
and completes the proof.
∎

5.2.
Quasi-admissible modules in type (An)

Below we recall some definitions and results of
[
17
]
.
Let
\mathfrak{g}
be of type (An). In this case

\Sigma_{\operatorname{pr}}=\{\alpha\in\Sigma|\ p(\alpha)=\overline{0}\}\cup\{2\alpha|\ \alpha\in\Sigma,\ p(\alpha)=\overline{1}\}

and
\Sigma_{\operatorname{pr}}
is connected.
Recall that for
L=L(\lambda)
we have

\Delta_{L}=\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}=R_{\lambda+\rho},

(see Definition
4.2
).
We take
\pi:=\Sigma_{\operatorname{pr}}
. The Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{\pi}
is a ”maximal Kac-Moody
subalgebra” of
\mathfrak{g}_{\overline{0}}
. For example, for
\mathfrak{g}
of type
C_{2}^{(2)}
,

\mathfrak{g}_{\overline{0}}
is not a Kac-Moody algebra: in this case
\Sigma=\{\alpha,\delta-\alpha\}

where
p(\alpha)=p(\delta-\alpha)=\overline{1}
and
\delta
is the minimal
imaginary roots;
\mathfrak{g}_{\pi}
is of type
A_{1}^{(1)}
with
the simple roots
\{2\alpha,2\delta-2\alpha\}
,
so
\mathfrak{g}_{\delta}\cap\mathfrak{g}_{\pi}=0
.

5.2.1.

By
[
17
]
, Theorem 4.5.1, for
N\in{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{g})
such that
\Delta_{N}
is well defined
(for instance, if
N
is indecomposable),
the following conditions
are equivalent:

(i)

the expression
De^{\rho}\operatorname{ch}N
is (naturally)

W[\Delta_{N}]
-anti-invariant, i.e.

s_{\alpha}(De^{\rho}\operatorname{ch}N)=-De^{\rho}\operatorname{ch}N\ \ \text{ for all }\alpha\in\Delta_{N}

(ii)

the expression
D_{\pi}e^{\rho_{\pi}}\operatorname{ch}N
is (naturally)

W[\Delta_{N}]
-anti-invariant.

5.2.2.

Definition.
If
N\in{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{g})
is such that
\Delta_{N}
is well defined,
we say that
N
is

quasi-admissible
4
4

4

In
[
17
]
quasi-admissible modules are called “snowflake”.
if
\operatorname{ch}N
satisfies the equivalent conditions (i) and (ii).

5.2.3.
Remark

The equivalence in
\lx@sectionsign

5.2.1
implies that

(5.2)

N\text{ is quasi-admissible }\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \ \operatorname{Res}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathfrak{g}_{\pi}}N\text{ is quasi-admissible}.

5.2.4.
Theorem

(
[
28
]
;
[
17
]
, Section 4).
Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a symmetrizable anisotropic affine Kac-Moody superalgebra.

(i)

A
\mathfrak{g}
-module
N
is quasi-admissible if and only if every irreducible subquotient of
N

is quasi-admissible.

(ii)

The following conditions are equivalent

\bullet

L:=L(\lambda)
is quasi-admissible;

\bullet

L^{\prime}

is an integrable
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
-module (see
\lx@sectionsign

4.3
for notation).

(iii)

If
L(\lambda)
is non-critical quasi-admissible, then

De^{\rho}\operatorname{ch}L(\lambda)=\!\!\sum\limits_{w\in W_{L}}\!(\operatorname{sgn}w)\ e^{w(\lambda+\rho)}
.

(iv)

If
L(\nu)
is a non-critical quasi-admissible subquotient of
M(\lambda)
, then
\lambda=\nu
.

(v)

All non-critical quasi-admissible modules are
completely reducible.

5.3.
Remark

Take
L:=L(\lambda)
and
L^{\prime}=L_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}(\lambda^{\prime})
as in
\lx@sectionsign

4.3
.
By Lemma
4.2.5
(ii), the set
\Sigma_{L}:=\psi(\Sigma^{\prime})

is the set of indecomposable elements in
\Delta_{L}^{+}
.
Since
\langle\lambda+\rho,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle=\langle\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime},\psi^{-1}(\alpha)^{\vee}\rangle
,
the following conditions are equivalent:

(a)

L_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}(\lambda^{\prime})
is integrable;

(b)

\langle\lambda+\rho,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
for all
\alpha\in\Sigma_{L}
.

5.4.
Quasi-admissible modules in general case

We retain notation of
\lx@sectionsign

5.1.8
.

5.4.1.
Notation

Recall that
\pi
is a linearly independent subset of

\Sigma_{\operatorname{pr}}
,
\mathfrak{g}_{\pi}
is the corresponding Kac-Moody subalgebra of

\mathfrak{g}_{\overline{0}}
and
\Delta_{\pi}=\Delta(\mathfrak{g}_{\pi})^{\operatorname{re}}
.
We denote by
W[\pi]
the subgroup of
W
generated by

\{s_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\pi}
. This
is the Weyl group of
\mathfrak{g}_{\pi}
and

\Delta_{\pi}=W[\pi](\pi)
. As before
D_{\pi}
,
\rho_{\pi}
denote the corresponding Weyl denominator
and Weyl vector respectively.

Recall that for any subset
X
of
\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}

the notation
W[X]
stands for the subgroup of
W
generated
by
s_{\alpha}
with
\alpha\in X
.

If
L
is fixed, we
retain notation of
\lx@sectionsign

4.3
and
denote by
\Sigma^{\prime}_{\operatorname{pr}}
the set of principal roots for
\Delta^{\prime}
; we set

\pi^{\prime}:=\{\alpha^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{\prime}_{\operatorname{pr}}|\ \psi(\alpha)\in\Delta_{\pi}\}.

We suggest the following definition.

5.4.2.
Definition

If
N\in{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{g})
is such that
\Delta_{N}

is well defined we say that
N
is

\pi
-quasi-admissible
if
D_{\pi}e^{\rho_{\pi}}\operatorname{ch}N
is naturally

W[\Delta_{L}\cap\Delta_{\pi}]
-anti-invariant, i.e.

s_{\alpha}(D_{\pi}e^{\rho_{\pi}}\operatorname{ch}N)=-D_{\pi}e^{\rho_{\pi}}\operatorname{ch}N\ \ \text{ for all }\alpha\in\Delta_{L}\cap\Delta_{\pi}.

5.4.3.
Remark

Note that
\Delta_{\overline{0}}^{+}
does not depend on
the choice of a base in
\operatorname{Sp}
, so the expression

D_{\pi}e^{\rho_{\pi}}\operatorname{ch}N
does not depend on this choice
as well.

5.4.4.

In the anisotropic case Definition
5.4.2
is equivalent to Definition
5.2.2
.

In Theorem
5.5.1
below we partially extend Theorem
5.2.4
(i) to general
\mathfrak{g}
;
in Theorem
5.5.3
below we extend Theorem
5.2.4
(ii).
The results which are similar of Theorem
5.2.4
(iii)–(v) and the
equivalence (a)
\Longleftrightarrow
(b) in
\lx@sectionsign

5.3

do not hold for atypical quasi-admissible modules, since these assertions do not hold for
atypical
\pi
-integrable modules which are particular cases of
\pi
-quasi-admissible
modules.

5.4.5.

Example.

By Lemma
5.1.12
, if
N\in\mathcal{O}_{\Sigma}(\mathfrak{g})
is such that
\Delta_{N}
is well defined and

\pi\subset\Delta_{N}
, then
the
\pi
-quasi-admissibility of
N
is equivalent to
the
\pi
-integrability.

5.5.
Main results

In this section we will prove the following theorems.

5.5.1.

Theorem.

Let
N\in\mathcal{O}^{\operatorname{fin}}(\mathfrak{g})
be such that
\Delta_{N}
is well-defined.
Then the following conditions are equivalent:

(a)

N
is
\pi
-quasi-admissible;

(b)

all irreducible
subquotients of
N
are
\pi
-quasi-admissible;

(c)

all irreducible
subquotients of

M:=\operatorname{Res}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathfrak{g}_{\pi}}N
are quasi-admissible

\mathfrak{g}_{\pi}
-modules,
\Delta_{M}
is well defined and
\Delta_{\pi}\cap\Delta_{N}=\Delta_{M}
.

The proof will be given in
\lx@sectionsign

5.8
.

5.5.2.

Corollary.

If
N\in\mathcal{O}^{\operatorname{fin}}(\mathfrak{g})
is
\pi
-quasi-admissible and

M:=\operatorname{Res}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathfrak{g}_{\pi}}N
is non-critical, then

M
is a completely reducible
\mathfrak{g}_{\pi}
-module.

The proof will be given in
\lx@sectionsign

5.9
.

5.5.3.

Theorem.

If
L:=L(\lambda)
is non-critical, then the following conditions are equivalent:

(a)

L
is
\pi
-quasi-admissible;

(b)

L^{\prime}
is
\pi^{\prime}
-integrable, where
\pi^{\prime}=\{\alpha^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{\prime}_{\operatorname{pr}}|\ \psi(\alpha^{\prime})\in\Delta_{\pi}\}
.

The proof will be given in
\lx@sectionsign

5.10
.

5.5.4.
Remark

By (
4.2
) we have

\{\alpha\in\Delta_{\pi}|\ \langle\lambda,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle\in\mathbb{Z}\}=R_{\lambda+\rho}\cap\Delta_{\pi}
.
Combining Theorem
5.5.1
and
\lx@sectionsign

5.6
we obtain

(5.3)

L(\lambda)\ \text{ is $\pi$-quasi-admissible }\ \Longrightarrow\ \ \ \Delta_{\lambda+\rho}\cap\Delta_{\pi}=R_{\lambda+\rho}\cap\Delta_{\pi}.

5.6.
The set
\Delta_{M}
for
M:=\operatorname{Res}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathfrak{g}_{\pi}}N

Recall that
\Delta_{\pi}\subset\mathfrak{h}^{*}
is the set of real roots of the Kac-Moody algebra

\mathfrak{g}_{\pi}
.

The set
\Delta_{M}
is well defined if and only if
the set

\{\alpha\in\Delta_{\pi}|\ \langle\nu,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle\in\mathbb{Z}\}

is the same for
all
\nu
such that
[M:L_{\mathfrak{g}_{\pi}}(\nu)]\not=0
; moreover

\Delta_{M}
coincides with these sets. (Notice that

\{\alpha\in\Delta_{\pi}|\ \langle\nu,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle\in\mathbb{Z}\}

is the integral root subsystem
of
\Delta_{\pi}
corresponding to the weight

\nu
.)
Take
\nu
such that
[M:L_{\mathfrak{g}_{\pi}}(\nu)]\not=0
. Then
there exists
\lambda\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}
such that
L(\lambda)
is a subquotient of
N
and

L(\lambda)_{\nu}\not=0
, so

\lambda-\nu\in\mathbb{Z}\pi
. Therefore

\{\beta\in\Delta_{\pi}|\ \langle\nu,\beta^{\vee}\rangle\in\mathbb{Z}\}=\{\beta\in\Delta_{\pi}|\ \langle\lambda,\beta^{\vee}\rangle\in\mathbb{Z}\}.

We conclude that

(i)

\Delta_{M}
is well defined if and only if
the set
\{\beta\in\Delta_{\pi}|\ \langle\lambda,\beta^{\vee}\rangle\in\mathbb{Z}\}

is the same for all
\lambda

such that
[N:L(\lambda)]\not=0
;

(ii)

if
\Delta_{M}
is well defined, then

\Delta_{M}=\{\beta\in\Delta_{\pi}|\ \langle\lambda,\beta^{\vee}\rangle\in\mathbb{Z}\}

for any
\lambda

such that
[N:L(\lambda)]\not=0
and
\Delta_{M}\subset(\Delta_{N}\cap\Delta_{\pi})
;

(iii)

\{\alpha\in\Delta_{\pi}|\ \langle\nu,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle\in\mathbb{Z}\}\subset(\Delta_{N}\cap\Delta_{\pi})
for any
\nu
such that
N_{\nu}\not=0
.

5.6.1.

The important example for the next sections is
L=L(\lambda)
,

M:=\operatorname{Res}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathfrak{g}_{\pi}}L
.
Then

\Delta_{L}\cap\Delta_{\pi}=\Delta_{\pi}\cap\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}.

Combining
(i) and (
4.2
) we get

\Delta_{M}=\{\beta\in\Delta_{\pi}|\ \langle\lambda,\beta^{\vee}\rangle\in\mathbb{Z}\}=R_{\lambda+\rho}\cap\Delta_{\pi}.

The condition
\Delta_{L}=\Delta_{M}\cap\Delta_{\pi}
holds in many cases, for example if

\bullet

\Delta_{\pi}
consists of short roots: in this case Lemma
4.1.4
(i) gives

\Delta_{\pi}\cap R_{\lambda+\rho}=\Delta_{\pi}\cap\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}
.

\bullet

if
L(\lambda)
is typical (in this case

\Delta_{L}=R_{\lambda+\rho}
).

Introduce
R_{\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime}}\subset\Delta^{\prime}
as in
\lx@sectionsign

4.1.1
.
By Lemma
4.3.2
,

\psi
maps
R_{\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime}}
to
R_{\lambda+\rho}
, and
\Delta_{L^{\prime}}=(\Delta^{\prime})^{\operatorname{re}}
.
Therefore
\Delta_{L^{\prime}}\cap\Delta_{\pi^{\prime}}=\Delta_{\pi^{\prime}}
which
gives the following useful equivalence

(5.4)

\Delta_{\pi}\cap\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}=\Delta_{\pi}\cap R_{\lambda+\rho}\ \ \Longleftrightarrow\ \ \pi^{\prime}\subset R_{\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime}}.

5.6.2.
Example

The following example shows that
\Delta_{\pi}\cap R_{\lambda+\rho}
can be a proper subset of

\Delta_{\pi}\cap\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}
(i.e.
\Delta_{M}

is a proper subset of
\Delta_{\pi}\cap\Delta_{L}
).

Take
\mathfrak{g}
of type
B(1|3)
with
\Sigma=\{\delta_{1}-\delta_{2},\delta_{2}-\delta_{3},\delta_{3}-\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{1}\}
. One has
p(\varepsilon_{1})=\overline{0}
and
p(\delta_{i})=\overline{1}
.
We have
\Sigma_{\operatorname{pr}}=\{\delta_{1}-\delta_{2},\delta_{2}-\delta_{3},\delta_{3},\varepsilon_{1}\}
and we take
\pi:=\{\delta_{1}-\delta_{2},\delta_{2}-\delta_{3},\delta_{3}\}
.

For

\lambda+\rho:=3\delta_{1}+2\delta_{2}+\delta_{3}-\varepsilon_{1}
we have

{R}_{\lambda+\rho}=\{\pm\varepsilon_{1};\pm\delta_{i}\pm\delta_{j};\pm(\delta_{3}-\varepsilon_{1})\}
.
Therefore

\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}=\Delta
and
\Delta_{\pi}\cap\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}=\Delta_{\pi}
is
the root system of type
B_{3}
, and

R_{\lambda+\rho}\cap\Delta_{\pi}=\{\pm\delta_{i}\pm\delta_{j}\}
is the root system of type

D_{3}
.

5.6.3.

Lemma.

(i)

The set
of indecomposable elements in
\Delta_{L}\cap\Delta^{+}_{\overline{0}}

coincides with
\psi(\Sigma^{\prime}_{\operatorname{pr}})
. One has

\Delta_{L}\cap\Delta_{\overline{0}}=W[\Delta_{L}](\psi(\Sigma^{\prime}_{\operatorname{pr}}))

and
W[\Delta_{L}]
is the Coxeter group
generated by
s_{\alpha}
with
\alpha\in\psi(\Sigma^{\prime}_{\operatorname{pr}})
.

(ii)

The set of indecomposable elements in
\Delta_{L}\cap\Delta^{+}_{\pi}

coincides with
\psi(\pi^{\prime})
. One has

\Delta_{L}\cap\Delta_{\pi}=W[\Delta_{L}\cap\Delta_{\pi}](\psi(\pi^{\prime}))

and
W[\Delta_{L}\cap\Delta_{\pi}]
is the Coxeter group
generated by
s_{\alpha}
with
\alpha\in\psi(\pi^{\prime})
.

Proof.

The map
s_{\alpha^{\prime}}\mapsto s_{\psi(\alpha^{\prime})}
for
\alpha^{\prime}\in\Delta^{\prime}
defines
a group homomorphism

\psi_{W}:\ W(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})\to W[\Delta_{L}]

which satisfies

\psi(w\alpha^{\prime})=\psi_{W}(w)\psi(\alpha^{\prime})
for all
w\in W(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}),\ \alpha^{\prime}\in\Delta^{\prime}
.
Since

\mathbb{C}\Delta^{\prime}
is a faithful representation of
W(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})
, the map
\psi_{W}

is bijective, so

\psi_{W}:W(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}W[\Delta_{L}].

By
\lx@sectionsign

2.8.3
,
(\Delta^{\prime}_{\overline{0}})^{\operatorname{re}}

is a real root system with the set of simple roots
\Sigma^{\prime}_{\operatorname{pr}}
and

W(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})
is the Coxeter group generated by
s_{\alpha^{\prime}}

with
\alpha^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{\prime}_{\operatorname{pr}}
. This implies (i).

For (ii) take any
\beta\in(\Delta_{L}\cap\Delta^{+}_{\pi})

and set
\beta^{\prime}:=\psi^{-1}(\beta)
. Then

\beta^{\prime}
is a positive even real root in
\Delta^{\prime}
.
By Lemma
2.8.4
,
there exist
\alpha^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\alpha^{\prime}_{j}\in\pi^{\prime}

such that
\beta^{\prime}=s_{\alpha^{\prime}_{j}}\ldots s_{\alpha^{\prime}_{2}}\alpha^{\prime}_{1}

and
\beta^{\prime}=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{j}k_{i}\alpha^{\prime}_{i}
with
k_{i}>0
.
Set
\alpha_{i}:=\psi(\alpha^{\prime}_{i})
and notice that
\alpha_{i}\in\psi(\Sigma^{\prime}_{\operatorname{pr}})
.
Therefore

\beta=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{j}k_{i}\alpha_{i}\ \ \text{ with }\ k_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0},\ \ \alpha_{i}\in\psi(\Sigma^{\prime}_{\operatorname{pr}}).

Let us show that
\alpha_{i}\in\psi(\pi^{\prime})
for all
i=1,\ldots,j
.
Since
\Sigma_{\operatorname{pr}}

is the set of indecomposable elements in
\Delta^{\operatorname{re}+}_{\overline{0}}
we have

\alpha_{i}=\sum\limits_{\gamma\in\Sigma_{\operatorname{pr}}}m^{i}_{\gamma}\gamma\ \text{
for some }\ m^{i}_{\gamma}\geq 0.

Therefore

\beta=\sum\limits_{\gamma\in\Sigma_{\operatorname{pr}}}n_{\gamma}\gamma,\ \ \text{ for }\ n_{\gamma}:=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{j}k_{i}m^{i}_{\gamma}.

If
\alpha_{i}\not\in\psi(\pi^{\prime})
, then

m^{i}_{\gamma}\not=0
for some
\gamma\not\in\pi
, so

n_{\gamma}\not=0
and thus

\beta\not\in\Delta_{\pi}
, a contradiction. Hence
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{j}\in\psi(\pi^{\prime})
. Therefore

\beta\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\psi(\pi^{\prime})

and

\beta=s_{\alpha_{j}}\ldots s_{\alpha_{2}}\alpha_{1}
. This proves that

\psi(\pi^{\prime})
is the set of indecomposable elements in
\Delta_{L}\cap\Delta^{+}_{\pi}

and establishes the formula

\Delta_{L}\cap\Delta_{\pi}=W[\psi(\pi^{\prime})](\psi(\pi^{\prime}))
which implies

W[\Delta_{L}\cap\Delta_{\pi}]=W[\psi(\pi^{\prime})]
. Since
\psi(\pi^{\prime})

is a subset of
\psi(\Sigma^{\prime}_{\operatorname{pr}})
and, by (i),
s_{\alpha}

with
\alpha\in\psi(\Sigma^{\prime}_{\operatorname{pr}})
are Coxeter generators of

W[\Delta_{L}]=W[\psi(\Sigma^{\prime}_{\operatorname{pr}})]
, the set
s_{\alpha}

with
\alpha\in\psi(\pi^{\prime})
are Coxeter generators of

W[\psi(\pi^{\prime})]
.
This completes the proof of (ii).
∎

5.7.
Enright functor

The proofs are based on the following construction.

5.7.1.

Let
\alpha
be a positive even real root and let
\mathfrak{t}\subset\mathfrak{g}

be any subalgebra containing
\mathfrak{g}_{\pm\alpha}
.
Let
\mathcal{M}^{e}(\mathfrak{t})
be the full subcategory of the category of
\mathfrak{t}
-modules
consisting of the objects where
\alpha^{\vee}
acts semisimply with non-integral eigenvalues
and
\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
acts locally nilpotently. By
[
22
]
;
[
17
]
, Section 2, the category
\mathcal{M}^{e}(\mathfrak{t})

admits the Enright functor
\mathcal{C}:\mathcal{M}^{e}(\mathfrak{t})\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{M}^{e}(\mathfrak{t})

which is an equivalence of categories
such that
\mathcal{C}^{2}(N)\cong N
for all
N\in\mathcal{M}^{e}(\mathfrak{t})
.
By construction, the functor
\mathcal{C}
commutes with the restriction functor, i.e.

\mathcal{C}(\operatorname{Res}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathfrak{t}}(N))
is (canonically) isomorphic to

\operatorname{Res}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathcal{C}(N))
for any
N\in\mathcal{M}^{e}(\mathfrak{t})
.

5.7.2.

Let
\pi\subset\Sigma_{\operatorname{pr}}
be as in
\lx@sectionsign

5.4.1
.
Assume that
\alpha\in\pi
is such that

\Sigma
contains
\alpha
or
\alpha/2
.
Let
\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}
be the full subcategory
of
\mathcal{O}^{\operatorname{fin}}(\mathfrak{g})
whose objects
are modules lying in
\mathcal{M}^{e}(\mathfrak{g})
.
By
[
22
]
;
[
17
]
, Theorems 2.3.1 and 3.2 the Enright functor gives
the equivalence of categories

\mathcal{C}:\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}
, such that

(5.5)

\begin{array}[]{l}\mathcal{C}(M(\nu))=M(s_{\alpha}(\nu+\rho)-\rho),\ \ \ \mathcal{C}(L(\nu))=L(s_{\alpha}(\nu+\rho)-\rho),\\
(e^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}-e^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}})\operatorname{ch}\mathcal{C}(N)=s_{\alpha}\bigl((e^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}-e^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}})\operatorname{ch}N\bigr)\end{array}

for all
L(\nu),N\in\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}
.
Notice that
L(\nu)\in\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}

if and only if
\langle\nu,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle\not\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Since
\alpha\in\pi
,
the product
e^{\rho_{\pi}-\alpha/2}D_{\pi}(1-e^{-\alpha})^{-1}

is naturally
s_{\alpha}
-invariant (see (
5.1
)).
Now the second formula in (
5.5
) gives

\begin{array}[]{ll}D_{\pi}e^{\rho_{\pi}}\operatorname{ch}N&=\frac{D_{\pi}}{1-e^{-\alpha}}\cdot\frac{e^{\rho_{\pi}}}{e^{\alpha/2}}\cdot(e^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}-e^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}})\operatorname{ch}N\\
&=\frac{D_{\pi}}{1-e^{-\alpha}}\cdot\frac{e^{\rho_{\pi}}}{e^{\alpha/2}}\cdot s_{\alpha}\bigl((e^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}-e^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}})\operatorname{ch}\mathcal{C}(N)\bigr)\\
&=s_{\alpha}\bigl(\frac{D_{\pi}}{1-e^{-\alpha}}\cdot\frac{e^{\rho_{\pi}}}{e^{\alpha/2}}\cdot(e^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}-e^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}})\operatorname{ch}\mathcal{C}(N)\bigr)=s_{\alpha}\bigl(D_{\pi}e^{\rho_{\pi}}\operatorname{ch}\mathcal{C}(N)\bigr)\end{array}

for the natural action of
s_{\alpha}
, which implies

(5.6)

D_{\pi}e^{\rho_{\pi}}\operatorname{ch}\mathcal{C}(N)=s_{\alpha}\bigl(D_{\pi}e^{\rho_{\pi}}\operatorname{ch}N\bigr)\ \ \text{ for all }N\in\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}

for the natural action of
s_{\alpha}
.

5.7.3.

Lemma.

For any
N\in\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}
and
\gamma\in\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}
one has

D_{\pi}e^{\rho_{\pi}}\operatorname{ch}N=-s_{\gamma}\bigl(D_{\pi}e^{\rho_{\pi}}\operatorname{ch}N\bigr)\ \ \Longleftrightarrow\ \ D_{\pi}e^{\rho_{\pi}}\operatorname{ch}\mathcal{C}(N)=-s_{s_{\alpha}\gamma}\bigl((D_{\pi}e^{\rho_{\pi}}\operatorname{ch}\mathcal{C}(N)\bigr).

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{C}^{2}(N)\cong N
it is enough to verify
the implication
\Longrightarrow
.
Combining the formulas

D_{\pi}e^{\rho_{\pi}}\operatorname{ch}N=-s_{\gamma}\bigl(D_{\pi}e^{\rho_{\pi}}\operatorname{ch}N\bigr)

and (
5.6
) we get

\begin{array}[]{rl}D_{\pi}e^{\rho_{\pi}}\operatorname{ch}\mathcal{C}(N)&=s_{\alpha}\bigl(D_{\pi}e^{\rho_{\pi}}\operatorname{ch}N\bigr)=-s_{\alpha}s_{\gamma}\bigl(D_{\pi}e^{\rho_{\pi}}\operatorname{ch}N\bigr)=-s_{s_{\alpha}\gamma}s_{\alpha}\bigl(D_{\pi}e^{\rho_{\pi}}\operatorname{ch}N\bigr)\\
&=-s_{s_{\alpha}\gamma}\bigl(D_{\pi}e^{\rho_{\pi}}\operatorname{ch}\mathcal{C}(N)\bigr)\end{array}

as required.
∎

5.7.4.

Let
N\in\mathcal{O}^{\operatorname{fin}}(\mathfrak{g})
be such that
\Delta_{N}

is well defined. We claim that

(5.7)

\alpha\not\in\Delta_{N}\ \ \Longrightarrow\ \ N\in\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}.

Indeed, if
\alpha\not\in\Delta_{N}
, then
by (
4.2
),

\langle\lambda,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle\not\in\mathbb{Z}

for any
\lambda
such that
[N:L(\lambda)]\not=0
;
this implies
\langle\mu,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle\not\in\mathbb{Z}

for all
\mu
such that
N_{\mu}\not=0
, so
N\in\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}
.

5.7.5.

We will use the following formula

(5.8)

[\operatorname{Res}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathfrak{g}_{\pi}}\mathcal{C}(N):L_{\mathfrak{g}_{\pi}}(s_{\alpha}(\nu+\rho_{\pi})-\rho_{\pi})]=[\operatorname{Res}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathfrak{g}_{\pi}}N:L_{\mathfrak{g}_{\pi}}(\nu)]\ \text{ for any }\ N\in\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}.

Indeed, take
N\in\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}
.
Then
\mathcal{C}(N)\in\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}
. Set
M:=\operatorname{Res}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathfrak{g}_{\pi}}N
.
(If
M\in\mathcal{O}^{\operatorname{fin}}(\mathfrak{g}_{\pi})
the formula follows
immediately from
\lx@sectionsign

5.7.2
, but
M

might be not finitely generated.)
By
\lx@sectionsign

2.10.3
,

M\in\mathcal{O}_{\Sigma}(\mathfrak{g}_{\pi})

and this module admits a weak composition series at
\nu
:

0=M_{0}\subset M_{1}\subset\ldots\subset M_{r}=M.

Observe that
M_{1},\ldots,M_{r}\in\mathcal{M}^{e}(\mathfrak{g}_{\pi})
. In the light of
\lx@sectionsign

5.7.1
we obtain a finite filtration

0=\mathcal{C}(M_{0})\subset\mathcal{C}(M_{1})\subset\ldots\mathcal{C}(M_{r})=\mathcal{C}(M).

Using (
5.5
) for the algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{\pi}
we get

\mathcal{C}(M_{i}/M_{i-1})\cong L_{\mathfrak{g}_{\pi}}(s_{\alpha}(\nu+\rho_{\pi})-\rho_{\pi})

if and only if
M_{i}/M_{i-1}\cong L_{\mathfrak{g}_{\pi}}(\nu)
. This gives the inequality
\geq

in (
5.8
). The opposite inequality follows from
the formula
\mathcal{C}^{2}(N)\cong N
. This establishes (
5.8
).

5.8.
Proof of Theorem
5.5.1

Let
N\in\mathcal{O}^{\operatorname{fin}}(\mathfrak{g})
be such that
\Delta_{N}
is well defined.
We want to prove the equivalence of the conditions

(a)

N
is
\pi
-quasi-admissible;

(b)

all irreducible
subquotients of
N
are
\pi
-quasi-admissible;

(c)

all irreducible
subquotients of

M:=\operatorname{Res}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathfrak{g}_{\pi}}N
are quasi-admissible

\mathfrak{g}_{\pi}
-modules, and
\Delta_{\pi}\cap\Delta_{N}=\Delta_{M}
.

5.8.1.

Lemma.

Fix any
\lambda

and introduce
\pi^{\prime}
for
L(\lambda)
as in
\lx@sectionsign

5.4.1
.
If
\gamma\in\psi(\pi^{\prime})
is such that
\gamma\not\in\pi
, then there exists

\alpha\in\pi
such that

s_{\alpha}\gamma=\gamma-j\alpha
for some
j\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}

and
s_{\alpha}\gamma\in\Delta^{+}_{\pi}
. Moreover,

\alpha\in\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}
.

Proof.

Recall that
\pi\subset\Sigma_{\operatorname{pr}}
and
\Delta_{\pi}=W[\pi](\pi)
.
Using Lemma
2.8.4
for
\Delta_{\pi}
, we conclude that there exists

\alpha\in\pi
such that
\gamma-s_{\alpha}\gamma=j\alpha
for some
j\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}

and
s_{\alpha}\gamma\in\Delta^{+}_{\pi}
.
If
\alpha\in\Delta_{N}
, then

s_{\alpha}\gamma\in\Delta_{N}
. Therefore

\gamma=s_{\alpha}\gamma+j\alpha
with
s_{\alpha}\gamma,\alpha

in
\Delta_{N}\cap\Delta^{+}_{\pi}

which contradicts to Lemma
5.6.3
(since
\gamma\in\psi(\pi^{\prime})
).
Therefore
\alpha\not\in\Delta_{N}
.
∎

5.8.2.

Lemma.

Fix any
\lambda
such that
[N:L(\lambda)]\not=0

and introduce
\pi^{\prime}
as in
\lx@sectionsign

5.4.1
.
Take
\gamma\in\psi(\pi^{\prime})
and
\nu
such that
[\operatorname{Res}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathfrak{g}_{\pi}}N:L_{\mathfrak{g}_{\pi}}(\nu)]\not=0
.
If
D_{\pi}e^{\rho_{\pi}}\operatorname{ch}N
is
s_{\gamma}
-anti-invariant, then

D_{\pi}e^{\rho_{\pi}}\operatorname{ch}L_{\mathfrak{g}_{\pi}}(\nu)
is
s_{\gamma}
-anti-invariant
and
\langle\nu,\gamma^{\vee}\rangle\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on the partial order on
\Delta_{\pi}
given
by
\alpha\geq\beta
if
\alpha-\beta\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\pi
.

If
\gamma\in\pi
, then, by Lemma
5.1.12
,
\mathfrak{g}_{-\gamma}
acts locally nilpotently on
N
and
thus acts locally nilpotently on
L_{\mathfrak{g}_{\pi}}(\nu)
.
By Lemma
5.1.12
,

R_{\pi}e^{\rho_{\pi}}\operatorname{ch}L_{\mathfrak{g}_{\pi}}(\nu)
is

s_{\gamma}
-anti-invariant.

Take
\gamma\not\in\pi
and
\alpha
as in Lemma
5.8.1
.
Since
\alpha\in\pi
,
either
\alpha
or
\alpha/2
lies in a certain base in the spine.
Using
5.4.3

we conclude that, without loss of generality, we can (and will) assume that

\Sigma
contains
\alpha
or
\alpha/2
. Let
\mathcal{C}

be the Enright functor for
\alpha
. Since
\alpha\not\in\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}=\Delta_{N}

we have
\langle\nu,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle\not\in\mathbb{Z}
by

\lx@sectionsign

5.6
(iii). By (
5.8
)
we have

[\operatorname{Res}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathfrak{g}_{\pi}}(\mathcal{C}(N)):L_{\mathfrak{g}_{\pi}}(\mu)]\not=0\ \ \text{ for }\mu:=s_{\alpha}(\nu+\rho_{\pi})-\rho_{\pi}.

Combining Lemma
5.7.3

and (
5.7
) we conclude that
the assumption that

D_{\pi}e^{\rho_{\pi}}\operatorname{ch}N

is
s_{\gamma}
-anti-invariant implies
the

s_{s_{\alpha}\gamma}
-anti-invariance
of
D_{\pi}e^{\rho_{\pi}}\operatorname{ch}\mathcal{C}(N)
.

By Lemma
4.5.2
we have

\psi_{s_{\alpha}(\lambda+\rho)-\rho}(\gamma^{\prime})=s_{\alpha}\gamma
.
By
\lx@sectionsign

5.7.2
, the module
L(s_{\alpha}(\lambda+\rho)-\rho)

is a subquotient of
\mathcal{C}(N)
.
Since
s_{\alpha}\gamma<\gamma
