, by induction hypothesis, the
s_{s_{\alpha}\gamma}
-anti-invariance
of
D_{\pi}e^{\rho_{\pi}}\operatorname{ch}\mathcal{C}(N)
implies

s_{s_{\alpha}\gamma}
-anti-invariance
of
D_{\pi}e^{\rho_{\pi}}\operatorname{ch}L_{\mathfrak{g}_{\pi}}(\mu)

and
\langle\mu,(s_{\alpha}\gamma)^{\vee}\rangle\not\in\mathbb{Z}
.
In particular,

\langle\mu,(s_{\alpha}\gamma)^{\vee}\rangle=\langle s_{\alpha}(\nu+\rho_{\pi}),(s_{\alpha}\gamma)^{\vee}\rangle=\langle\nu+\rho_{\pi},\gamma^{\vee}\rangle\not\in\mathbb{Z}

so
\langle\nu,\gamma^{\vee}\rangle\not\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Since
L_{\mathfrak{g}_{\pi}}(\mu)=\mathcal{C}(L_{\mathfrak{g}_{\pi}}(\nu))
,
the
s_{s_{\alpha}\gamma}
-anti-invariance
of
D_{\pi}e^{\rho_{\pi}}\operatorname{ch}L_{\mathfrak{g}_{\pi}}(\mu)
implies
the
s_{\gamma}
-anti-invariance
of
D_{\pi}e^{\rho_{\pi}}\operatorname{ch}L_{\mathfrak{g}_{\pi}}(\nu)
. This completes the proof.
∎

5.8.3.

Assume that
N
is
\pi
-quasi-admissible.
Combining the above lemma and
\lx@sectionsign

5.6
(iii) we get

\psi(\pi^{\prime})\subset\{\beta\in\Delta_{\pi}|\ \langle\nu,\beta^{\vee}\rangle\in\mathbb{Z}\}\subset(\Delta_{N}\cap\Delta_{\pi}).

Using Lemma
5.6.3
we conclude
(\Delta_{\pi}\cap\Delta_{N})=\{\beta\in\Delta_{\pi}|\ \langle\nu,\beta^{\vee}\rangle\in\mathbb{Z}\}
. Since this formula holds for all
\nu
such that

[M:L_{\mathfrak{g}_{\pi}}(\nu)]\not=0
, the set
\Delta_{M}
is well defined
and coincides with
\Delta_{\pi}\cap\Delta_{N}
, see
\lx@sectionsign

5.6
(ii).
Combining the above lemma and Lemma
5.6.3
we conclude that

L_{\mathfrak{g}_{\pi}}(\nu)
is quasi-admissible. This establishes the implication (a)
\Longrightarrow

(c).

Since
\operatorname{ch}N
is the sum of
the characters of irreducible subquotients of
N
, (b) implies (a).
Similarly,
\operatorname{ch}N
is the sum of
the characters of irreducible subquotients of
M
, so (c) implies (a).
Hence (a)
\Longleftrightarrow
(c) and (b)
\Longrightarrow
(a).

Now assume that (c) holds.
Let
L
be any subquotient of
N
. We have
\Delta_{N}=\Delta_{L}
. Any irreducible subquotient
L_{1}
of

\operatorname{Res}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathfrak{g}_{\pi}}L
is a subquotient of
M
, so, by (c),
it is quasi-admissible
\mathfrak{g}_{\pi}
-module. Moreover,
and
\Delta_{\pi}\cap\Delta_{L}=\Delta_{\pi}\cap\Delta_{N}=\Delta_{L_{1}}
.
Using the implication (c)
\Longrightarrow
(a) for
L
we conclude that

L
is
\pi
-quasi-admissible. This completes the proof of
the implication (c)
\Longrightarrow
(b) and the proof of Theorem
5.5.1
.

5.9.
Proof of Corollary
5.5.2

Let
N
be a
\pi
-quasi-admissible
module and
M:=\operatorname{Res}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathfrak{g}_{\pi}}(N)
. Assume that
M
is non-critical.
Fix
v\in M
and let
M_{1}
be a cyclic module generated by
v
.
Then
M_{1}\in\mathcal{O}^{\operatorname{fin}}(\mathfrak{g}_{\pi})
.
By Theorem
5.5.1
,
any irreducible subquotient of
M_{1}

is quasi-admissible. Since
M_{1}
is non-critical,
M_{1}

is completely reducible, see Theorem
5.2.4
. Hence any cyclic submodule of
M
is completely reducible.
Thus
M
is a sum of irreducible modules, so
M
is completely reducible
(see
[
42
]
, Chapter XVII).

5.10.
Proof of Theorem
5.5.3

By Lemma
5.6.3
, (a) is equivalent to
s_{\alpha}(D_{\pi}e^{\rho_{\pi}}\operatorname{ch}L)=-D_{\pi}e^{\rho_{\pi}}\operatorname{ch}L

for all
\alpha\in\psi(\pi^{\prime})
.
On the other hand, (b) is equivalent to
the fact that for all
\gamma^{\prime}\in\pi^{\prime}
the root space
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{-\gamma^{\prime}}

acts locally nilpotently on
L^{\prime}
. Using Lemma
5.1.12

we conclude that it is enough to verify that for
\gamma^{\prime}\in\pi^{\prime}

the following conditions are equivalent

(a’)

s_{\gamma}(D_{\pi}e^{\rho_{\pi}}\operatorname{ch}L)=-D_{\pi}e^{\rho_{\pi}}\operatorname{ch}L\

for
\gamma:=\psi(\gamma^{\prime})
;

(b’)

s_{\gamma^{\prime}}(D_{\pi^{\prime}}e^{\rho_{\pi^{\prime}}}\operatorname{ch}L^{\prime})=-D_{\pi^{\prime}}e^{\rho_{\pi^{\prime}}}\operatorname{ch}L^{\prime}
.

This equivalence is established in the lemma below.

5.10.1.

Lemma.

For
\gamma^{\prime}\in\pi^{\prime}
the assertions (a’)
and (b’) are equivalent.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on the partial order on
\Delta_{\pi}
given
by
\nu\geq\mu
if
\nu-\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\pi
.
For the induction base we
assume that
\gamma\in\pi
.
Then either
\gamma
or
\gamma/2
lies in a certain base in the spine.
Using
\lx@sectionsign\lx@sectionsign

4.4.1
and
5.4.3

we conclude that, without loss of generality, we can (and will) assume that

\Sigma
contains
\gamma
or
\gamma/2
.
Then
\Sigma^{\prime}
contains
\gamma^{\prime}
or
\gamma^{\prime}/2
respectively.
By Lemma
5.1.12
(a’) and (b’) are equivalent to locally nilpotence of actions
of
\mathfrak{g}_{-\gamma}
and
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{\gamma^{\prime}}
on
L
and
L^{\prime}
respectively.
It is well-known that
\mathfrak{g}_{-\gamma}
acts locally nilpotently on
L

if and only if
\frac{2(\lambda+\rho,\gamma)}{(\gamma,\gamma)}

is a positive integer which is odd if
\gamma/2\in\Sigma
.
Using the formula

\frac{2(\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})}{(\gamma^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})}=\frac{2(\lambda+\rho,\gamma)}{(\gamma,\gamma)}
.

Now assume that
\gamma\not\in\pi
.
Take
\gamma\not\in\pi
and
\alpha
as in Lemma
5.8.1
.
Since
\alpha\in\pi
,
either
\alpha
or
\alpha/2
lies in a certain base in the spine.
Using
\lx@sectionsign\lx@sectionsign

4.4.1
and
5.4.3

we conclude that, without loss of generality,
we can (and will) assume that

\Sigma
contains
\alpha
or
\alpha/2
and thus

\Sigma^{\prime}
contains
\alpha^{\prime}
or
\alpha^{\prime}/2
respectively.
Take
\nu:=s_{\alpha}(\lambda+\rho)-\rho
.
Combining Lemma
5.7.3

and (
5.7
) we conclude that (a’) is equivalent to

D_{\pi}e^{\rho_{\pi}}\operatorname{ch}L(\nu)=-s_{s_{\alpha}\gamma}\bigl((D_{\pi}e^{\rho_{\pi}}\operatorname{ch}L(\nu)\bigr).

By Lemma
4.5.2
we have

\psi_{\nu}(\gamma^{\prime})=s_{\alpha}\gamma
. Since
s_{\alpha}\gamma<\gamma
,
by induction hypothesis, (b’) is equivalent to
s_{s_{\alpha}\gamma}
-anti-invariance
of
D_{\pi}e^{\rho_{\pi}}\operatorname{ch}L(\nu)
. Hence (a’) is equivalent to (b’) for
\gamma
.
This completes the proof.
∎

6.
Admissible weights

In this section
\mathfrak{g}\not=D(2|1,a)^{(1)}
is an indecomposable symmetrizable affine Kac-Moody superalgebra,
which is not anisotropic (i.e.,
\Delta
contains a real isotropic
root).

6.1.
Notation

We normalise the bilinear form
(-,-)
as in
\lx@sectionsign

3.5
and in
Appendix
A
. For a weight
\lambda\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}
we call
k:=(\lambda,\delta)
the
level
of
\lambda
.

We let
\dot{\mathfrak{g}}\subset\mathfrak{g}
be the finite-dimensional
Kac-Moody superalgebra described in Appendix
A
.
Note that

\mathfrak{g}=\dot{\mathfrak{g}}^{(1)}
if
\mathfrak{g}
is non-twisted.
We denote by
\dot{\Delta}
the root system of
\dot{\mathfrak{g}}
.
We let
\Lambda_{0}\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}
be such that

(\Lambda_{0},\delta)=1
and

(\Lambda_{0},\dot{\Delta})=(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{0})=0
. We let

\operatorname{cl}
be the canonical map

\operatorname{cl}:\mathbb{Q}\Delta\to\mathbb{Q}\Delta/\mathbb{Q}\delta=\mathbb{Q}\dot{\Delta}
.
By Remark
2.8.2
,

\dot{\Sigma}_{\operatorname{pr}}:=\Sigma_{\operatorname{pr}}\cap\dot{\Delta}
is the set of principal roots
for
\dot{\Delta}
. We set

\begin{array}[]{l}\pi^{\#}:=\{\alpha\in\Sigma_{\operatorname{pr}}|\ (\alpha,\alpha)>0\},\ \ \ \Delta^{\#}:=\Delta_{\pi^{\#}}=\{\alpha\in\Delta_{\overline{0}}|\ (\alpha,\alpha)>0\}\\
\pi:=\pi^{\#}\cup\dot{\Sigma}_{\operatorname{pr}}=\{\alpha\in\Sigma_{\operatorname{pr}}|\ \alpha\in\dot{\Delta}\text{ or }(\alpha,\alpha)>0\}.\end{array}

The assumption
\mathfrak{g}\not=D(2|1,a)^{(1)}
ensures that
\pi

is linearly independent. If
\mathfrak{g}=\dot{\mathfrak{g}}^{(1)}

is a non-twisted affine Kac-Moody superalgebra,
then
\mathfrak{g}_{\pi^{\#}}
is the direct product of an abelian Lie algebra and
a non-twisted affine Kac-Moody algebra,
which is the affinization of the ”largest component”
\dot{\mathfrak{g}}^{\#}
of
\dot{\mathfrak{g}}_{\overline{0}}
, since the root system

\Delta(\mathfrak{g}_{\pi^{\#}})
is equal to
\Delta(\dot{\mathfrak{g}}^{\#})+\mathbb{Z}\delta
.

Since we use the normalised invariant form, the longest root in
\Delta^{\#}
is of square length
2
and
L(k\Lambda_{0})

is
\pi^{\#}
-integrable if and only if
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.

6.1.1.
Vacuum modules

For each
k\in\mathbb{C}
we introduce the vacuum module
V^{k}
of level
k
in the usual way:
we set
\mathfrak{g}_{+}:=\mathfrak{h}+\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\Delta:(\alpha,\Lambda_{0})\geq 0}\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}

and let
\mathbb{C}_{k}
the one-dimensional
\mathfrak{g}_{+}
-module
where each
h\in\mathfrak{h}
acts by multiplication by
\langle k\Lambda_{0},h\rangle
; we set

V^{k}:=\operatorname{Ind}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathfrak{g}_{+}}\mathbb{C}_{k}.

Notice that
L(k\Lambda_{0})
is the unique irreducible quotient of
V^{k}
;
we call this module a
simple vacuum module
; this term will be also used
for the corresponding
[\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}]
-module.

6.1.2.

Remark.

Since
V^{k}
is a cyclic module generated by
\mathbb{C}_{k}

and
\dot{\mathfrak{g}}\mathbb{C}_{k}=0
,
V^{k}
is
\dot{\Sigma}_{\operatorname{pr}}
-integrable. Therefore any subquotient of
V^{k}

is
\dot{\Sigma}_{\operatorname{pr}}
-integrable and any

\pi^{\#}
-integrable subquotient is
\pi
-integrable.
Moreover, any
\pi^{\#}
-quasi-admissible subquotient of
V^{k}

is
\pi
-quasi-admissible.

6.2.
The module
L^{\prime}
for
L:=L(k\Lambda_{0})

Consider the case
\mathfrak{g}=\dot{\mathfrak{g}}^{(1)}
or
D(m+1|n)^{(2)}
.
Let
L:=L(k\Lambda_{0})
for
k\in\mathbb{Q}
.
We let
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}:=\mathfrak{g}_{k\Lambda_{0}}

and let
\psi:\mathbb{Z}\Delta(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})\to\mathbb{Z}\Delta

be the linear map
as in
\lx@sectionsign

4.3
. It is easy to see that

\dot{\Delta}\subset\Delta_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho}
and
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}:=\mathfrak{g}_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho}

is of type (Aff).
Let
(-,-)^{\prime}
be the normalised invariant form on
\Delta(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})
.

6.3.

Lemma.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a non-twisted affine Kac-Moody superalgebra or
D(m+1|n)^{(2)}
.
Let
k\in\mathbb{Q}
,
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime},\psi
be as
\lx@sectionsign

6.2
.

(i)

One has
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}\cong\mathfrak{g}
if
\mathfrak{g}\not=B(m|n)^{(1)}
or
D(m+1|n)^{(2)}
.

(ii)

If
\mathfrak{g}
is
B(m|n)^{(1)}
or
D(m+1|n)^{(2)}
,
then
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
is also
B(m|n)^{(1)}
or
D(m+1|n)^{(2)}
.

(iii)

One has

(\psi(v_{1}),\psi(v_{2}))=(v_{1},v_{2})^{\prime}
for
v_{1},v_{2}\in\Delta(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})

and
\psi^{-1}(\dot{\Delta})
coincides with
\dot{\Delta}^{\prime}
as introduced in Appendix
A.1.1
.

(iv)

For
L:=L(k\Lambda_{0})

the module
L^{\prime}
is a simple vacuum
[\mathfrak{g}^{\prime},\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}]
-module (we denote its level by
k^{\prime}
).

Proof.

We have

\dot{\Delta}\subset\operatorname{cl}(\Delta_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho})\subset\operatorname{cl}(\Delta)
.
Since
\mathfrak{g}=\dot{\mathfrak{g}}^{(1)}
or
D(m+1|n)^{(2)}
, we have

\operatorname{cl}(\Delta)=\dot{\Delta}\cup\{0\}
, so

\operatorname{cl}(\Delta_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho})=\dot{\Delta}
.
Using the table in Appendix
A.1.1
,
we conclude that

\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}\cong\mathfrak{g}
if
\mathfrak{g}
is non-twisted and

\mathfrak{g}\not=B(m|n)^{(1)}
. If
\mathfrak{g}=B(m|n)^{(1)}

or
D(m+1|n)^{(2)}
, then
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
is also
\mathfrak{g}=B(m|n)^{(1)}

or
D(m+1|n)^{(2)}
. This establishes (i), (ii) and
the formula
\psi^{-1}(\dot{\Delta})=\dot{\Delta}^{\prime}
.
The restriction
of
(-,-)^{\prime}
to
\operatorname{cl}(\Delta^{\prime})\cong\dot{\Delta}
is the normalised invariant form, so
the restriction of
(-,-)^{\prime}
to
\mathbb{Z}\Delta(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})

coincides with the restriction of the normalised invariant form. This establishes (iii).
For (iv)
set
\lambda:=k\Lambda_{0}
and introduce
\lambda^{\prime}

as in
\lx@sectionsign

4.3
. For
\alpha\in\dot{\Delta}
we have

(\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime},\psi^{-1}(\alpha))=(\lambda+\rho,\alpha)=(\dot{\rho},\alpha)

where
\dot{\rho}
is the Weyl vector for
\dot{\Delta}^{+}
.
Since
\psi^{-1}(\dot{\Delta})=\dot{\Delta}^{\prime}
,
this gives
(\lambda^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})=0

for all
\alpha^{\prime}\in\dot{\Delta}^{\prime}
. Therefore

L_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}(\lambda^{\prime})
is a vacuum module.
∎

6.4.
Admissibility

We retain notation of
\lx@sectionsign

6.1
.

6.4.1.
Definition

We call
N\in\mathcal{O}_{\Sigma}(\mathfrak{g})

admissible
if
N
is non-critical,
\pi^{\#}
-quasi-admissible,
and
\mathbb{Q}(\Delta_{N}\cap\Delta^{\#})=\mathbb{Q}\Delta^{\#}
.
We call a weight
\lambda

admissible
if
L(\lambda)

is admissible (i.e., if
L(\lambda)
is
\pi^{\#}
-quasi-admissible
and
\mathbb{Q}(\Delta^{\#}\cap\Delta_{\lambda+\rho})=\mathbb{Q}\Delta^{\#}
).
The level
k
is called
admissible if the weight
k\Lambda_{0}
is admissible.
An admissible level
k
is called

principal admissible

if

\Delta_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho}
is isometric to
\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}
, and
is called
subprincipal admissible

otherwise. An admissible weight

\lambda
is called
principal
(resp., subprincipal) admissible
if

(\lambda,\delta)
is a principal
(resp., subprincipal) admissible level, and

\Delta^{\#}\cap\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}
is isometric to

\Delta^{\#}\cap\Delta_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho}
.

6.4.2.

We claim that if
\lambda
is a principal or a subprincipal
admissible weight, then
\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}

is isometric to the set of real roots of a
Kac-Moody superalgebra.

Indeed, by Theorem
B.6
(iii), (iv),
if
\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}\not\cong\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})

for a non-critical
\lambda\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}
, then

\mathfrak{g}=A(2m-1|2n-1)^{(2)}
and
\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}

has an indecomposable component of type
A(1|1)^{(2)}
.
Then
\Delta^{\#}\cap\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}

has an indecomposable component of type

\{\alpha\in A(1|1)^{(2)}|\ (\alpha,\alpha)>0\}\cong A_{1}^{(1)}
,
so
\operatorname{cl}(\Delta^{\#}\cap\Delta_{\lambda+\rho})

has an indecomposable component of type
A_{1}
.
However, if
\lambda
is a principal or a subprincipal
admissible weight, then
\operatorname{cl}(\Delta^{\#}\cap\Delta_{\lambda+\rho})
is isometric to

\operatorname{cl}(\Delta^{\#}\cap\Delta_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho})
.
Without loss of generality we take
m\leq n
. Then
n>1
.
Since
\dot{\Delta}\subset\Delta_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho})
we have

D_{n}=\dot{\Delta}^{\#}\subset\operatorname{cl}(\Delta^{\#}\cap\Delta_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho})\subset\operatorname{cl}(\Delta^{\#})=C_{n}.

Therefore
\operatorname{cl}(\Delta^{\#}\cap\Delta_{\lambda+\rho})

does not have an indecomposable component of type
A_{1}
.

In Example
4.2.4

\lambda
is an admissible weight and
\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}

is not isometric to the set of real roots of a
Kac-Moody superalgebra.

6.4.3.

Lemma
6.3
implies the following

6.4.4.

Corollary.

If

\mathfrak{g}
is a non-twisted affine Kac-Moody superalgebra, which is not an affine Kac-Moody
algebra,
B(m|n)^{(1)}
,
D(2|1,a)^{(1)}
,
then any admissible level is principal admissible.

6.4.5.

Lemma.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a non-twisted affine Kac-Moody superalgebra.

(i)

If
\lambda
is admissible, then
(\lambda+\rho,\delta)\in\mathbb{Q}_{>0}
.

(ii)

Any (principal) admissible level for
\mathfrak{g}
is a (principal) admissible level for
the non-twisted affine Kac-Moody algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{\pi^{\#}}
.

Proof.

We set

(\pi^{\#})^{\prime}:=\{\alpha^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{\prime}_{\operatorname{pr}}|\ \psi(\alpha^{\prime})\in\Delta^{\#}\}
.
Then

(\pi^{\#})^{\prime}=\{\alpha^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{\prime}_{\operatorname{pr}}|\ \psi(\alpha^{\prime})\in\Delta^{\#}\}=\{\alpha^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{\prime}_{\operatorname{pr}}|\ (\alpha^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})>0\}.

The condition
\mathbb{Q}\Delta^{\#}_{\lambda+\rho}=\mathbb{Q}\Delta^{\#}

implies the existence of
q\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
such that
q\delta\in\Delta^{\#}_{\lambda+\rho}
.
Then
\psi^{-1}(q\delta)
is a positive imaginary root in
\Delta(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})
.
By Theorem
5.5.3
,
L_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}(\lambda^{\prime})
is
(\pi^{\#})^{\prime}
-integrable,
so
(\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime},\psi^{-1}(q\delta))\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}

which gives
(\lambda+\rho,q\delta)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.
By definition,
\lambda
is non-critical, thus
(\lambda+\rho,\delta)\in\mathbb{Q}_{>0}
.
This proves (i).

For (ii) let
k
be a (principal) admissible weight.
Combining Theorem
5.5.1
(iii) and
\lx@sectionsign

5.6
(ii),
we conclude that
L_{\mathfrak{g}_{\pi^{\#}}}(k\Lambda_{0})

is quasi-admissible (since this module is a subquotient of
\operatorname{Res}_{\mathfrak{g}_{\pi^{\#}}}^{\mathfrak{g}}L(k\Lambda_{0})
) and

\{\alpha\in\Delta^{\#}|\ \langle\lambda,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle\in\mathbb{Z}\}=\Delta^{\#}\cap R_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho}
, so
the
Q
-span of this set coincides
with
\mathbb{Q}\Delta^{\#}
. In order to prove that
k
is a (principal) admissible level for
\mathfrak{g}_{\pi^{\#}}
,
it remains to verify that

k
is not the critical level for
\mathfrak{g}_{\pi^{\#}}
.
Let
\rho^{\#}
be the Weyl vector
for the Kac-Moody algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{\pi^{\#}}
. Since

\mathfrak{g}
is non-twisted,

\delta
is the minimal imaginary root of
\mathfrak{g}_{\pi^{\#}}
and
the restriction of
(-,-)

to
\Delta^{\#}
is the normalised invariant form. Thus

(\rho^{\#},\delta)=h^{\vee,\#}
is the dual Coxeter
number for
\Delta^{\#}
.
Using the table in
\lx@sectionsign

3.5
we see that

h^{\vee}\leq h^{\vee,\#}
(the equality holds if and only if
\mathfrak{g}
is a Lie algebra,
that is
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{g}_{\pi^{\#}}
). Therefore

(\rho^{\#}-\rho,\delta)\geq 0

and thus

(k\Lambda_{0}+\rho^{\#},\delta)=(k\Lambda_{0}+\rho,\delta)+(\rho^{\#}-\rho,\delta)>0,

since, by (i),
(k\Lambda_{0}+\rho,\delta)>0
.
∎

6.5.

Proposition.

(i)

Let
\mathfrak{g}\not=D(2|1,a)^{(1)}
be of type (Aff).
Assume that
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
is such that
k+h^{\vee}\not=0
.
Then
L(k\Lambda_{0})
is the unique
\pi^{\#}
-integrable subquotient of
V^{k}
.

(ii)

Let
\mathfrak{g}\not=D(2|1,a)^{(1)}
be a non-twisted affine Kac-Moody superalgebra.
If
k
is an admissible level, then
L(k\Lambda_{0})
is
the unique
\pi^{\#}
-quasi-admissible subquotient of
V^{k}
.

Proof.

We fix a base
\Sigma
as in Appendix
A
; then
the set

\dot{\Sigma}=\Sigma\cap\dot{\Delta}
is a base of
\dot{\Delta}
.

For (i) let
L(k\Lambda_{0}-\mu)
be a
\pi
-integrable subquotient of the Verma module
M(k\Lambda_{0})

and
\mu\not=0
.
Then
\langle k\Lambda_{0}-\mu,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle\geq 0
for all
\alpha\in\pi

and
2(k\Lambda_{0}+\rho,\mu)=(\mu,\mu)
.
By Proposition
A.3
this gives
(k\Lambda_{0}-\mu)\in\mathbb{Z}\dot{\Sigma}
,
so the
(k\Lambda_{0}-\mu)
-weight space in
V^{k}
is zero. Hence

L(k\Lambda_{0}-\mu)
is not a subquotient of
V^{k}
.

For (ii) let
L(\nu)
be a
\pi^{\#}
-quasi-admissible subquotient of
V^{k}

and
\nu\not=k\Lambda_{0}
.
By Proposition
4.6.2
(i),
\nu\in U(k\Lambda_{0})
.
Set
\lambda:=k\Lambda_{0}
. By Lemma
6.3
(iv),
we have
\lambda^{\prime}=k^{\prime}\Lambda^{\prime}_{0}
. Take
\nu^{\prime}
as in
\lx@sectionsign

4.5.1
.
By
\lx@sectionsign

4.6.6
we can choose

\nu^{\prime}\in U(\lambda^{\prime})
.
By Theorem
5.5.3
,
L_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}(\nu^{\prime})

is
\pi^{\prime}
-integrable where
\pi^{\prime}:=\{\alpha^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{\prime}_{\operatorname{pr}}|\ \psi(\alpha^{\prime})\in\Delta_{\pi}\}
.
By Lemma
6.3
(iii), we have

\pi^{\prime}:=\{\alpha^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{\prime}_{\operatorname{pr}}|\ (\alpha^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})>0\}\cup(\Sigma^{\prime}_{\operatorname{pr}}\cap\dot{\Delta}^{\prime}).

Using Proposition
A.3
we obtain

\lambda^{\prime}-\nu^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}\dot{\Delta}^{\prime}
.
Now
\lx@sectionsign

4.6.6
gives
\lambda-\nu\in\mathbb{Z}\dot{\Delta}
,
so the
\nu
-weight space in
V^{k}
is zero (since
\nu\not=k\Lambda_{0}
).
Hence
L(\nu)
is not a subquotient of
V^{k}
.
Now both assertions follow from Remark
6.1.2
.
∎

7.
V_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
-modules

In this section
\mathfrak{g}=\dot{\mathfrak{g}}^{(1)}
is an indecomposable non-twisted affine Kac-Moody
superalgebra and
\mathfrak{g}\not=D(2|1,a)^{(1)}
.

7.1.
Notation

We denote by

V^{k}(\mathfrak{g})
,
V_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
the universal and simple
affine vertex superalgebras of level
k
respectively.
We always assume that
k
is non-critical.
We denote by
\mathcal{O}_{\Sigma}(\mathfrak{g})^{k}
,
{\mathcal{O}}^{\inf}(\mathfrak{g})^{k}

the full subcategories of
\mathcal{O}_{\Sigma}(\mathfrak{g})
,
{\mathcal{O}}^{\inf}(\mathfrak{g})

consisting of the modules of level
k
(i.e., the modules
annihilated by
(K-k)
).
Any module in
{\mathcal{O}}^{\inf}(\mathfrak{g})^{k}
has the natural structure of
V^{k}(\mathfrak{g})
-module.

We retain notation of
\lx@sectionsign\lx@sectionsign

5.1.8
and
5.4
.
Recall that
\mathfrak{g}_{\pi^{\#}}
is a subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}

generated by
\mathfrak{h}
and
\mathfrak{g}_{\pm\alpha}
for
\alpha\in\pi^{\#}
;
this is the direct product of
a subalgebra of
\mathfrak{h}
and
a non-twisted affine Kac-Moody algebra, see
\lx@sectionsign

6.1
, which we denote

\mathfrak{g}^{\#}
. The restriction of
(-,-)
to
\mathfrak{g}^{\#}

is the normalised non-degenerate invariant bilinear form. As in Lemma
6.4.5

we denote by
\rho^{\#}
and
(h^{\#})^{\vee}
the Weyl vector and
the dual Coxeter number of
\mathfrak{g}^{\#}
respectively.

By Lemma
6.4.5
any admissible level for
\mathfrak{g}
is admissible for
\mathfrak{g}^{\#}
.

7.2.
Anisotropic case

Arakawa’s Theorem states that for a non-twisted affine Kac-Moody algebra
\mathfrak{g}

and an admissible level
k
,
any
V_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
-module in the category
\mathcal{O}
is completely reducible and
irreducible modules in this category are
admissible
\mathfrak{g}
-modules
L(\lambda)
such that

\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}
is isometric to
\Delta_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho}
.

In
[
17
]
this assertion was extended to

\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{osp}(1|2n)^{(1)}
(which is
the only anisotropic non-twisted affine Kac-Moody superalgebra
with
\mathfrak{g}_{\overline{1}}\not=0
). It was also shown that any
V_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
-module
in the category
{\mathcal{O}}^{\inf}(\mathfrak{g})^{k}
is completely reducible (see
[
17
]
, Corollary 5.4.1).

7.3.
Main results

The main results of this section are the following theorems.

7.3.1.

Theorem.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be an indecomposable non-twisted affine Kac-Moody superalgebra, which is not
of type

D(2|1;a)^{(1)}
. Let
k
be an admissible level. Then a
V^{k}(\mathfrak{g})
-module
N

is a
V_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
-module if and only if

\operatorname{Res}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathfrak{g}^{\#}}N
is a
V_{k}(\mathfrak{g}^{\#})
-module (then
k
is
an admissible level for
\mathfrak{g}^{\#}
).

For the integral level
k
the above assertion is Theorem 5.3.1 in
[
16
]
.

7.3.2.

Theorem.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
and
k
be as in Theorem
7.3.1
, and let
N\in\mathcal{O}_{\Sigma}(\mathfrak{g})^{k}
be
an indecomposable
\mathfrak{g}
-module.
If
N
is admissible and

\Delta_{N}\cap\Delta^{\#}
is isometric to
\Delta_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho}\cap\Delta^{\#}
, then

N
is a
V_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
-module. The converse holds for
\mathfrak{g}\not=B(1|1)^{(1)}

and for
\mathfrak{g}=B(1|1)^{(1)}
if
k
is principal admissible.

7.3.3.
Remark

We do not know whether the last statement holds for
B(1|1)^{(1)}
for
subprincipal level
k
.

7.3.4.

Corollary.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
and
k
be as in Theorem
7.3.1
.
A module
N\in{\mathcal{O}}^{\inf}(\mathfrak{g})^{k}
is
a
V_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
-module if and only if all irreducible subquotients of
N

are
V_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
-modules.

Proof.

By Theorem
7.3.1
,
N
is a
V_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
-module if and only if

\operatorname{Res}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathfrak{g}^{\#}}N
is a
V_{k}(\mathfrak{g}^{\#})
-module.
If
N\in{\mathcal{O}}^{\inf}(\mathfrak{g})^{k}
, then
M:=\operatorname{Res}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathfrak{g}^{\#}}N

lies in
{\mathcal{O}}^{\inf}(\mathfrak{g}^{\#})^{k}
(here we have to use
\mathcal{O}^{\inf}

since the weight spaces
in
M
might be infinite-dimensional), so

M
is completely reducible (by Corollary 5.4.1 in
[
17
]
).
Thus
N\in{\mathcal{O}}^{\inf}(\mathfrak{g})^{k}
is
a
V_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
-module if and only if all irreducible subquotients of

M
are
V_{k}(\mathfrak{g}^{\#})
-modules. Using Theorem
7.3.1
again
we obtain the required statement.
∎

7.4.
Proof of Theorem
7.3.1

We denote by
V^{\#,k}
the vacuum module for
\mathfrak{g}^{\#}
and view it as a submodule
of
V^{k}
. We denote by
J
and by
J^{\#}
the maximal proper submodules of
V^{k}
and
V^{\#,k}

respectively (then

L(k\Lambda_{0})=V^{k}/J
and

L_{\mathfrak{g}^{\#}}(k\Lambda_{0})=V^{\#,k}/J^{\#}
).
A
V^{k}(\mathfrak{g})
-module
N

is a
V_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
-module if and only if
Y(v,z)N=0
for all
v\in J
.
Similarly, a
V^{k}(\mathfrak{g}^{\#})
-module
N

is a
V_{k}(\mathfrak{g}^{\#})
-module if and only if
Y(v,z)N=0
for all
v\in J^{\#}
.
Thus it is enough to verify that the submodule
J
is generated by
J^{\#}
,
which is established in the following lemma.

7.4.1.

Proposition.

If
k
is an admissible level, then
J
is generated by
J^{\#}
.

Proof.

Since
V^{\#,k}
is a submodule
of
\operatorname{Res}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathfrak{g}^{\#}}V^{k}
, the module
V^{\#,k}/(V^{\#,k}\cap J)

is a submodule of
\operatorname{Res}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathfrak{g}^{\#}}L(k\Lambda_{0})
. Since
k
is an admissible level,
L(k\Lambda_{0})
is admissible, so,
by Theorem
5.5.1
, any irreducible subquotient of
V^{\#,k}/(V^{\#,k}\cap J)

is quasi-admissible. By Proposition
6.5
(ii) this implies

V^{\#,k}/(V^{\#,k}\cap J)=L_{\mathfrak{g}^{\#}}(k\Lambda_{0})
. Thus

V^{\#,k}\cap J=J^{\#}
.

Denote by
J_{1}
the
\mathfrak{g}
-submodule of
V^{k}
generated by
J^{\#}
.
By above,
J_{1}\subset J
.
Let
V_{1}:=V^{k}(\mathfrak{g})/J_{1}
be the quotient vertex algebra. Then the
V_{1}
-modules are the
V^{k}(\mathfrak{g})
-modules which are annihilated by

Y(v,z)
for all
v\in J^{\#}
. Considering the adjoint representation of
V_{1}

we obtain
Y(v,z)V_{1}=0

for all
v\in J^{\#}
. Then

M:=\operatorname{Res}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathfrak{g}^{\#}}V_{1}

is a
V^{k}(\mathfrak{g}^{\#})
-module. By above,
Y(v,z)M=0
for all
v\in J^{\#}
, so

M
is a
V_{k}(\mathfrak{g}^{\#})
-module. Since
k
is a
\mathfrak{g}^{\#}
-admissible level
and
M\in\mathcal{O}_{\Sigma}(\mathfrak{g}^{\#})
,
Arakawa’s Theorem for
\mathfrak{g}^{\#}
implies that
M

is an admissible
\mathfrak{g}^{\#}
-module.

Let

L(\nu)
be an irreducible subquotient of
V^{k}/J_{1}
.
Let us verify that
L(\nu)
if
\pi^{\#}
-quasi-admissible.
By Theorem
5.5.1
it is enough to check that
any irreducible subquotient of
M_{1}:=\operatorname{Res}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathfrak{g}^{\#}}L(\nu)
is
a quasi-admissible
\mathfrak{g}^{\#}
-module and that
\Delta_{M_{1}}=\Delta_{\nu+\rho}\cap\Delta^{\#}
.
Since
M_{1}
is a subquotient of
M
, any irreducible subquotient of
M_{1}
is quasi-admissible.
By
\lx@sectionsign

5.6
we have

\Delta_{M_{1}}=R_{\nu+\rho}\cap\Delta^{\#}=R_{\lambda+\rho}\cap\Delta^{\#}=\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}\cap\Delta^{\#}

(the second equality follows from (
4.2
) and the last equality
follows from (
5.3
) and the fact that
L(\lambda)

is admissible ). By Lemma
4.6.3

we have
\Delta_{\nu+\rho}=\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}
. Therefore

\Delta_{M_{1}}=\Delta_{\nu+\rho}\cap\Delta^{\#}
.
Hence
L(\nu)
is quasi-admissible. By Proposition
6.5
(ii),

\nu=k\Lambda_{0}
. Hence
J_{1}=J
as required.
∎

7.5.
Zhu algebra of
V_{k}(\mathfrak{g})

Let
k
be an admissible level.
Since
k
is an admissible level for
\mathfrak{g}^{\#}
,

J^{\#}
is generated by a
\mathfrak{g}^{\#}
-singular vector
a|0\rangle
(where
a\in U(\mathfrak{n}^{\#-})
),
see
[
28
]
, Theorem 1, and the Zhu algebra for
V_{k}(\mathfrak{g}^{\#})
is
the quotient of

U(\dot{\mathfrak{g}}^{\#})
by the principal ideal

U(\dot{\mathfrak{g}}^{\#})F(a)

where
F
is the isomorphism given by the formula
(3.1.1) in
[
6
]
.
By Proposition
7.4.1
, the Zhu algebra for
V_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
is
the quotient of

U(\dot{\mathfrak{g}}^{\#})
by the principal ideal

U(\dot{\mathfrak{g}})F(a)
.

7.5.1.
Remark

By
[
28
]
, Theorem 1,
if
\dot{\mathfrak{g}}
is a Lie algebra, then

J
is
generated by a singular vector of weight

s_{\alpha}.k\Lambda_{0}
, where

\Sigma_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho}=\{\alpha\}\cup\dot{\Sigma}
.
If
k
is a principal
admissible level, then

\alpha=q\delta-\theta
, where
\theta

is the maximal root of
\dot{\mathfrak{g}}
, and
if
k
is a subprincipal
admissible level, then

\alpha=\frac{q}{u}\delta-\theta_{s}
, where

u
is the lacety and
\theta_{s}

is the maximal short (=non-long) root of
\dot{\mathfrak{g}}
).
By Proposition
7.4.1
, in general case,
if
k
is admissible, then
J
is generated by
a
\mathfrak{g}^{\#}
-singular vector of weight
s_{\alpha}(k\Lambda_{0}+\rho^{\#})-\rho^{\#}

where
\alpha\in{\Delta}^{\#}

is such that the root subsystem

\Delta^{\#}\cap\Delta_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho}
has the base

\dot{\Sigma}^{\#}\cup\{\alpha\}
. A natural question is whether

J
is
generated by a singular vector of weight

s_{\gamma}.k\Lambda_{0}

for certain choice of
\Sigma
and some
\gamma\in\Delta^{\#}\cap\Delta_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho}
?

The answer is positive if
q=1
.
In this case we choose
\Sigma
containing
\gamma:=\delta-\theta
,
where
\theta
is the maximal root of
\dot{\mathfrak{g}}^{\#}
.
Then
M(k\Lambda_{0})
contains a singular vector
v
of weight

s_{\gamma}.k\Lambda_{0}
and
M(k\Lambda_{0})/U(\mathfrak{g})v
is

\mathfrak{g}_{\pm\gamma}
-integrable. Let
\overline{v}
be the image
of
v
in
V^{k}
. Then
V^{k}/U(\mathfrak{g})\overline{v}
is a quotient of

M(k\Lambda_{0})/U(\mathfrak{g})v
, so
V^{k}/U(\mathfrak{g})\overline{v}
is

\mathfrak{g}_{\pm\gamma}
-integrable. Since
V^{k}
is

\dot{\mathfrak{g}}_{\overline{0}}
-integrable,
V^{k}/U(\mathfrak{g})\overline{v}
is
\mathfrak{g}^{\#}
-integrable. Using Proposition
6.5
(i)
we conclude that
V^{k}/U(\mathfrak{g})\overline{v}
is simple.

By
[
17
]
, Corollary 4.3, the answer is positive for

\mathfrak{osp}(1|2\ell)^{(1)}
. Using this corollary and the description of
\Sigma_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho}
given in
\lx@sectionsign

8.1
below we obtain
that for an admissible level

k=-h^{\vee}+\frac{p}{q}
one has

\gamma=q\delta-\theta
if
pq
is odd, and

\gamma=q\delta-\theta_{s}
if
pq
is even, where

\theta_{s}

is the maximal non-long root for
\mathfrak{osp}(1|2\ell)

(i.e.,
\theta_{s}=\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2}
for
\ell>1
and

\theta_{s}=\varepsilon_{1}
for
\ell=1
); in particular,

\gamma=\alpha
except for the case
\ell=1
with even
pq
.
In Section
11

we show that the answer is positive for
\mathfrak{sl}(2|1)^{(1)}

(in this case
\alpha=q\delta-\theta
).

7.6.
Proof of Theorem
7.3.2

Let
k
be an admissible level and
N\in\mathcal{O}_{\Sigma}(\mathfrak{g})^{k}

be an indecomposable module. Consider the properties

(a)

N
is admissible and
\Delta^{\#}\cap R_{\lambda+\rho}
is isometric to
\Delta^{\#}_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho}
;

(b)

\operatorname{Res}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathfrak{g}^{\#}}N
is a
V_{k}(\mathfrak{g}^{\#})
-module.

Our goal is to prove that (a) implies (b), and that (b) implies (a)
for all cases except for
B(1|1)^{(1)}
with a subprincipal level
k
.

7.6.1.

By Theorem
5.5.3
, (a) is equivalent to

(a’)

\operatorname{Res}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathfrak{g}^{\#}}N

is admissible,
\Delta^{\#}\cap R_{\lambda+\rho}=\Delta^{\#}\cap\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}
, and
\Delta^{\#}\cap\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}

is isometric to
\Delta^{\#}\cap\Delta_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho}
.

Let
L(\lambda)

be a subquotient of
N
. Then

\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}\cap\Delta^{\#}=\Delta_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho}\cap\Delta^{\#}
.
By
\lx@sectionsign

5.6
we have

\{\alpha\in\Delta^{\#}|\ \langle\nu,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle\in\mathbb{Z}\}=\Delta^{\#}\cap R_{\lambda+\rho}

for any
\nu
such that
L_{\mathfrak{g}^{\#}}(\nu)

is a subquotient of
\operatorname{Res}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathfrak{g}^{\#}}N
. Since
k
is a
\mathfrak{g}^{\#}
-admissible level,
Arakawa’s Theorem implies that (b) is equivalent to

(b’)

\operatorname{Res}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathfrak{g}^{\#}}N

is admissible
and
\Delta^{\#}\cap R_{\lambda+\rho}
is isometric to

\Delta^{\#}\cap\Delta_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho}
.

Since (a’) implies (b’),
(a) implies (b). It remains to show that (b’) implies (a’)
except for the case
\mathfrak{g}=B(1|1)^{(1)}
when
k
is subprincipal admissible.
It is enough to verify that in these cases
one has

(7.1)

(\Delta^{\#}\cap R_{\lambda+\rho})\text{ is isometric to }(\Delta^{\#}\cap\Delta_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho})\ \ \ \Longrightarrow\ \ \ \Delta^{\#}\cap R_{\lambda+\rho}=\Delta^{\#}\cap\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}

if
\lambda
is of the level
k
.

7.6.2.

Lemma.

If
\Delta^{\#}\cap R_{\lambda+\rho}
is isometric to
(\Delta^{\#})^{\operatorname{re}}
and
\Delta^{\#}
contains a short even root of
\Delta
, then (
7.1
) holds.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.1.4
(i),
\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}
and
R_{\lambda+\rho}

contain the same short even roots. Thus

(\Delta^{\#}\cap R_{\lambda+\rho})\subset(\Delta^{\#}\cap\Delta_{\lambda+\rho})
are two affine root systems,
which share the same short roots and the smaller one is non-twisted
(since
\Delta^{\#}\cap R_{\lambda+\rho}
is isometric to
(\Delta^{\#})^{\operatorname{re}}
).
Using the description of affine root systems
given in
[
26
]
, Proposition 6.3,
we conclude that these root systems coincide.
∎

7.6.3.

Let
k
be a principal admissible level. By Lemma
6.4.5
,

k
is principal admissible for
\mathfrak{g}^{\#}
, so
\Delta^{\#}\cap R_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho}

is isometric to
(\Delta^{\#})^{\operatorname{re}}
.
Thus the first assumption of the above lemma is satisfied.
From the classification of simple finite-dimensional
Kac-Moody superalgebras
[
24
]
, it follows that

\Delta^{\#}
contains a short even root of
\Delta

if
\mathfrak{g}\not=B(m|n)^{(1)}
with
n\geq m
.

By Corollary
6.4.4
, for
\mathfrak{g}\not=B(m|n)^{(1)}
any admissible level
k
is principal. Hence the above lemma establishes (
7.1
)
in all cases except for
B(m|n)^{(1)}
with
n\geq m
,
or
m>n
and
k
being subprincipal. This completes the proof of Theorem
7.3.2

for these cases. The remaining cases
will be treated in Section
8
(see
\lx@sectionsign

8.3
).

7.7.
Classification of admissible levels for
\mathfrak{g}\not=B(m|n)^{(1)}

We denote by
r^{\vee}
the lacety of
\mathfrak{g}^{\#}
, i.e.
the ratio of square lengths of a long root and of
a short root of

\Delta^{\#}
. (This number coincides with the dual tier number used in
[
29
]
).

Let
k
be an admissible level.
By Lemma
6.4.5
, we have
k+h^{\vee}\in\mathbb{Q}_{>0}
;
we write
k+h^{\vee}=\frac{p}{q}
where
p,q
are positive coprime integers.
By Lemma
6.3
(iv), for
L=L(k\Lambda_{0})
the
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
-module
L^{\prime}

is a simple vacuum module, i.e.
L^{\prime}=L_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}(k^{\prime}\Lambda^{\prime}_{0})
.

7.7.1.
Admissible levels for affine Kac-Moody algebras

This case is well-known
[
34
]
.
Indeed, we have
\Sigma=\dot{\Sigma}\cup\{\delta-\theta\}
where
(\theta,\theta)=2
.
If
\gcd(q,r^{\vee})=1
, then
\Sigma_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho}=\dot{\Sigma}\cup\{q\delta-\theta\}
. If

\gcd(q,r^{\vee})=r^{\vee}
, then
\Sigma_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho}=\dot{\Sigma}\cup\{q\delta-\theta^{\prime}\}

where
\theta^{\prime}
is the maximal short root; in this case

\Delta_{k\Lambda_{0}}
is the root system of a twisted affine Kac-Moody algebra
of type
X_{\ell}^{(r^{\vee})}
. As a result,
k
is principal admissible if and only if

\gcd(q,r^{\vee})=1
and
p\geq h^{\vee}
, and
k
is subprincipal admissible if and only if

\gcd(q,r^{\vee})\not=1
and
p
is greater or equal to the dual Coxeter number
of
X_{\ell}^{(r^{\vee})}
.

7.7.2.

Proposition.

If
\mathfrak{g}
is an indecomposable affine Kac-Moody superalgebra,
which is not of type
B(m|n)^{(1)}
, and is
not an affine Kac-Moody algebra, then

k
is admissible if and
only if
\gcd(q,r^{\vee})=1
and

p-h^{\vee}\geq 0
(then
k^{\prime}=p-h^{\vee}
).

Proof.

By Corollary
6.4.4
, for
\mathfrak{g}
in question any admissible level
k

is principal, so, by Lemma
6.4.5
,
k
is principal admissible for
\mathfrak{g}^{\#}
. By
\lx@sectionsign

7.7.1

this implies
\gcd(q,r^{\vee})=1
.
It is easy to see that
\gcd(q,r^{\vee})=1
implies

\Delta_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho}=\{\mathbb{Z}q\delta+\dot{\Delta}\}
.
Therefore
\psi(\delta^{\prime})=q\delta

(where
\psi
is as in
\lx@sectionsign

4.3
), so

k^{\prime}:=qk-(\rho^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})=p-h^{\vee},

since
\mathfrak{g}\cong\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
.
By Theorem
5.5.3
,
k\Lambda_{0}
is quasi-admissible if and only if

L(k^{\prime}\Lambda^{\prime}_{0})
is
\pi^{\#}
-integrable, that is
k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.
∎

The remaining case
B(m|n)^{(1)}
will be considered in the next section.

8.
Admissible levels for
B(m|n)^{(1)}

In this section we consider
\mathfrak{g}:=B(m|n)^{(1)}
.
One has
\mathfrak{g}^{\#}=B_{m}^{(1)}
if
m>n
and
\mathfrak{g}^{\#}=C_{n}^{(1)}

if
n\geq m
.

Recall that
k+h^{\vee}
is a positive rational number, if
k
is an admissible level,
see Lemma
6.4.5
(i). Fix a rational number
k
.
For
m>n

we write
k+h^{\vee}=\frac{p}{q}
where
p,q
are non-zero coprime integers and
q>0
;
for
n\geq m
we write
k+h^{\vee}=\frac{p}{2q}
where
p,q
are non-zero coprime integers and
q>0
.
For example, for
k\in\mathbb{Z}
we have
q=1
and
p=k+m-n-1
for
m>n
, and

p=2(k+n-m)+1
for
n\geq m
.

By Lemma
6.3
(iv), for
L=L(k\Lambda_{0})
we have

L^{\prime}=L_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}(k^{\prime}\Lambda_{0})

for some
k^{\prime}
.

In this section we will show that for
0\leq m\leq n
,
k
is principal admissible
if and only if
p,q
are odd and
\frac{p}{2}\geq h^{\vee}
; in this case,

\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}\cong\mathfrak{g}
and
k^{\prime}=\frac{p}{2}-h^{\vee}
.
For
m>n>0
,
k
is principal admissible
if and only if
q
is odd and
p\geq h^{\vee}
; in this case,

\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}\cong\mathfrak{g}
and
k^{\prime}=p-h^{\vee}
.

Furthermore, the subprincipal admissible levels exist only for the cases

m=0
when
p
or
q
is even,
m=n=1
when
p
is even, and
m>n

when
q
is even. In all these cases
k
is a subprincipal admissible level
if and only if
\frac{p}{2}\geq(h^{\prime})^{\vee}
and
p\not=0
, where

(h^{\prime})^{\vee}
is the dual Coxeter number for

\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
, which is of type
A(0|2n-1)^{(2)}
if
m=0
and of type
D(m+1|n)^{(2)}
if
m>0
.
In all these cases
k^{\prime}=\frac{p}{2}-(h^{\prime})^{\vee}
.

8.1.
Case
B(0|\ell)^{(1)}

In this case
\mathfrak{g}^{\#}=\mathfrak{g}_{\overline{0}}\cong C_{\ell}^{(1)}
.
We have
h^{\vee}=\ell+\frac{1}{2}
and
\Sigma=\dot{\Sigma}\cup\{\delta-\theta\}

where
\theta=2\delta_{1}
. Recall that
(\theta,\theta)=2
.

For
\alpha\in\dot{\Delta}

we have

(i\delta+\alpha)\in\overline{R}_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho}\ \ \Longleftrightarrow\ \ \left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}\frac{ip}{q}\in\mathbb{Z},&&(\alpha,\alpha)=\frac{1}{2},1\\
\frac{ip}{q}\in 2\mathbb{Z}+1,&&(\alpha,\alpha)=2,\ \ \text{and $i$ is odd}\end{array}\right.

(the condition
i
is odd in the last line reflects the fact that
i\delta+\alpha\not\in\overline{\Delta}
if
i
is even). This gives

\Sigma_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}\dot{\Sigma}\cup\{q\delta-\theta\}&&\text{ if $p,q$ are odd},\\
\dot{\Sigma}\cup\{q\delta-\theta_{s}\}&&\text{otherwise},\end{array}\right.

where
\theta_{s}
is the maximal among non-long roots:

\theta_{s}=\delta_{1}
for
\ell=1
and
\theta_{s}=\delta_{1}+\delta_{2}
for
\ell>1
.
Therefore
\psi(\delta^{\prime})=q\delta
, so

k^{\prime}=(\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})-(\rho^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})=(\lambda+\rho,q\delta)-(h^{\prime})^{\vee}=\frac{p}{2}-(h^{\prime})^{\vee},

where
(h^{\prime})^{\vee}
is the dual Coxeter number of
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
.
This gives

\bullet

If
pq
is odd, then
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
is of type
B(0|\ell)^{(1)}
and

k^{\prime}=\frac{p}{2}-h^{\vee}
.

\bullet

If
pq
is even, then
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
is of type
A(0|2\ell-1)^{(2)}

and
k^{\prime}=\frac{p}{2}-(h^{\prime})^{\vee}
with
(h^{\prime})^{\vee}=\ell-\frac{1}{2}

(note that
A(0|1)^{(2)}=D(1|1)^{(2)}
since
\mathfrak{sl}(1|2)=\mathfrak{osp}(2|2)
).

By Theorem
5.5.3
,
k
is admissible if and only if
L_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}(k^{\prime}\Lambda_{0})

is integrable. If
pq
is odd, then
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}\cong\mathfrak{g}
, so
L_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}(k^{\prime}\Lambda_{0})

is integrable if and only if
k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. If
pq
is even,
then
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
is of type
A(0|2\ell-1)^{(2)}
with the normalised invariant bilinear form
(the square length of the long root is
2
). The set of simple roots for
A(0|1)^{(2)}

is
\{\delta-\delta_{1},\delta_{1}\}
(both roots are odd) and
L_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}(k^{\prime}\Lambda_{0})

is integrable if and only if
2k^{\prime}=p-2(h^{\prime})^{\vee}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
i.e.

p\geq 2(h^{\prime})^{\vee}=1
. For
\ell>1
the
set of simple roots for
A(0|2\ell-1)^{(2)}

is
\{\delta-\delta_{1}-\delta_{2},\delta_{1}-\delta_{2},\ldots,\delta_{n}\}
(the last root is odd) and
L_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}(k^{\prime}\Lambda_{0})

is integrable if and only if
k^{\prime}=\frac{p}{2}-(h^{\prime})^{\vee}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}

which means that
p
is odd and
p\geq 2(h^{\prime})^{\vee}
.
We conclude that

\bullet

k
is principal admissible if and only if

p,q
are odd
and
p\geq 2h^{\vee}=2\ell+1
;

\bullet

k
is subprincipal admissible if and only if
p\geq 2\ell-1
,
and, in addition,
pq
is even for
\ell=1
, and
q
is even for
\ell>1
;
if
k
subprincipal admissible, then
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}\cong A(0|2\ell-1)^{(2)}
.

8.1.1.

Let
\lambda
be an admissible weight of
B(0|\ell)^{(1)}
satisfying

\operatorname{cl}(\Delta_{\lambda+\rho})=\dot{\Delta}_{\overline{0}}=C_{\ell}
. Retain notation of
\lx@sectionsign

4.5.1
.
By
[
25
]
,
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
is one of the following affine Kac-Moody superalgebras:

C_{\ell}^{(1)}
,
A_{2\ell-1}^{(2)}
,
B(0|\ell)^{(1)}
,
A(0|2\ell-1)^{(2)}
,
or
D(1|\ell)^{(2)}=C(\ell+1)^{(2)}

(notice that
D(1|1)^{(2)}=C(2)^{(2)}=A(0|1)^{(2)}
).

Recall that
\alpha\in\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}
is even if
(\alpha,\alpha)=1,2
,
and is odd if
(\alpha,\alpha)=1/2
. If
(\alpha,\alpha)=1,1/2
,
then
\alpha\in\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}
is equivalent to
\alpha\in\overline{R}_{\lambda+\rho}
,
so
\alpha,\alpha+j\delta\in\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}
is equivalent to
\frac{jp}{q}\in\mathbb{Z}
.
This gives

(8.1)

(\alpha+\mathbb{Z}\delta)\cap\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}=\alpha+\mathbb{Z}q\delta\ \text{ if }\alpha\in\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}\ \text{ is s.t. }(\alpha,\alpha)=1,1/2.

Since
\operatorname{cl}(\Delta_{\lambda+\rho})=\dot{\Delta}_{\overline{0}}\
,

\operatorname{cl}(\Delta(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})_{\overline{0}})=C_{\ell}
and the square length of the longest root
in
\Delta(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})
is
2
.
Let
\delta^{\prime}
be the minimal imaginary root of
\Delta(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})
.
Recall that
\psi(\delta^{\prime})=u\delta
for some
u\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
.
By
\lx@sectionsign

3.2.6
if

\beta
is a short root of
\Delta(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})
, then
(\beta+j\delta^{\prime})\in\Delta(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})

is equivalent to
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, so

\{\psi(\beta)+\mathbb{Z}\delta\}\cap\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}=\psi(\beta)+\mathbb{Z}\psi(\delta^{\prime}).

If
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}\not\cong C_{1}^{(1)}
, then
(\beta,\beta)\not=2
, so (
8.1
) implies

\psi(\delta^{\prime})=q\delta
. Hence

(8.2)

\psi(\delta^{\prime})=q\delta\ \text{ for }\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}\not\cong C_{1}^{(1)}.

Therefore
(\lambda^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})=(\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})-(h^{\prime})^{\vee}=(\lambda+\rho,\delta)-(h^{\prime})^{\vee}
where
(h^{\prime})^{\vee}
is the dual Coxeter number of
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
.
Hence

(8.3)

(\lambda^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})=\frac{p}{2}-(h^{\prime})^{\vee}\text{ for }\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}\not\cong C_{1}^{(1)}.

If
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}\cong C_{1}^{(1)}
, then
\ell=1
. For

\alpha\in\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}
one has
(\alpha,\alpha)=2

and
\alpha/2\not\in\Delta
, so
(\lambda+\rho,\alpha)\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Therefore
(\alpha+j\delta)\in\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}
is equivalent to

s(k+h^{\vee})\in\mathbb{Z}
that is
s\in 2\mathbb{Z}q
. Therefore

(8.4)

\psi(\delta^{\prime})=2q\delta,\ \ (\lambda^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})=p-2\ \text{ for }\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}\cong C_{1}^{(1)}.

Let us show that
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}\not\cong D(1|\ell)^{(2)}
for
\ell>1
. Suppose the contrary.
Then
\Delta(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})_{\overline{0}}
contains
\alpha
such that
(\alpha,\alpha)=1

and
\alpha+\delta^{\prime}\not\in\Delta(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})
. Then
\psi(\alpha)\in\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}

and
\psi(\alpha+\delta^{\prime})=\psi(\alpha)+q\delta\not\in\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}

which contradicts to (
8.1
). Hence

(8.5)

\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}\cong C_{\ell}^{(1)},\ B(0|\ell)^{(1)},\ A_{2\ell-1}^{(2)},\text{or }A(0|2\ell-1)^{(2)}.

8.1.2.

Corollary.

Let
\mathfrak{g}=B(0|\ell)^{(1)}
,

k
be an admissible level, and
L(\lambda)
be a
V_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
-module.

(i)

If
k
is principal admissible, then

\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}\cong\Delta_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho}
and
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}\cong B(0|\ell)^{(1)}
.
In this case
L_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}(\lambda^{\prime})
has level
\frac{p}{2}-h^{\vee}
.

(ii)

Let
k
be a subprincipal admissible level. Then
either
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}\cong A(0|2\ell-1)^{(2)}
,
or
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}\cong A_{2\ell-1}^{(2)}
for
\ell>1
,
or
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}\cong C_{1}^{(1)}
for
\ell=1
.
If
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}\not\cong C_{1}^{(1)}
, then

L_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}(\lambda^{\prime})
has level
\frac{p}{2}-(h^{\prime})^{\vee}

where
(h^{\prime})^{\vee}
is
the dual Coxeter number of
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
.
If
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}\cong C_{1}^{(1)}
, then
L_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}(\lambda^{\prime})
has level

p-2
.

Proof.

By Arakawa’s Theorem (established in
[
17
]
for this case),

\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}\cap\Delta_{\overline{0}}
is isometric to

\Delta_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho}\cap\Delta_{\overline{0}}
.

Consider the case
\ell>1
and

q
is even. Then, by
\lx@sectionsign

8.1
,
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{\overline{0}}
is of type
A_{2\ell-1}^{(2)}
,
and, by
[
25
]
,
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
is
A(0|2\ell-1)^{(2)}

or
A_{2\ell-1}^{(2)}
.

Consider the case when
\ell>1
and
q
is odd.
By (
8.2
),

\psi
maps the minimal imaginary root of
\Delta^{\prime}_{\overline{0}}\cong C_{\ell}^{(1)}
to
q\delta
.
Hence

(\gamma+\mathbb{Z}\delta)\cap\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}=\gamma+\mathbb{Z}q\delta\ \text{ for all }\gamma\in\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}\cap\Delta_{\overline{0}}.

Take
\gamma\in\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}
such that
(\gamma,\gamma)=2
,
that is
\gamma=s\delta\pm 2\delta_{i}
. By above,
(s+q)\delta\pm 2\delta_{i}

lies in
\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}
. Since
s
or
s+q
is even,

\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}
contains an element of the form
2\beta

where
\beta
is odd. Thus
\beta\in\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}
, so
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
contains an odd root.
By
\lx@sectionsign

8.1
,
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{\overline{0}}
is of type
C_{\ell}^{(1)}
.
Hence, by
[
25
]
,
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}\cong B(0|\ell)^{(1)}
.

Now consider the case
\ell=1
. Then
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{\overline{0}}\cong C_{1}^{(1)}
.
We have the following possibilities

(a)

\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}\cong B(0|1)^{(1)}
with

\Sigma_{\lambda}=\{s_{1}\delta\pm 2\delta_{1},s_{2}\delta\mp\delta_{1}\}
, where
s_{1}
is odd;

(b)

\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}\cong A(0|1)^{(2)}
with

\Sigma_{\lambda}=\{s_{1}\delta-\delta_{1},s_{2}\delta+\delta_{1}\}
;

(c)

\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}\cong C_{1}^{(1)}

with
\Sigma_{\lambda}=\{s_{1}\delta-2\delta_{1},s_{2}\delta+2\delta_{1}\}
, where
s_{1},s_{2}
are odd.

If
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}\cong B(0|1)^{(1)}
, then

(\lambda+\rho,s_{1}\delta\pm 2\delta_{1})\in\mathbb{Z}

and
4(\lambda+\rho,s_{2}\delta\mp\delta_{1})\in 2\mathbb{Z}+1
, so

\frac{(s_{1}+2s_{2})p}{q}\in 2\mathbb{Z}+1
.
Thus
p,q
are odd.

If
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}\cong A(0|1)^{(2)}
, then

(s\delta+\delta_{1})\in\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}
for some
s

and
((2s+2i+1)\delta+2\delta_{1})\not\in\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}

for all
i
. Thus
\frac{p(2i+1)}{q}\not\in 2\mathbb{Z}+1
for all
i
, so
pq

is even.

If
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}\cong C_{1}^{(1)}
, then

(s\delta+2\delta_{1})\in\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}
for some odd
s

and
(i\delta+\delta_{1})\not\in\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}

for all
i
. This implies
\frac{jp}{q}\not\in 2\mathbb{Z}+1

for all odd
j
, so
pq
is even.

The rest of the assertions follow from (
8.4
)
and (
8.3
).
∎

8.2.
Admissible levels for
\mathfrak{g}:=B(m|n)^{(1)}
with
m,n\geq 1

Let
k
be as in the beginning of Section
8
.
By Lemma
6.3
(ii),
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
is either
B(m|n)^{(1)}

or
D(m+1|n)^{(2)}
. Thus, if
k
is admissible, then

k
is principal if
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
is
B(m|n)^{(1)}
, and
is subprincipal if
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
is
D(m+1|n)^{(2)}
.

We denote by
\delta^{\prime}
the minimal imaginary root of
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
; recall that

\psi(\delta^{\prime})=u\delta
for some
u\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
. One has

(k^{\prime}\Lambda^{\prime}_{0}+\rho^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})=(k\Lambda_{0}+\rho,\psi(\delta))
, so

k^{\prime}=\frac{up}{2q}-(h^{\prime})^{\vee}\text{ if }n\geq m,\ \ \ \ k^{\prime}=\frac{up}{q}-(h^{\prime})^{\vee}\text{ if }n<m,

where
(h^{\prime})^{\vee}:=(\rho^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})
is the dual Coxeter number of
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
.

Note that the lacety
r^{\vee}
of
\mathfrak{g}^{\#}
is equal to
1
if
m=n=1

and is equal to
2
otherwise.

8.2.1.

By Lemma
6.3
(iii), the bilinear form on
\Delta^{\prime}

is the normalised invariant form.
If
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}\cong B(m|n)^{(1)}
, then
L_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}(k^{\prime}\Lambda_{0})

is
(\pi^{\prime})^{\#}
-integrable if and only if
k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.
If
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}\cong D(m+1|n)^{(2)}
, then
L_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}(k^{\prime}\Lambda_{0})

is
(\pi^{\prime})^{\#}
-integrable if and only if
2k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.

8.2.2.

Proposition.

Let
\mathfrak{g}=B(m|n)^{(1)}
with
m,n\geq 1
. Recall that
h^{\vee}=n-m+\frac{1}{2}
,
(h^{\prime})^{\vee}=\frac{n-m}{2}
if

n\geq m
, and
h^{\vee}=2m-2n-1
,
(h^{\prime})^{\vee}=m-n
if
m>n
.

(i)

Let
m=n=1
.
If
q
is even, then
k
is not admissible.
If
p,q
are odd, then
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}\cong B(1|1)^{(1)}

and
k^{\prime}=\frac{p}{2}-h^{\vee}
, so
k+h^{\vee}=\frac{k^{\prime}+h^{\vee}}{q}
.
If
p
is even, then
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}\cong D(2|1)^{(2)}

and
k^{\prime}=\frac{p}{2}-(h^{\prime})^{\vee}=\frac{p}{2}
, so
k+h^{\vee}=\frac{k^{\prime}}{q}
.
In both cases

k
is admissible if and only if
k^{\prime}\geq 0
.

(ii)

Let
1\leq n<m
.
If
q
is odd, then
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}\cong B(m|n)^{(1)}
, and

k
is admissible (and principal) if and only if

k^{\prime}=p-h^{\vee}\geq 0
.
If
q
is even, then
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}\cong D(m+1|n)^{(2)}
, and

k
is admissible (and subprincipal) if and only if

k^{\prime}=\frac{p}{2}-(h^{\prime})^{\vee}\geq 0

(notice that
k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}
).

(iii)

If
1\leq m\leq n\not=1
, then
k
is not admissible except for the case when

p
and
q
are odd. If
p,q
are odd, then
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}\cong B(m|n)^{(1)}
,
k^{\prime}=\frac{p}{2}-h^{\vee}
,
and

k
is admissible
(and principal) if and only if
k^{\prime}\geq 0
. In this case
k+h^{\vee}=\frac{k^{\prime}+h^{\vee}}{q}
.

8.2.3.

Corollary.

(i)

The level
k
is principal admissible for
\mathfrak{g}=B(m|n)^{(1)}
with
m\geq 0
,
n\geq 1

if and only if
p,q
are odd and
\frac{p}{2}\geq h^{\vee}

for
m\leq n
, and
q
is odd and
p\geq h^{\vee}

for
m>n
.

(ii)

The level
k
is subprincipal admissible for
\mathfrak{g}=B(m|n)^{(1)}
with
m\geq 0
,
n\geq 1

in the following cases:
\frac{p}{2}\geq(h^{\prime})^{\vee}
,
p\not=0
and

\cdot

m=0
,
n=1
when
pq
is even;

\cdot

m>n\geq 1
or
m=0
,
n>1
when

q
is even;

\cdot

m=n=1
when

p
is even for
m=n=1
.

One has
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}=D(m+1|n)^{(2)}
(so

(h^{\prime})^{\vee}=n-\frac{1}{2}

for
m=0
,
(h^{\prime})^{\vee}=m-n
for
m>n
and
m=n=1
).

8.2.4.
Proof of Proposition
8.2.2

We retain the usual notation for the roots in
B(m|n)
.
By abuse of notation, we use the same notation for the set of real roots of affine Kac-Moody superalgebra

\mathfrak{g}
and
\mathfrak{g}
itself (for example,
\Delta_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho}\cong B(m|n)^{(1)}
means
that
\Delta_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho}
is isomorphic to the set of real roots of
B(m|n)^{(1)}
).
It is convenient to use the normalization
(\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{1})=1

(which coincides with the original normalization if
m>n
). Then

(k\Lambda_{0}+\rho,\delta)=\frac{p_{1}}{q}
where
p_{1}=p
for
m>n

and
p_{1}=-p
if
n\geq m
.

By Theorem
5.5.3
, if
k
is admissible, then it satisfies

(8.6)

\Delta_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho}\cap\Delta^{\#}\subset{R}_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho}.

In the light of Theorem
5.5.3
and
\lx@sectionsign

8.2.1
,
it is enough to verify that for

u:=\frac{q}{\gcd(2,q)}
(i.e.,
u=q
is
q
is odd and
u=q/2

otherwise) we have:

(a)

if
n\geq m
and
p,q
are odd, or
m>n

and
q
is odd, then (
8.6
) holds and

(8.7)

\Delta_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho}=\dot{\Delta}+\mathbb{Z}u\delta\cong B(m|n)^{(1)},

(b)

if
m=n=1
and
p
is even, or
m>n

and
q
is even, then (
8.6
) holds and

(8.8)

\Delta_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho}=\{\dot{\Delta}+2\mathbb{Z}u\}\cup\{\alpha+\mathbb{Z}u\delta|\ \alpha\in\dot{\Delta},\ (\alpha,\alpha)=\pm 1\}\cong D(m+1|n)^{(2)},

(c)

formula (
8.6
)
does not hold in all other cases, i.e. for
m=n=1
if
q
is even,
and for
n\geq m
,
n\not=1
if
p
or
q
are even.

For
\alpha\in\dot{\Delta}

we have

(\alpha+i\delta)\in\overline{R}_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho}\ \ \Longrightarrow\ \ \left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}i=0&&\text{ if }(\alpha,\alpha)=0\\
\frac{pi}{q}\in\mathbb{Z}&&\text{ if }(\alpha,\alpha)=\pm 2,-1\\
\frac{2pi}{q}\in\mathbb{Z}&&\text{ if }(\alpha,\alpha)=1,\\
i,\frac{pi}{q}\in 2\mathbb{Z}+1&&\text{ if }(\alpha,\alpha)=-4.\end{array}\right.

If
p,q
are odd, then

\Delta_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho}=\dot{\Delta}+\mathbb{Z}q\delta\cong B(m|n)^{(1)}

and
\Delta_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho}\cap\overline{\Delta}^{\operatorname{an}}=\overline{R}_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho}
, so (
8.6
)
holds. This proves (a).

In our normalization the short even roots (i.e.,
s\delta\pm\varepsilon_{i}
)
are of square length
1
.
By Lemma
4.1.4
(i), the root

si\delta\pm\varepsilon_{i})
lies in
\Delta_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho}

if and only if it lies in
\overline{R}_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho}
, that is

(s\delta\pm\varepsilon_{i})\in\Delta_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho}
if and only if
2s
is divisible by

q
.
Using Lemma
6.3
(ii), we conclude that
\Delta_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho}

satisfies one of the formulas (
8.7
), (
8.8
) for

u=\frac{q}{\gcd(2,q)}
.

If
q
is even, then the set given by formula (
8.8
) contains

\overline{R}_{k\Lambda+\rho}
, so
\Delta_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho}
is
as in (
8.8
) with
u=\frac{q}{2}
.
In this case
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}

is of type
D(m+1|n)^{(2)}
. For
m>n
formula (
8.6
)
holds.
If
n\geq m
, then
(q\delta+2\delta_{1})\in\Delta_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho}\setminus R_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho}
,
so (
8.6
)
does not hold.

Now consider the remaining case when
p
is even (and
q
is odd).
For
B(1|1)
we have

\Delta_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho}=\{2\mathbb{Z}q\delta\pm\varepsilon_{1}\pm\delta_{1},\mathbb{Z}q\delta\pm\varepsilon_{1},\mathbb{Z}q\delta\pm\delta_{1},2\mathbb{Z}q\delta\pm 2\delta_{1}\}\cong D(2|1)^{(2)},

so
\Delta_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho}\cap\Delta^{\#}=\{2\mathbb{Z}q\delta\pm 2\delta_{1}\}={R}_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho}\cap\Delta^{\#}
and (
8.6
)
holds.

For
(m,n)\not=(1,1)
the set

\dot{\Delta}

contains
\alpha
such that
(\alpha,\alpha)\in\{\pm 2\}
and

\alpha+q\delta\in\overline{R}_{\lambda+\rho}
. Thus

\Delta_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho}\cong B(m|n)^{(1)}
is given by formula (
8.7
).
If
m>n
,

{R}_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho}\cap\Delta^{\#}=\{\mathbb{Z}q\delta\pm\dot{\Delta}^{\#}\}=\Delta_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho}\cap\Delta^{\#},

so (
8.6
)
holds.
If
n\geq m
, then
q\delta+2\delta_{1}\in\Delta_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho}\setminus{R}_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho}
, so (
8.6
)
does not hold. This completes the proof.
∎

8.3.
Completion of the proof of Theorem
7.3.2

It remains to verify (
7.1
) for

B(m|n)^{(1)}
with
n\geq m
and
k
being prinicpal admissible,
and for
m>n
and
k
being subprincipal admissible.
The first case is treated in the following lemma.

8.3.1.

Lemma.

Let
\mathfrak{g}=B(m|n)^{(1)}
with
n\geq m
and
k
such that
k+h^{\vee}=\frac{p}{2q}

where
p,q
are odd. Let
\lambda
be a weight of level
k

such that
R_{\lambda+\rho}\cap\Delta^{\#}
is
isometric to
(\Delta^{\#})^{\operatorname{re}}
. Then

\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}\cap\Delta^{\#}=R_{\lambda+\rho}\cap\Delta^{\#}
.

Proof.

We will use the normalization
(\delta_{1},\delta_{1})=1
.
For this normalization

(k\Lambda_{0}+\rho,\delta)=\frac{p}{q}

and all roots are of integral square length, so

2(\lambda+\rho,\gamma)
is integral for all
\gamma\in\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}
.

We will use the following observation:

(s\delta+2\delta_{i})\in R_{\lambda+\rho}
if and only if

(\lambda+\rho,s\delta+2\delta_{i})

is odd if
s
is even, and
(\lambda+\rho,s\delta+2\delta_{i})

is even if
s
is odd.

The assumption that

R_{\lambda+\rho}\cap\Delta^{\#}
is isometric to
(\Delta^{\#})^{\operatorname{re}}

implies that for each
i=1,\ldots,n

there exists
s
such that
(s\delta+2\delta_{i})\in R_{\lambda+\rho}
.
If
s
is odd, then the above observation gives

(s\delta+2\delta_{i})\in R_{\lambda+\rho}\ \ \Longleftrightarrow\ \ (\lambda+\rho,s\delta+2\delta_{i})\in 2\mathbb{Z}\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \ (\lambda+\rho,(s+q)\delta+2\delta_{i})\in 2\mathbb{Z}+1

since
p
is odd. Thus
(s\delta+2\delta_{i})\in R_{\lambda+\rho}

for some even
s
. Therefore
for each
i=1,\ldots,n
there exists

p_{i}
such that
(2p_{i}\delta+2\delta_{i})\in R_{\lambda+\rho}
,
which means that

\beta_{i}:=p_{i}\delta+\delta_{i}
lies in
\overline{R}_{\lambda+\rho}
, that is

a_{i}:=(\lambda+\rho,\beta_{i})\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}.

Take
\gamma\in(\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}\cap\Delta^{\#})
and set

b:=(\lambda+\rho,\gamma)
.
Since
\gamma\in\Delta^{\#}
we have
(\gamma,\gamma)
is
2
or
4
, and

\gamma=s\delta\pm\beta_{i}\pm\beta_{j}

for some
s\in\mathbb{Z}
,
1\leq i,j\leq n
, so

b=(\pm a_{i}\pm a_{j})+\frac{sp}{q}.

Since
\gamma\in\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}
, we have
2b\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Since
(\pm a_{i}\pm a_{j})
is integral,
\frac{2sp}{q}
is integral, so

s
is divisible by
q
(since
q
is odd). Therefore

b\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Hence
\gamma\in R_{\lambda+\rho}
if
(\gamma,\gamma)=2
.
If
(\gamma,\gamma)=4
, then

i=j
and

b=\pm 2a_{i}+\frac{sp}{q}
. Thus
b

is even if
s
is odd and is odd if
s

is even (since
p
is odd). Using the above observation we obtain

\gamma\in R_{\lambda+\rho}
as required.
∎

8.3.2.

It remains to verify (
7.1
) for

B(m|n)^{(1)}
with
m>n
and
k
being subprincipal admissible.
In the light of Proposition
8.2.2

this corresponds to the case when

q
is even.

8.3.3.

Set

P:=\{\nu\in\mathbb{C}\dot{\Delta}|\ (\nu,\alpha)\in\mathbb{Z}\ \ \text{ for all }\alpha\in\dot{\Delta}\}
.
We consider the map
t:P\to GL(\mathfrak{h}^{*})

given by

(8.9)

t_{\nu}(\mu):=\mu+(\mu,\delta)\nu-((\mu,\nu)+\frac{(\mu,\delta)}{2}(\nu,\nu))\delta.

The map

t
is a group monomorphism; we denote by
\tilde{T}
the image
of this map. Then
\tilde{T}\in O(\mathfrak{h}^{*})
(i.e.,
t_{\nu}

preserves the bilinear form
(-,-)
on
\mathfrak{h}^{*}
). Note that for
\gamma\in\Delta
we have

t_{\nu}(\gamma)=\gamma-(\gamma,\nu)\delta.

In particular,
t_{\nu}(\delta)=\delta
and
and
t_{\nu}(\Delta)=\Delta
. We denote by
\tilde{W}
the subgroup of

O(\mathfrak{h}^{*})
generated by
\dot{W}
and
\tilde{T}

(by
[
26
]
, Proposition 6.5,
\tilde{W}=\dot{W}\ltimes\tilde{T}
, but we won’t use this fact). For each
w\in\tilde{W}
we have

(8.10)

w\Delta=\Delta,\ \ w\delta=\delta,\ \ R_{w(\lambda+\rho)}=wR_{\lambda+\rho},\ \ \ \Delta_{w(\lambda+\rho)}=w\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}\ \text{ for }\lambda\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}.

8.3.4.

Recall that
(\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{1})=1
for
m>n
.
For this normalization
P=\mathbb{Z}\Delta
,
and all roots are of integral square length, so

2(\lambda+\rho,\gamma)
is integral for all
\gamma\in\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}
.

8.3.5.

We want to prove that for
m>n
we have

\Delta^{\#}\cap R_{\lambda+\rho}\ \text{ is isometric to }\ \Delta^{\#}\cap\Delta_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho}\ \ \Longrightarrow\ \ \Delta^{\#}\cap R_{\lambda+\rho}=\Delta^{\#}\cap\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}

if
\lambda
is of level
k
, where
k+h^{\vee}=\frac{p}{q}
and
q
is even.
Using (
8.10
) we conclude that
the above implication for
\lambda
is equivalent to the similar implication for
the weight
w(\lambda+\rho)-\rho
for
w\in\tilde{W}
.

8.3.6.

We set

\begin{array}[]{l}I_{\varepsilon}:=\{i|\ \varepsilon_{i}\in\operatorname{cl}(R_{\lambda+\rho})\}=\{i|\ \exists s\in\mathbb{Z}\ \text{ s. t.}\ 2(\lambda+\rho,s\delta+\varepsilon_{i})\in\mathbb{Z}\},\\
I_{\delta}:=\{j|\ \delta_{j}\in\operatorname{cl}(R_{\lambda+\rho})\}=\{j|\ \exists s\in\mathbb{Z}\ \text{ s. t.}\ 2(\lambda+\rho,s\delta+\delta_{j})\in 2\mathbb{Z}+1\}.\end{array}

The assumption that

\Delta^{\#}\cap R_{\lambda+\rho}
is isometric to
\Delta^{\#}\cap\Delta_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho}

implies
\dot{\Delta}^{\#}\subset\operatorname{cl}(R_{\lambda+\rho})
,
so
I_{\varepsilon}=\{1,\ldots,m\}
.

Since
2(\lambda,\alpha+\frac{q}{2}\delta)=2(\lambda,\alpha)+p
and
p
is odd
we have

\begin{array}[]{l}I_{\varepsilon}=\{i|\ \exists s\in\mathbb{Z}\ \text{ s. t.}\ 2(\lambda+\rho,s\delta+\varepsilon_{i})\in 2\mathbb{Z}+1\}\\
I_{\delta}=\{j|\ \exists s\in\mathbb{Z}\ \text{ s. t.}\ 2(\lambda+\rho,s\delta+\delta_{j})\in\mathbb{Z}\}.\end{array}

8.3.7.

If
R_{\lambda+\rho}
does not contain isotropic roots, then

R_{\lambda+\rho}=\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}
. Thus we may (and will)
assume that
R_{\lambda+\rho}

contains an isotropic root, that is
(\lambda+\rho,s\delta+\varepsilon_{i}-\delta_{j})=0

for some
s,i,j
. Then
\delta_{j}\in I_{\delta}
. Acting by a suitable element of
\dot{W}

we can assume that
I_{\delta}=\{1,\ldots,n_{1}\}
for some
n_{1}
such that
1\leq n_{1}\leq n
and that

(8.11)

\begin{array}[]{l}a_{i}:=(\lambda+\rho,\varepsilon_{i})\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}\ \ \text{ for }i=1,\ldots,m;\\
b_{j}:=(\lambda+\rho,\delta_{j})\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}\ \ \text{ for }j=1,\ldots,n_{1};\\
a_{n}=b_{1}.\end{array}

We let
B(m|n_{1})^{(1)}
be
the corresponding natural copy in

\Delta(\mathfrak{g})
.
The intersection of
\Delta_{\overline{0}}^{\operatorname{re}}

with the span of
\delta
and
\{\delta_{j}\}_{j>n_{1}}

is isomorphic to
C_{n-n_{1}}^{(1)}

and we denote this intersection by
C_{n-n_{1}}^{(1)}
.

For
j>n_{1}
we have
j\not\in I_{\delta}
, so

R_{\lambda+\rho}

does not contain the roots of the form
s\delta\pm\delta_{j}
,

s\delta\pm\varepsilon_{i}\pm\delta_{j}
and

s\delta\pm\delta_{r}\pm\delta_{j}
for
i=1,\ldots,m
and
r=1,\ldots,n_{1}
.
This means that
R_{\lambda+\rho}
lies in
B(m|n_{1})^{(1)}\coprod C_{n-n_{1}}^{(1)}
.
Hence

\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}\subset\bigl(B(m|n_{1})^{(1)}\coprod C_{n-n_{1}}^{(1)}\bigr).

The set
\Delta^{\#}\cap\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}
lies

\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}\cap B(m|n_{1})^{(1)}
.
Therefore we have to verify

(8.12)

\bigl(\Delta^{\#}\cap(\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}\cap B(m|n_{1})^{(1)})\bigr)\subset\bigl(R_{\lambda+\rho}\cap B(m|n_{1})^{(1)}\bigr).

Note that

\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}\cap B(m|n_{1})^{(1)}

is the minimal subset of
B(m|n_{1})^{(1)}
which contains the set

R_{\lambda+\rho}\cap B(m|n_{1})^{(1)}
and satisfies properties (a)–(c)
of
\lx@sectionsign

4.1.1
.

Let us compute the set
R_{\lambda+\rho}\cap B(m|n_{1})^{(1)}
.
For a non-isotropic
\alpha\in B(m|n_{1})
we have

(8.13)

(s\delta+\alpha)\in\overline{R}_{\lambda+\rho}\ \ \Longleftrightarrow\ \ \left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}q|s&\text{ if }(\alpha,\alpha)=-1,\pm 2\\
\frac{q}{2}|s&\text{ if }(\alpha,\alpha)=1\\
\end{array}\right.

(The roots
s\delta\pm 2\delta_{1}
do not lie in
\overline{R}_{\lambda+\rho}
,
since
\frac{ps}{q}\pm 2b_{j}\not\in 2\mathbb{Z}
for odd
s
).
If
s\delta-\varepsilon_{i}\pm\delta_{j}
lies in
\overline{R}_{\lambda+\rho}
,
then
\frac{ps}{q}-a_{i}\pm b_{j}=0
, so
s=\frac{q(a_{i}\mp b_{j})}{p}
is divisible by
q
.
We conclude that
R_{\lambda+\rho}\cap B(m|n_{1})^{(1)}
lies in the set

\{\alpha+q\delta|\ \alpha\in B(m|n_{1})\}\cup\{\alpha+\frac{q}{2}\delta|\ \alpha\in B(m|n_{1}),\ (\alpha,\alpha)=\pm 1\}\cong D(m+1|n_{1})^{(2)}.

Hence
\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}\cap B(m|n_{1})^{(1)}
lies the copy

D(m+1|n_{1})^{(2)}
given by the formula above (in fact,

\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}\cap B(m|n_{1})^{(1)}
coincides with this set).
Using (
8.13
) we obtain (
8.12
) and complete the proof.

8.4.
Example:
B(1|1)^{(1)}
when
p
is even

Assume that

\Delta^{\#}\cap R_{\lambda+\rho}\ \text{ is isometric to }\ \Delta^{\#}\cap R_{k\Lambda_{0}+\rho}\ \ \text{ and }\ \ (\Delta^{\#}\cap\Delta_{\lambda+\rho})\not\subset R_{\lambda+\rho}.

Then
R_{\lambda+\rho}
contains an isotropic root and a root of the form

s\delta+2\delta_{1}
.

We will use the normalization
(\delta_{1},\delta_{1})=-1
.
For this normalization

(k\Lambda_{0}+\rho,\delta)=\frac{p}{q}
.

Retain notation of
\lx@sectionsign

8.3.3
. If
s
is even, then,
acting by a suitable element of
\tilde{W}
,
we can (and will) assume that
R_{\lambda+\rho}
contains
\varepsilon_{1}-\delta_{1}
and

2\delta_{1}
. Since
R_{\lambda+\rho}
contains
\varepsilon_{1}-\delta_{1}
,
one has
(\lambda+\rho,\varepsilon_{1}-\delta_{1})=0
and

{R}_{\lambda+\rho}
does not contain
j\delta+\varepsilon_{1}-\delta_{1}
for
j\not=0

(since
(\lambda+\rho,\delta)\not=0
).
Set

a:=(\lambda+\rho,\delta_{1}).

Since
(\lambda+\rho,\varepsilon_{1}-\delta_{1})=0
we have
a=(\lambda+\rho,\varepsilon_{1})
.
Since
2\delta_{1}\in R_{\lambda+\rho}
,
2a
is an odd integer.
In this case
\overline{R}_{\lambda+\rho}\cap\Delta^{\operatorname{an}}=\{\mathbb{Z}q\delta\pm\varepsilon_{1};\mathbb{Z}q\delta\pm\delta_{1}\}
.
If
j\delta+\varepsilon_{1}+\delta
lies in
\overline{R}_{\lambda+\rho}
,
then
\frac{jp}{q}=2a
which is impossible since
p
is even.
Hence

\overline{R}_{\lambda+\rho}=\{\mathbb{Z}q\delta\pm\varepsilon_{1};\mathbb{Z}q\delta\pm\delta_{1};\pm(\varepsilon_{1}-\delta_{1})\}
,
so

\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}=\{\mathbb{Z}q\delta\pm\varepsilon_{1};\mathbb{Z}q\delta\pm\delta_{1}\}\cup\{2\mathbb{Z}q\pm\varepsilon_{1}\pm\delta_{1},2\mathbb{Z}q\delta\pm 2\delta_{1}\}\cong D(2|1)^{(2)}

and
\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}\cap\Delta^{\#}=\{2\mathbb{Z}q\delta\pm 2\delta_{1}\}\subset R_{\lambda+\rho}
.

Now consider the case

R_{\lambda+\rho}
contains an isotropic root
\beta

and a root of the form

s\delta+2\delta_{1}
for an odd
s
.
Using the normalization
(\delta_{1},\delta_{1})=1
we have

(\lambda+\rho,s\delta+2\delta_{1})\in 2\mathbb{Z},\ \ (\lambda+\rho,\beta)=0

for some isotropic root
\beta
. Then
2\beta=s\delta+2\delta_{1}+j\delta\pm 2\varepsilon_{1}

(for some odd
j
), so

(\lambda+\rho,\frac{j}{2}\delta\pm\varepsilon_{1})
is integral and thus

(\lambda+\rho,\frac{j+q}{2}\delta\pm\varepsilon_{1})
is integral.
Since
j+q
is even, we get
\varepsilon_{1}\in\operatorname{cl}(R_{\lambda+\rho})
.
Hence
\operatorname{cl}(\Delta_{\lambda+\rho})
contains
\pm\varepsilon_{1}
,
\pm 2\delta_{1}

and an isotropic root
\operatorname{cl}(\beta)
. Since
\operatorname{cl}(\Delta_{\lambda+\rho})\subset B(1|1)

and
s_{\alpha}\operatorname{cl}(\Delta_{\lambda+\rho})

for any non-isotropic
\alpha\in\operatorname{cl}(\Delta_{\lambda+\rho})

we obtain
\operatorname{cl}(\Delta_{\lambda+\rho})=B(1|1)
, so

\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}
is isomorphic to
B(1|1)^{(1)}

or to
D(2|1)^{(1)}
. If
\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}\cong D(2|1)^{(1)}
, then
for any
\alpha\in(\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}\cap\Delta^{\#})

one has
\alpha/2\in\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}
; since

(s\delta+2\delta_{1})/2\not\in\Delta
we conclude

\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}\not\cong D(2|1)^{(1)}
. Hence

\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}\cong B(1|1)^{(1)}
. Let us show that

\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}\cap\Delta^{\#}\not\subset R_{\lambda+\rho}.

Since
\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}\cong B(1|1)^{(1)}
, the set

\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}\cap\Delta^{\#}

contains a root of the form
2\alpha
where
\alpha\in\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}
,
that is
\alpha=r\delta\pm\delta_{1}
. If
2\alpha\in R_{\lambda+\rho}
, then

(\lambda+\rho,2r\delta\pm 2\delta_{1})
is odd.
Since
(\lambda+\rho,s\delta+2\delta_{1})
is even,

\frac{(2r\pm s)p}{q}
is an odd integer which is impossible since
p
is even.

8.5.
Subprincipal levels

Summarizing, we obtain: if
\mathfrak{g}
is an indecomposable
non-twisted affine Kac-Moody superalgebra which is
not a Lie algebra and

k
is a subprincipal admissible level,
then
\mathfrak{g}
is of type
B(m|n)^{(1)}
with
m>n
, or
m=n=1
, or
m=0
.
For
B(0|n)^{(1)}
the subprincipal admissible levels
are of the form
k=-h^{\vee}+\frac{p}{2q}
, where
p,q
are coprime positive integers,

pq
is even and
p\geq 2n-1
(in this case
k^{\prime}=\frac{p-2n+1}{2}
).
For
B(m|n)^{(1)}
with
m>n
the subprincipal admissible levels
are of the form
k=-h^{\vee}+\frac{p}{q}
,
where
p,q
are coprime positive integers

q
is even and
p>2(m-n)
(in this case
k^{\prime}=\frac{p}{2}-(m-n)

lies in
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}+\frac{1}{2}
).
For
B(1|1)^{(1)}
the subprincipal admissible levels
are of the form
k=-h^{\vee}+\frac{p}{2q}
,
where
p,q
are positive coprime integers and
q
is odd
(in this case
k^{\prime}=p)
.

9.
The set
\Delta_{L}
for
V_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
-module
L
when
k
is principal admissible

Let
k
be a principal admissible level. As before, we write
k+h^{\vee}=\frac{p}{q}

for
\mathfrak{g}\not=B(m|n)^{(1)}
with
n\geq m
and
k+h^{\vee}=\frac{p}{2q}

for
\mathfrak{g}=B(m|n)^{(1)}
with
n\geq m
, where
p,q
are positive coprime integers.
Let
L(\lambda)
be a
V_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
-module.

9.1.
Notation

By Theorem
7.3.2
one has

\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}\cap\Delta^{\#}=R_{\lambda+\rho}\cap\Delta^{\#}
is isometric to
\Delta^{\#}
.

Retain notation of
\lx@sectionsign

4.3
. By Theorem
B.6

all components of
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
are finite-dimensional or affine Kac-Moody superalgebras.

Since
\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}\cap\Delta^{\#}
is isometric to

(\Delta^{\#})^{\operatorname{re}}
, this
is an indecomposable root system, so

\psi^{-1}(\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}\cap\Delta^{\#})

lies in the root system of an indecomposable component of
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
.
We denote this component by
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime\prime}
and its root system
by
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
.

Since
(\Delta^{\#})^{\operatorname{re}}
is infinite,
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
is infinite, so

\mathfrak{g}^{\prime\prime}
is an affine Kac-Moody superalgebra.
We denote by
\delta^{\prime\prime}

the minimal imaginary root of
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime\prime}
.
Recall that
\psi(\delta^{\prime\prime})=u\delta
for some
u\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, see
\lx@sectionsign

4.2.7
.

9.2.
Example

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be
A(2|2)^{(1)}
with

\Sigma=\{\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2},\varepsilon_{2}-\varepsilon_{3},\varepsilon_{3}-\delta_{1},\delta_{1}-\delta_{2},\delta_{2}-\delta_{3},\delta-\varepsilon_{1}+\delta_{3}\}.

Let
\lambda=\Lambda_{0}+a(\delta_{2}+\delta_{3})
where
a\not\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Then
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{g}^{\prime\prime}\times\mathfrak{g}^{\prime\prime\prime}
, where
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime\prime}=A(2|1)^{(1)}
with the simple roots

\{\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2},\varepsilon_{2}-\varepsilon_{3},\varepsilon_{3}-\delta_{1},\delta-\varepsilon_{1}+\delta_{1}\})

and
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime\prime\prime}=A_{1}^{(1)}
with the simple roots
\{\delta_{2}-\delta_{3},\delta-\delta_{2}+\delta_{3}\}
.

9.3.

Proposition.

(i)

If
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime\prime\prime}\not=\mathfrak{g}^{\prime\prime}
is a component of
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
,
then for any real root
\alpha
of
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime\prime\prime}
we have

(\alpha,\alpha)<0
(in particular,
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime\prime\prime}
is anisotropic).

(ii)

The Kac-Moody algebra
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime\prime}
is the non-twisted affinization of
\dot{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime\prime}
, where

\dot{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime\prime}=A(m|n_{1})
if
\dot{\mathfrak{g}}=A(m|n)
with
m\geq n
,

\dot{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime\prime}=G_{2}
or
G(3)
for
\dot{\mathfrak{g}}=G(3)
,

\dot{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime\prime}=B_{3}
or
F(4)
for
\dot{\mathfrak{g}}=F(4)
, and

\begin{array}[]{|c||c|c| c|c|c|c|c|c|c|}\hline\cr\dot{\mathfrak{g}}&B(m|n),\,m>n&B(m|n),\,n\geq m&D(m|n),\,m>n&D(m|n),\,n\geq m\\
\hline\cr\dot{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime\prime}&B(m|n_{1})&B(m_{1}|n)&D(m|n_{1})&D(m_{1}|n)\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

where
0\leq n_{1}\leq n
,
0\leq m_{1}\leq m
. Moreover,
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime\prime}\cong\mathfrak{g}^{\#}

if
\lambda
is typical, and
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime\prime}
is not anisotropic
if
\lambda
is atypical.

(iii)

One has
\psi(\delta^{\prime\prime})=q\delta
.

9.3.1.

Corollary.

If the dual Coxeter number of
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime\prime}

is equal to
h^{\vee}
, then
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime\prime}\cong\mathfrak{g}
and
\Delta^{\prime}=\Delta^{\prime\prime}
.
If
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}\not\cong\mathfrak{g}
, then the dual Coxeter number of
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime\prime}

is greater than
h^{\vee}
.

Proof.

From
the table for the dual Coxeter numbers in
\lx@sectionsign

3.5
we see
that the dual Coxeter number of
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime\prime}

is greater than
h^{\vee}
if
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime\prime}\not\cong\mathfrak{g}
. If
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime\prime}\cong\mathfrak{g}
, then

\operatorname{cl}(\Delta^{\prime\prime})\cong\operatorname{cl}(\Delta)
. But
\operatorname{cl}(\psi(\Delta^{\prime\prime}))
is a subset of

\dot{\Delta}=\operatorname{cl}(\Delta^{\operatorname{re}})

(and
\dot{\Delta}
is finite), so
\operatorname{cl}(\psi(\Delta^{\prime\prime}))=\dot{\Delta}
. This implies

\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{g}^{\prime\prime}
(since, if
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime\prime\prime}\not=\mathfrak{g}^{\prime\prime}
is a component of
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
,
then
\Delta(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime\prime\prime})
is orthogonal to
\Delta(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime\prime})
, so

\psi(\Delta(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime\prime\prime}))\subset\dot{\Delta}
is orthogonal to
\psi(\Delta(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime\prime}))=\dot{\Delta}
,
a contradiction).
∎

9.4.
Proof of Proposition
9.3

The case
\dot{\mathfrak{g}}=B(0|n)
is treated in Corollary
8.1.2
.
Now we assume that
\dot{\mathfrak{g}}
is not anisotropic.

Using
\psi
we view
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
as a subset of
\Delta
.

9.4.1.
Proof of (i)

By construction,
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
contains
\psi^{-1}(\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}\cap\Delta^{\#})

which is isometric to
\Delta^{\#}
, so

\operatorname{cl}\bigl((\Delta^{\prime\prime})^{\operatorname{re}}\bigr)
is a subset of

\operatorname{cl}\bigl((\Delta)^{\operatorname{re}}\bigr)=\dot{\Delta}
which contains a subset isometric
to
\operatorname{cl}(\Delta^{\#})=\dot{\Delta}^{\#}
. Since
\dot{\Delta}

is finite, we obtain

(9.1)

\dot{\Delta}^{\#}\subset\operatorname{cl}\bigl((\Delta^{\prime\prime})^{\operatorname{re}}\bigr)\subset\dot{\Delta}.

Take
\alpha\in\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}
such that
(\alpha,\alpha)\geq 0
.
We have to verify that
\alpha\in\Delta^{\prime\prime}
. Suppose the contrary.
Then
(\alpha,\Delta^{\prime\prime})=0
.
The set
\dot{\Delta}^{\#}
contains a root
\dot{\gamma}
such that

(\operatorname{cl}(\alpha),\dot{\gamma})\not=0
(one has
\mathbb{R}\dot{\Delta}=\mathbb{R}\dot{\Delta}^{\#}\oplus V^{\prime}
, where
the restriction of
(-,-)
to
V^{\prime}
is negative definite;
if
(\operatorname{cl}(\alpha),\dot{\Delta}^{\#})=0
,
then
\operatorname{cl}(\alpha)\in V^{\prime}
, so
(\alpha,\alpha)=(\operatorname{cl}(\alpha),\operatorname{cl}(\alpha))<0
).
By (
9.1
),

\Delta^{\prime\prime}
contains
\gamma

such that
\operatorname{cl}(\gamma)=\dot{\gamma}
and thus

(\gamma,\alpha)=0
, a contradiction.

9.4.2.
Proof of (ii) for
\mathfrak{g}\not=B(m|n)^{(1)}
,
n\geq m

The assumption
\mathfrak{g}\not=B(m|n)^{(1)}
with
n\geq m
gives

\Delta^{\#}=\{\alpha\in\Delta|\ (\alpha,\alpha)>0\}.

If
\lambda
is typical,
then
\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}
does not contain
isotropic roots and, by (i),

(9.2)

(\Delta^{\prime\prime})^{\operatorname{re}}=\{\alpha\in\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}|\ (\alpha,\alpha)>0\}.

The assumption
\mathfrak{g}\not=B(m|n)^{(1)}
with
n\geq m
gives

\Delta^{\#}=\{\alpha\in\Delta|\ (\alpha,\alpha)>0\}
, so

\{\alpha\in(\Delta^{\prime\prime})^{\operatorname{re}}|\ (\alpha,\alpha)>0\}=(\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}\cap\Delta^{\#})\text{
is isometric to }(\Delta^{\#})^{\operatorname{re}}.

If
\lambda
is atypical, then
\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}

contains isotropic roots and these roots lie in
\Delta^{\prime\prime}

by (i). Using (
9.1
) and
the table in Appendix
A.1.1
we obtain the statement for
\dot{\mathfrak{g}}\not=B(m|n)
.

For
\dot{\mathfrak{g}}=B(m|n)
the same argument implies that
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime\prime}
is of type
B(m|n_{1})^{(1)}

or
D(m+1|n_{1})^{(2)}
for some
0<n_{1}\leq n
.
If
m>n
and
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime\prime}=D(m+1|n_{1})^{(2)}
, then

\{\alpha\in(\Delta^{\prime\prime})^{\operatorname{re}}|\ (\alpha,\alpha)>0\}=D_{m=1}^{(2)}

is not isometric to
(\Delta^{\#})^{\operatorname{re}}=B_{m}^{(1)}
, a contradiction.
This completes the proof of (ii) for
\mathfrak{g}\not=B(m|n)
,
n\geq m
.

9.4.3.
Proof of (iii) for
\mathfrak{g}\not=B(m|n)^{(1)}
,
n\geq m

Recall that

\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}\cap\Delta^{\#}=R_{\lambda+\rho}\cap\Delta^{\#}

is isometric to
(\Delta^{\#})^{\operatorname{re}}
. Since,
for
\mathfrak{g}
in question,
\alpha/2\not\in\Delta
for all
\alpha\in\Delta^{\#}

we have

\overline{R}_{\lambda+\rho}\cap\Delta^{\#}={R}_{\lambda+\rho}\cap\Delta^{\#}.

Since this set is isometric to
(\Delta^{\#})^{\operatorname{re}}
, it contains
\alpha

such that
(\alpha,\alpha)=2
. Since

\alpha\in\overline{R}_{\lambda+\rho}
, so
(\lambda+\rho,\alpha)\in\mathbb{Z}
and

(\lambda+\rho,i\delta+\alpha)=i(k+h^{\vee})+(\lambda+\rho,\alpha)=\frac{ip}{q}+(\lambda+\rho,\alpha).

Therefore
(i\delta+\alpha)\in\overline{R}_{\lambda+\rho}
if and only if
i

is divisible by
q
. Since
\overline{R}_{\lambda+\rho}\cap\Delta^{\#}

is isometric to
(\Delta^{\#})^{\operatorname{re}}
, this gives

(\overline{R}_{\lambda+\rho}\cap\Delta^{\#})+i\delta=\overline{R}_{\lambda+\rho}\cap\Delta^{\#}\ \ \Longleftrightarrow\ \ q|i

so
u=q
and thus
\psi(\delta^{\prime\prime})=q\delta
as required.

9.4.4.

For the remaining case
\mathfrak{g}=B(m|n)^{(1)}
with
n\geq m
, (ii) and (iii)
is proved in Lemma
9.5
below.∎

9.5.

Lemma.

Let
\mathfrak{g}=B(m|n)^{(1)}
with
n\geq m
and
k
such that
k+h^{\vee}=\frac{p}{2q}

where
p,q
are odd coprime positive integers. Let
\lambda
be a weight of level
k

such that
R_{\lambda+\rho}\cap\Delta^{\#}
is
isometric to
(\Delta^{\#})^{\operatorname{re}}
. Then

\mathfrak{g}^{\prime\prime}\cong B(m_{1}|n)^{(1)}
for some
0\leq m_{1}\leq m
,
where
m=0
if and only if
\lambda
is typical. Moreover,

\psi(\delta^{\prime\prime})=q\delta
.

Proof.

We will use the normalization
(\delta_{1},\delta_{1})=1
.
For this normalization

(k\Lambda_{0}+\rho,\delta)=\frac{p}{q}

and all roots are of integral square length, so

2(\lambda+\rho,\gamma)
is integral for all
\gamma\in\Delta_{\lambda+\rho}
.

As in the proof of Lemma
8.3.1

we will use the following observation:

(s\delta+2\delta_{i})\in R_{\lambda+\rho}
if and only if

(\lambda+\rho,s\delta+2\delta_{i})

is odd if
s
is even, and
(\lambda+\rho,s\delta+2\delta_{i})

is even if
s
is odd.
The assumption that

R_{\lambda+\rho}\cap\Delta^{\#}
is isometric to
(\Delta^{\#})^{\operatorname{re}}

implies that
(s\delta+2\delta_{1})\in R_{\lambda+\rho}
for some
s
.
Since
p
is odd, the above observation implies

((s+q)\delta+2\delta_{1})\in R_{\lambda+\rho}
.
Since
q
is odd, one of the numbers
s
and
s+q
is even and another is odd.
Without loss of generality we assume that
s
is even.
Then
(s\delta+2\delta_{1})\in R_{\lambda+\rho}

implies
\beta\in{R}_{\lambda+\rho}
for
\beta:=\frac{s}{2}\delta+\delta_{1}
.
Moreover,
R_{\lambda+\rho}
contains
\gamma=((s+q)\delta+2\delta_{1})

such that
\gamma/2\not\in R_{\lambda+\rho}

(since
s+q
is odd) and

\operatorname{cl}(\gamma)/2=\operatorname{cl}(\beta)\in\operatorname{cl}(R_{\lambda+\rho})
.

Set
X:=\{\alpha\in R_{\lambda+\rho}|\ (\alpha,\alpha)>0\}
.
Notice that

\beta,\gamma\in X
.

Since
R_{\lambda+\rho}\cap\Delta^{\#}
is isometric to
(\Delta^{\#})^{\operatorname{re}}
,

\operatorname{cl}(X)
contains
\dot{\Delta}^{\#}
.
Observe that
X
is a real root subsystem
(this means that

s_{\alpha}\gamma\in X
for all
\alpha,\gamma\in X
) in the root system

\{\alpha\in\Delta|\ (\alpha,\alpha)>0\}
which is of type
B(0|n)^{(1)}
.
By Proposition
B.8
,
X
is isomertic to the set of real roots
of an anisotropic affine Kac-Moody superalgebra
\mathfrak{t}
. By above,

X
contains an odd non-isotropic root
\beta
and
\operatorname{cl}(X)
contains
\dot{\Delta}^{\#}

(of type
C_{n}
).
Using
the classification in
[
25
]
, we conclude that

\mathfrak{t}
is of type
B(0|n)^{(1)}
or
D(1|n+1)^{(2)}
.
By above,
X

contains
\gamma

such that
\operatorname{cl}(\gamma)/2\in\operatorname{cl}(X)
and
\gamma/2\not\in X
.
The root system of
D(1|n+1)^{(2)}
does not contain a root with these properties, so

\mathfrak{t}
is of type
B(0|n)^{(1)}
.

One has
(\beta+j\delta)\in R_{\lambda+\rho}

if and only if
j
is divisible by
q
. Thus

X+j\delta=X
if and only if
j
is divisible by
q
