\Sigma^{\prime}
and
\tau^{\prime}
is the set
of odd roots in this base. The set
\Sigma^{\prime}

coincides with the set of indecomposable
elements in
\{\alpha\in R_{1,1}|\ \omega(\alpha)>0\}
.
Since

\{\alpha\in R_{1,1}|\ \omega(\alpha)>0\}
is equal to

\{\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\delta\pm\varepsilon_{1}\pm\delta_{1},\ 2\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\delta\pm 2\delta_{1},\ (2\mathbb{Z}_{>0}-1)\delta\pm 2\varepsilon_{1}\}\cup\{\varepsilon_{1}\pm\delta_{1},2\delta_{1}\},

one has
\Sigma^{\prime}=\{\delta-2\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{1}-\delta_{1},\ 2\delta_{1}\}
.
The Gram matrix of
\Sigma^{\prime}
is

A^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}4&-2&0\\
-2&0&2\\
0&2&-4\end{pmatrix}
.
We have
\tau^{\prime}=\{2\}
, since

a_{22}=0
(so
2\in\tau^{\prime}
) and

\frac{2a_{12}}{a_{11}}
,
\frac{2a_{32}}{a_{33}}
are odd integers
(so
1,3\not\in\tau^{\prime}
). Thus
(A^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})
is the Cartan datum
of
A(1|1)
, so
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{g}(A^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})=\mathfrak{gl}(2|2)
. However,
the set
\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}(\mathfrak{gl}(2|2))
is finite, so

R_{1,1}
is not isomorphic to a quotient of this set.

B.5.2.

Assume that

\Delta_{\lambda}
has an
indecomposable component
R
which is
isomorphic to
A(1|1)^{(2)}
. Let us show that the bijection

R\cong A(1|1)^{(2)}
preserves the parity.
Indeed, the roots of the form
\mathbb{Z}\delta^{\prime}\pm\varepsilon^{\prime}_{1}\pm\delta^{\prime}_{1}
are isotropic,
so they are odd. All other roots in
R

are non-isotropic. If
R
contains a non-isotropic odd root

\alpha
, then
2\alpha\in R
. However, for any non-isotropic root

\alpha\in R
we have
2\alpha\not\in R
. Therefore all non-isotropic roots
in
R
are even. Hence the bijection between
R
and
A(1|1)^{(2)}

preserves the parity.

B.6.

Theorem.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a symmetrizable Kac-Moody superalgebra of finite rank
and let
\lambda\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}
.

(i)

If
\Delta_{\lambda}
is a bijective quotient of

\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})
, where
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
is a symmetrizable Kac-Moody
superalgebra of at most countable rank,
then the bijection
\Delta_{\lambda}\to\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})

preserves the parity.

(ii)

If
\Delta_{\lambda}

does not contain isotropic roots, then
\Delta_{\lambda}

is a bijective quotient of the system of real roots
of an anisotropic symmetrizable Kac-Moody superalgebra
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}

of at most countable rank.

(iii)

If
\mathfrak{g}
is a finite-dimensional Kac-Moody superalgebra, then

\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
is as well.
If
\mathfrak{g}
is an affine symmetrizable
Kac-Moody superalgebra and each indecomposable component
R
of

\Delta_{\lambda}
is not of type
A(1|1)^{(2)}
,
then
\Delta_{\lambda}
is isomorphic
to
\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})
, where

\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}=\prod\limits_{i\in I}\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{i}

and each
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{i}
is either finite-dimensional or affine
symmetrizable Kac-Moody superalgebra.
Moreover, the set
I
is finite if
(\lambda,\delta)\not=0
.

(iv)

If
\Delta_{\lambda}
has an indecomposable component
of type
A(1|1)^{(2)}
, then

\mathfrak{g}
is of the type
A(2m-1|2n-1)^{(2)}
and

(\lambda,\delta)=\frac{p}{q}
, where
p,q
are coprime integers and
q
is odd (see
\lx@sectionsign

3.5
for the normalization).

B.6.1.
Remark

Theorem
B.6
was stated in
[
14
]
without proof
and the case

A(1|1)^{(2)}
was not excluded.

B.6.2.
Proof of (i)

Let
\psi:\mathbb{C}\Delta_{\lambda}\to\mathbb{C}\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})

be the linear map which gives the bijection
\Delta_{\lambda}\to\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})
.

Take an odd root
\alpha\in\Delta_{\lambda}
. If

\alpha
is isotropic, then
\psi(\alpha)
is isotropic, so it is odd.
If
\alpha
is anisotropic, then, by (d) in
\lx@sectionsign

B.3
,

2\alpha\in\Delta_{\lambda}
. Then

2\psi(\alpha)\in\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})
, so

\psi(\alpha)
is odd.

Take an even root
\alpha\in\Delta_{\lambda}
.
By Lemma
B.2.9
,
\Delta_{\lambda}
contains an isotropic
\beta

such that
(\beta,\alpha)\not=0
. By (c) in
\lx@sectionsign

B.3
,
\Delta_{\lambda}
contains

\beta-\alpha
or
\beta+\alpha
. Without loss of generality we can assume

\beta-\alpha\in\Delta_{\lambda}
. Since
\beta
is odd and
\alpha
is even,

\beta-\alpha
is odd. By above,
\psi(\beta)
and
\psi(\beta-\alpha)

are odd, so
\psi(\alpha)
is even. ∎

B.6.3.

Assume that
R\subset\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}(\mathfrak{g})

satisfies (GR0)–(GR2) and each indecomposable component
R_{i}
of

R
is a bijective quotient of the system of real roots
of a symmetrizable Kac-Moody superalgebra
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{i}
of at most countable rank. Then
R
is a bijective quotient of

\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})
, where
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}=\prod\limits_{i}\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{i}

is a symmetrizable Kac-Moody superalgebra
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
of at most countable rank.

B.6.4.
Proof of (ii)

This statement follows from
\lx@sectionsign

B.6.3
and Proposition
B.8
(i) below.

B.6.5.

Lemma.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a symmetrizable affine Kac-Moody superalgebra.
Then for each
\lambda\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}
the set
\Delta_{\lambda}

has finitely many indecomposable components not isomorphic
to
\Delta(\mathfrak{sl}(1|1))
. If
(\lambda,\delta)\not=0
, then
the set
\Delta_{\lambda}

has finitely many indecomposable components.

Proof.

Set
R_{\lambda,i}:=\Delta_{\lambda,i}\cap R_{\lambda}
.
Note that
(\Delta_{\lambda,i},R_{\lambda,j})=0
for
i\not=j\in I
.
If
R_{\lambda,i}=\emptyset
, then for any
\gamma\in\Delta_{\lambda_{i}}

one has
\gamma\not\in R_{\lambda}
and
(\gamma,R_{\lambda})=0
which contradicts to Lemma
4.1.4
(ii). Hence
R=\coprod_{i\in I}R_{\lambda,i}
where
R_{\lambda,i}
are pairwise orthogonal non-empty sets.

Let
J
be the set of indices
j
such that
\Delta_{\lambda,j}\not\subset\Delta^{\operatorname{iso}}
.

For
j\in J
the sets

\Delta_{\lambda_{j}}
contains a non-isotropic root
\gamma_{j}
, and
for
j_{1},j_{2}\in J
one has

\operatorname{cl}(\gamma_{j_{1}})\not=\operatorname{cl}(\gamma_{j_{2}})
. Since

\operatorname{cl}(\Delta^{\operatorname{re}})
is finite, the set
J
is finite.

Take
i\in I\setminus J
. Then
\Delta_{\lambda,j}\subset\Delta^{\operatorname{iso}}
.
For each
i\in I\setminus J

fix
\beta_{i}\in\ R_{\lambda,i}
; note that
\beta_{i}\in\Delta^{\operatorname{iso}}

(since
\Delta_{\lambda,i}\subset\Delta^{\operatorname{iso}}
), so

(\lambda,\beta_{i})=0
(by definition of
R_{\lambda}
).

Let us show that
\Delta_{\lambda,i}
is the root system of
\mathfrak{sl}(1|1)

for each
i\in I\setminus J
.
Indeed, if
\Delta_{\lambda,i}\not=\{\pm\beta_{i}\}
, then

\Delta_{\lambda,i}
contains
\beta
which is not orthogonal
to
\beta_{i}
(since
\Delta_{\lambda,i}
is indecomposable).
Then, by (GR2),

\Delta_{\lambda,j}
contains
\gamma\in\{\beta_{i}+\beta,\beta_{i}-\beta\}
.
Since
\beta,\beta_{i}
are isotropic and non-orthogonal,
\gamma

is anisotropic, a contradiction.

Suppose that
I\setminus J
is infinite.
Since
\operatorname{cl}(\Delta^{\operatorname{re}})
is finite, one has
\operatorname{cl}(\beta_{i_{1}})=\operatorname{cl}(\beta_{i_{2}})

for some
i_{1},i_{2}\in I\setminus J
, so
\beta_{i_{1}}-\beta_{i_{2}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\not=0}\delta
.
Hence
(\lambda,\delta)=0
as required.
∎

B.6.6.

We will prove (iv) in
\lx@sectionsign

B.13
below.
Using
\lx@sectionsign

B.6.3
and Lemma
B.6.5

we reduce Theorem
B.6
(iii) to the following statement
which will be proved in
\lx@sectionsign\lx@sectionsign

B.9
–

B.13
below.

B.6.7.

Proposition.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a finite-dimensional or affine
symmetrizable Kac-Moody superalgebra.

(i)

If
R\subset\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}(\mathfrak{g})

satisfying (GR0)–(GR2) is indecomposable, contains an isotropic root,
and
\operatorname{cl}(R)

is not isomorphic to
\Delta(\mathfrak{psl}(2|2))
, then
R
is isomorphic
to
\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})
, where

\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
is a finite-dimensional or affine symmetrizable Kac-Moody superalgebra.

(ii)

If
\lambda\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}
and
R
is an indecomposable component of
\Delta_{\lambda}

and
\operatorname{cl}(R)

is isomorphic to
\Delta(\mathfrak{psl}(2|2))
, then
R

is isomorphic to
A(1|1)^{(2)}
and
(\lambda,\delta)=\frac{p}{q}
,
where
q
is odd.

B.6.8.
Remark

In
[
47
]
V. Serganova defined “generalized root systems” (GRS) as finite sets
satisfying the condition that
(-,-)
is non-degenerate on the span of
R
, and axioms (GR0)–(GR2),
and showed that any indecomposable GRS
is isomorphic to the root system of a basic classical Lie superalgebra different from
\mathfrak{psl}(2|2)
.
Several attempts to generalize this approach to affine Lie superalgebras were made,
but, to the best of our knowledge, root systems of affine Lie superalgebras
are not singled out by a set of axioms.
In
[
51
]
(cf.
[
50
]
) M. Yousofzadeh introduced and classified the
extended affine supersystems (with the “string property” instead of the axiom (GR2)),
which include root systems of affine Lie superalgebras.
Proposition
B.6.7
(i)
can be deduced from the classification theorem
[
51
]
, Theorem 2.2 (which is based on the Serganova’s result).
Since the latter classification is rather complicated
we deduce Proposition
B.6.7
(i)
directly from Serganova’s result.

B.7.
Gram matrices

For a finite subset

\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{s}
of
L

we denote by

\Gamma_{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{s}}=\bigl((\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j})\bigr)

the corresponding Gram matrix.
We will use the following fact.

B.7.1.

Lemma.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a symmetrizable finite-dimensional or affine Kac-Moody superalgebra and let
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{s}\in\Delta^{\operatorname{an}}(\mathfrak{g})
be such that

(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i+1})\not=0
for
i=1,\ldots,s-1
.
Then the matrix
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{1})^{-1}\Gamma_{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{s}}
is positive semidefinite and
\operatorname{cl}(\alpha_{1}),\ldots,\operatorname{cl}(\alpha_{s})
are linearly dependent
if
\det\Gamma_{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{s}}=0
.

Proof.

Set
\gamma_{i}:=\operatorname{cl}(\alpha_{i})
and note that

\Gamma_{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{s}}=\Gamma_{\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{s}}
.
Since
(\gamma_{i},\gamma_{i+1})\not=0
for
i=1,\ldots,s-1
,
the vectors
\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{s}
lie in the same indecomposable component of the set
\operatorname{cl}(\Delta^{\operatorname{an}})
.
These components are the sets of real roots of
finite-dimensional anisotropic Kac-Moody superalgebras.
The restriction of the bilinear form
(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{1})^{-1}(-,-)
to the span of such component is positive definite.
This implies the statement.
∎

B.8.

Proposition.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a symmetrizable Kac-Moody superalgebra, possibly of countable rank.
Let
R\subset\Delta^{\operatorname{an}}(\mathfrak{g})
be an indecomposable set (in the sense of Definition
B.2.7
)
satisfying
s_{\alpha}\beta\in R
for all
\alpha,\beta\in R

and
2\alpha\in R
for all odd
\alpha\in R
. Then

(i)

There exists an indecomposable symmetrizable
anisotropic Kac-Moody superalgebra
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
,
possibly of countable rank, such that
R
is a
bijective quotient of
\Delta(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})^{\operatorname{re}}
.

(ii)

If
\mathfrak{g}
is finite-dimensional or affine Kac-Moody superalgebra, then

\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
in (i) is finite-dimensional or affine Kac-Moody superalgebra, and
R
is isomorphic to
\Delta(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})^{\operatorname{re}}
.

Proof.

If
\alpha\in\Delta^{\operatorname{an}}(\mathfrak{g})
,
then
\mathfrak{g}_{\pm\alpha}
act locally nilpotently,
so for each
\gamma\in\Delta(\mathfrak{g})
,

\frac{2(\alpha,\gamma)}{(\alpha,\alpha)}
is integral
and is even if
\alpha
is odd. Set
R^{+}:=\Delta^{+}(\mathfrak{g})\cap R
and

(B.2)

\Sigma^{\prime}:=\{\alpha\in R|\ s_{\alpha}\bigl(R^{+}\setminus\{\alpha,2\alpha\}\bigr)\subset R^{+}\}.

Arguing as in
[
39
]
we obtain
\frac{2(\alpha,\beta)}{(\beta,\beta)}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}

for all
\alpha,\beta\in\Sigma^{\prime}
with
\alpha\not=\beta

and

(B.3)

R=W[\Sigma^{\prime}]\bigl(\Sigma^{\prime}\coprod\{2\alpha|\alpha\in\Sigma^{\prime},p(\alpha)=\overline{1}\}\bigr).

By above,
\frac{2(\alpha,\beta)}{(\beta,\beta)}

is even if
\beta
is odd. Therefore
the matrix
A^{\prime}:=(\langle\alpha,\beta^{\vee}\rangle)_{\alpha,\beta\in\Sigma^{\prime}}

is a Cartan matrix for a anisotropic symmetrizable generalized Kac-Moody superalgebra
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}

with a base
\Sigma^{\prime\prime}

and the parity function
p^{\prime}:\Sigma^{\prime\prime}\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}
induced
by the parity function
p:\Delta(\mathfrak{g})\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Moreover, the map
\Sigma^{\prime\prime}\to\Sigma^{\prime}
extends
to a linear homomorphism
\psi:\mathbb{Z}\Sigma^{\prime\prime}\to\mathbb{Z}\Sigma^{\prime}
which preserves the parity and
the bilinear forms,
and maps
\overline{\Delta}^{\operatorname{re}}(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})=W[\Sigma^{\prime\prime}](\Sigma^{\prime\prime})

to
R\cap\overline{\Delta}^{\operatorname{re}}(\mathfrak{g})=W[\Sigma^{\prime}](\Sigma^{\prime})
.
By (
2.3
), this implies

\psi\bigl(\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})\bigr)=R
. Using formula (
B.3
) and the assumption that
R
is indecomposable, we conclude
that the Dynkin diagram of

\Sigma^{\prime\prime}
is connected. This establishes (i).

For (ii) assume that
\mathfrak{g}
is finite-dimensional or affine
Kac-Moody superalgebra.

Let us show that
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
is a Kac-Moody superalgebra, i.e. that
\Sigma^{\prime}
is finite. Indeed, if
\Delta
is finite, then
R
is finite.
If
\Delta
is affine, then the image of
\Delta
in
\mathbb{Z}\Delta/\mathbb{Z}\delta
is finite, so,
if
\Sigma^{\prime}
is infinite, then

\alpha,\alpha+j\delta\in\Sigma^{\prime}
for some
j\not=0
, but
\frac{2(\alpha,\alpha+j\delta)}{(\alpha,\alpha)}=2
,
a contradiction (by above, this number is non-positive).
Hence
\Sigma^{\prime}
is finite, so
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
is a Kac-Moody superalgebra.

Renormalize the bilinear form on
\Delta(\mathfrak{g})
in such a way
that
(\gamma,\gamma)=2
for some
\gamma\in\Sigma^{\prime}
.
By (i), the Dynkin diagram of
\Sigma^{\prime\prime}
is connected.
By Lemma
B.7.1
, the Gram matrix
\Gamma_{\Sigma^{\prime}}=\Gamma_{\Sigma^{\prime\prime}}

is either positive definite or
positive semidefinite; the algebra
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
is finite-dimensional in the first case
and affine in the second case (see
[
26
]
, Theorem 4.3 and
[
25
]
).

It remains to verify that the linear map

\psi:\mathbb{C}\Sigma^{\prime\prime}\to\mathbb{C}\Sigma^{\prime}
is injective.
Since
\psi
preserves the bilinear forms,
\operatorname{Ker}\psi
lies
in the kernel of the bilinear form on
\mathbb{C}\Sigma^{\prime\prime}
. In particular,
\operatorname{Ker}\psi=0
if

\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
is finite-dimensional. In the remaining case
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
is affine. Suppose the contrary, that
\operatorname{Ker}\psi\not=0
, so

\operatorname{Ker}\psi=\mathbb{C}\delta^{\prime}

where
\delta^{\prime}
is the minimal imaginary root in
\Delta(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})
. Then

R\cap\overline{\Delta}^{\operatorname{re}}(\mathfrak{g})=\psi(\overline{\Delta}^{\operatorname{re}}(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}))

is a classical finite root system
and
\Sigma^{\prime}
is a set of simple roots of this root system.
Thus the Cartan matrix
A^{\prime}
is invertible, so
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
is not affine, a contradiction. This completes the proof of (ii).
∎

B.9.
Set
F(\alpha)

Let
R\subset L
be a set satisfying (GR0), (GR1) and (WGR2).
Let
K
be the kernel of the restriction of
(-,-)
to
\mathbb{C}R
.
Fix any element
\delta_{0}\in K

and let
\operatorname{cl}_{0}
be the canonical map

\operatorname{cl}_{0}:\mathbb{C}R\to\mathbb{C}R/\mathbb{C}\delta_{0}
.
For example, if
R\subset\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}(\mathfrak{g})
, then
for
\delta_{0}=\delta
we have
\operatorname{cl}=\operatorname{cl}_{0}
.
For any

\alpha\in R
we set

F(\alpha):=\{c\in\mathbb{C}|\ \alpha+c\delta_{0}\in R\}.

Since
\mathbb{Z}R
is a free module of finite rank,
we have
F(\alpha)\subset\mathbb{Z}a
for some
a\in\mathbb{C}
.

B.9.1.

Lemma.

(i)

For all
\alpha\in R
we have
0\in F(\alpha)
,
F(-\alpha)=-F(\alpha)

and
F(w\alpha)=F(\alpha)
for all
w\in W[R]
.

(ii)

Let
\alpha\in R
be such that
(\alpha,\alpha)\not=0
.
Then
F(\alpha)=\mathbb{Z}a
for some
a\in\mathbb{C}
. Moreover,
for
\gamma\in R
the set
F(\gamma)

has a structure of
\mathbb{Z}
-module with respect to the action

j*c:=c-j\langle\gamma,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle a

(for
j\in\mathbb{Z}
and
c\in F(\gamma)
). In particular,

\langle\gamma,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle F(\alpha)\subset F(\gamma)\ \ \text{ for all }\alpha,\gamma\in R\ \text{ with }(\alpha,\alpha)\not=0.

(iii)

If
\beta,\gamma\in R
are such that

(\beta,\beta)=0,\ \ \ (\gamma,\beta)\not=0,\ \ \ \bigl(\operatorname{cl}_{0}(\gamma)-\operatorname{cl}_{0}(\beta)\bigr)\not\in\operatorname{cl}_{0}(R),

then
\beta+\gamma\in R
and
F(\beta),F(\gamma)\subset F(\beta+\gamma)
.

Proof.

(i) follows from (GR0) and (GR1) and (iii) follows from (GR2).
For
(ii) take
\alpha\in R
such that
(\alpha,\alpha)\not=0
. For
x\in F(\alpha)
we have

x\delta_{0}-\alpha\in R
. Thus for

\gamma\in R
we have

s_{x\delta_{0}-\alpha}s_{\alpha}(\gamma)=\gamma-x\langle\gamma,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle\delta_{0}\in R,

so
c\in F(\gamma)
forces
(c-x\langle\gamma,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle)\in F(\gamma)
.
In particular, fo all
x,c\in F(\alpha)
we have
c-2x\in F(\alpha)
. Hence

F(\alpha)
is a subgroup of
\mathbb{C}
.
Since
R\subset\Delta

we have
F(\alpha)\subset\mathbb{Z}a
for some
a\in\mathbb{C}
.
By above,

c-x\langle\gamma,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle\in R
. Since
F(\gamma)

contains
0
this gives
\langle\gamma,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle F(\alpha)\subset F(\gamma)
.
∎

B.9.2.

By (ii) we have
F(\alpha)=F(-\alpha)
for a non-isotropic

\alpha\in R
.
In
\lx@sectionsign\lx@sectionsign

B.9.3
–
B.9.5
we will see that
the same formula holds for isotropic
\alpha
if

\operatorname{cl}(R)\not=C(1|1)
, using that in all other cases any isotropic
root
\alpha
can be included in
A(1|0)
,
B(1|1)
or
BC(1|1)
.

B.9.3.

Consider the case when

\operatorname{cl}_{0}(R)
is of type
A(1|0)
. Take
\alpha,\beta\in R

such that
\operatorname{cl}(\alpha)=\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2}
,

\operatorname{cl}(\beta)=\varepsilon_{2}-\delta_{1}
. Then
\alpha\in R^{\operatorname{an}}
,

\beta\in R^{\operatorname{iso}}

and
s_{\alpha}\beta=\beta+\alpha
. Let
E
be the
span of
\alpha,\beta
. By Lemma
B.9.1
(i),

R^{\operatorname{an}}=\{\pm\alpha+\mathbb{Z}a\delta_{0}\}

for some
a\in\mathbb{C}
. Then

W[R]\beta=\{\beta+\mathbb{Z}a\delta_{0},\beta+\alpha+\mathbb{Z}a\delta_{0}\}
.
The Gram matrix
\Gamma_{\alpha,\beta}

is equal to the Gram matrix
\Gamma_{\operatorname{cl}(\alpha),\operatorname{cl}(\beta)}

which is non-degenerate, so

E\cap\mathbb{C}\delta_{0}=0
which forces
-\beta\not\in W[R]\beta
.
Using Lemma
B.2.9
(ii) we obtain

R^{\operatorname{iso}}=-W[R]\beta\coprod W[R]\beta
, that is

R=\{\pm\alpha+\mathbb{Z}a\delta_{0},\pm\beta+\mathbb{Z}a\delta_{0},\pm(\beta+\alpha)+\mathbb{Z}a\delta_{0}\}.

Hence
E\cap R

can be identified with
A(1|0)
and

R=(E\cap R)+\mathbb{Z}a\delta_{0}

is isomorphic to
A(1|0)
if
a\delta_{0}=0
and to

A(1|0)^{(1)}
otherwise.

B.9.4.

Consider the case when
\operatorname{cl}_{0}(R)
is of type
B(1|1)
, i.e.

\operatorname{cl}_{0}(R)=\{\pm\varepsilon_{1},\pm\varepsilon_{1}\pm\delta_{1},\pm\delta_{1},\pm 2\delta_{1}\}.

Take
\alpha,\beta\in R
such that
\operatorname{cl}_{0}(\alpha)=\delta_{1}

and
\operatorname{cl}_{0}(\beta)=\varepsilon_{1}-\delta_{1}
.
By Lemma
B.9.1
(iii),
R
contains
\alpha+\beta

and
F(\alpha),F(\beta)\subset F(\alpha+\beta)
.
Applying Lemma
B.9.1
(iii) to the pair
-\beta,\alpha+\beta

we obtain
F(\alpha+\beta)\subset F(\alpha)
, that is

F(\alpha)=F(\alpha+\beta)
. By (GR1),
s_{\alpha}\beta=\beta+2\alpha\in R
and

F(\beta+2\alpha)=F(\beta)
(by Lemma
B.9.1
(i)).
Applying Lemma
B.9.1
(iii) to the pair

-\beta,\beta+2\alpha
we obtain
2\alpha\in R

and
F(\beta)\subset F(2\alpha)
.
Using Lemma
B.9.1
(ii)
we get
F(\beta)=F(2\alpha)=\mathbb{Z}c
for some
c\in\mathbb{C}
.
By above,
R
contains
\pm\beta
,
\pm\alpha

\pm 2\alpha
and
\pm(\beta+2\alpha)
; these elements form
B(1|1)

and we identify this subset of
R
with
\operatorname{cl}_{0}(R)
.
By Lemma
B.9.1
(ii),

F(\delta_{1})=\mathbb{Z}a
for some
a\in\mathbb{C}
.
Summarizing we have

F(\varepsilon_{1})=F(\delta_{1})=\mathbb{Z}a,\ \ F(\pm\varepsilon_{1}\pm\delta_{1})=F(\varepsilon_{1}-\delta_{1})=F(2\delta_{1})=\mathbb{Z}c\subset\mathbb{Z}a.

By Lemma
B.9.1
(ii), for any
b\in F(\varepsilon_{1}-\delta_{1})

we have
(b+2a)\in F(\varepsilon_{1}-\delta_{1})
.
Thus either
c=\pm a
or
c=\pm 2a
for
a\not=0
. In the first case
R=B(1|1)+\mathbb{Z}a
,
that is
R\cong B(1|1)
or

R\cong B(1|1)^{(1)}
; in the second case

R\cong D(2|1)^{(1)}
.

B.9.5.

Consider the case when
R
satisfies (GR2) and

\operatorname{cl}_{0}(R)
is of type
BC(1|1)
that is

\operatorname{cl}_{0}(R)=\{\pm\varepsilon_{1},\pm\varepsilon_{1}\pm\delta_{1},\pm\delta_{1},\pm 2\delta_{1},\pm 2\varepsilon_{1}\}

where
(\varepsilon_{1},\delta_{1})=0
,
(\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{1})=-(\delta_{1},\delta_{1})=1
.

Take
\beta,\alpha
in
R
such that
\operatorname{cl}_{0}(\beta)=\varepsilon_{1}-\delta_{1}

and
\operatorname{cl}_{0}(\alpha)=\delta_{1}
. Notice that
R
lies in the span
of
\alpha,\beta
and
\delta_{0}
. We identify

\alpha,\beta

with
\delta_{1}
and
\varepsilon_{1}-\delta_{1}
.
Then
R
contains
\pm\delta_{1}
and
\pm\varepsilon_{1}\pm\delta_{1}
.
By Lemma
B.9.1
(i),
F(\delta_{1})=\mathbb{Z}a
for some
a\in\mathbb{C}
.
Lemma
B.9.1
(ii) gives
\varepsilon_{1}\in R

and
F(\delta_{1}),F(\varepsilon_{1}-\delta_{1})\subset F(\varepsilon_{1})
.
Similarly,
F(\varepsilon_{1})\subset F(\delta_{1})
. Hence

F(\varepsilon_{1})=\mathbb{Z}a
and
F(\varepsilon_{1}-\delta_{1})\subset\mathbb{Z}a
. By Lemma
B.9.1
(i),

F(\varepsilon_{1}-\delta_{1})+2\mathbb{Z}a=F(\varepsilon_{1}-\delta_{1}).

Therefore

(B.4)

F(\pm\varepsilon_{1}\pm\delta_{1})=F(\varepsilon_{1}-\delta_{1})\text{ is $\mathbb{Z}a$ or $2\mathbb{Z}a$},\ \ \ \ F(\varepsilon_{1})=F(\delta_{1})=\mathbb{Z}a.

for some
a\in\mathbb{C}
.
Since
R
contains

\varepsilon_{1}\pm\delta_{1}
and satisfies (GR2),
R
contains either

2\delta_{1}
or
2\varepsilon_{1}
. The case
2\varepsilon_{1}\in R
reduces to the case

2\delta_{1}\in R
by
permuting the roles of
\varepsilon_{1}
and
\delta_{1}
.
Thus, without loss of generality, we can (and will)
assume that
2\delta_{1}\in R
. Applying Lemma
B.9.1
(i)
to the pair
2\delta_{1}
and
\delta_{1}
we get

4\mathbb{Z}a\subset F(2\delta_{1})
. On the other hand, Lemma
B.9.1
(ii) gives
F(2\delta_{1})\subset F(\varepsilon_{1}+\delta_{1})
.
Thus

(B.5)

4\mathbb{Z}a\subset F(2\delta_{1})\subset F(\varepsilon_{1}-\delta_{1}).

Set
Y:=\{y\in\mathbb{C}|\ 2\varepsilon_{1}+y\delta_{0}\in R\}
.
Since
2\varepsilon_{1}\in\operatorname{cl}_{0}(R)
,
Y

is not empty. For
y\in Y
one has

s_{2\varepsilon_{1}+y\delta}(\delta_{1}-\varepsilon_{1})=\delta_{1}+\varepsilon_{1}+y\delta\in R

so
y\in F(\varepsilon_{1}-\delta_{1})
.
For each
x\in F(\varepsilon_{1}-\delta_{1})
,

R
contains
\varepsilon_{1}-\delta_{1}

and
\varepsilon_{1}+\delta_{1}+x\delta_{0}
, so, by (GR2), the set

\{2\varepsilon_{1}+x\delta_{0},2\delta_{1}+x\delta_{0}\}\cap R

has the cardinality one. Therefore

F(\varepsilon_{1}-\delta_{1})=F(2\delta_{1})\coprod Y
.
By Lemma
B.9.1
(i) we have

F(2\delta_{1})=\mathbb{Z}c

and
Y=y_{0}+\mathbb{Z}d
for some
c,d,y\in\mathbb{C}
.
Applying Lemma
B.9.1
(i)
to the pair
2\varepsilon_{1}+y\delta_{0}
and
\varepsilon_{1}
we get

4\mathbb{Z}a\subset\mathbb{Z}d
.
Using (
B.4
) and (
B.4
)
we obtain

4\mathbb{Z}a\subset\mathbb{Z}c,\ \ \ c,d,y_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}a,\ \ \ 2a\in F(\pm\varepsilon_{1}\pm\delta_{1})=\mathbb{Z}c\coprod(y_{0}+\mathbb{Z}d).

This gives the following possibilities:

\begin{array}[]{lllll}(1)&F(2\delta_{1})=2\mathbb{Z}a,&F(\varepsilon_{1}-\delta_{1})=\mathbb{Z}a,&2\varepsilon_{1}+\delta_{0}\in R,&F(2\varepsilon_{1}+\delta_{0})=2\mathbb{Z}a\\
(2)&F(2\delta_{1})=4\mathbb{Z}a,&F(\varepsilon_{1}-\delta_{1})=2\mathbb{Z}a,&2\varepsilon_{1}+2\delta_{0}\in R,&F(2\varepsilon_{1}+\delta_{0})=4\mathbb{Z}a.\\
\end{array}

We have
R\cong\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})
, where

\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
is of type
A(2|1)^{(2)}
for (1),
and of type
A(2|2)^{(4)}
for (2).

We will use the following observation: if
\gamma\in R
is such that

\operatorname{cl}(\gamma)=\pm 2\delta_{1}
,
then
\gamma/2\in R
(this holds,
because
s/2\in F(\delta_{1})
for any
s\in F(2\delta_{1})
).

B.9.6.

Let
\operatorname{cl}_{0}(R)
be of the type
A(m-1|n-1)
for
m,n\geq 1

with
mn>1
, or
\Delta(\mathfrak{psl}(n|n))
for
n\geq 3
.

We consider the distinguished base

\{\alpha^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m+n-1}

in
\operatorname{cl}_{0}(R)=A(m-1|n-1)
:

\alpha^{\prime}_{i}:=\varepsilon_{i}-\varepsilon_{i+1}

for
i=1,\ldots,m
,
\alpha^{\prime}_{m+j})=\delta_{j}-\delta_{j+1}

for
j=1,\ldots,n
and
\alpha^{\prime}_{m}:=\varepsilon_{m}-\delta_{1}
.
Let
\alpha_{i}\in R

be such that
\operatorname{cl}_{0}(\alpha_{i})=\alpha^{\prime}_{i}
.
Let
E
be the span of
\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m+n-1}
.

Set
\dot{R}:=R\cap E
. Then
\dot{R}

satisfies (GR0)–(GR2) and contains
\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m+n-1}
.
The Gram matrix of
\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m+n-1}
is the
Gram matrix of
\{\alpha^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m+n-1}
. Therefore

s_{\alpha_{i}}
for
i=1,\ldots,m+n-1
,
i\not=m
,
generate the group isomorphic to
W[\operatorname{cl}_{0}(R)]
.
Since any element in
\operatorname{cl}_{0}(R)=A(m-1|n-1)

is conjugated to one of the elements in

\{\alpha^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m+n-1}\cup\{-\alpha_{m}\}
,
the restriction of
\operatorname{cl}_{0}
to
\dot{R}

gives a surjective map
\dot{R}\to\operatorname{cl}_{0}(R)
.
If the restriction of

\operatorname{cl}_{0}
to
\mathbb{C}\dot{R}
is injective, then

\dot{R}\cong\operatorname{cl}_{0}(R)
.

For
m\not=n
,
the Gram matrix of
\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m+n-1}
is
non-degenerate, so
\dot{R}\cong A(m-1|n-1)
.
For
m=n
the Gram matrix of
\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m+n-1}

has a one-dimensional kernel,
so
\dot{R}
is isomorphic to

\Delta(\mathfrak{psl}(n|n))

or to
A(n-1|n-1)=\Delta(\mathfrak{gl}(n|n))
.
Notice that if
R\subset\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}(\mathfrak{g})
, then

\dot{R}\cong A(m-1|n-1)
, by Remark
B.4.3
.

B.10.
Set
\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)

From now on
\mathfrak{g}
is an indecomposable symmetrizable
finite-dimensional or affine
Kac-Moody superalgebra with the set of real roots

\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}
. If
\mathfrak{g}
is finite-dimensional, we set
\delta:=0
.
In all cases

\operatorname{cl}:\mathbb{C}\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}\to\mathbb{C}\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}/\mathbb{C}\delta
is the canonical map.

From now on
R\subset\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}
is an
indecomposable set
satisfying (GR0)–(GR3) and containing an isotropic root.
As before,
K=\{v\in\mathbb{C}R|\ (v,R)=0\}
and
we let
\operatorname{cl}_{K}:\mathbb{C}R\to\mathbb{C}R/K
be the canonical map.
The bilinear form
(-,-)

induces a non-degenerate bilinear form on
\mathbb{C}R/K
.

B.10.1.

Lemma.

Let
R\subset\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}
be an
indecomposable set
satisfying (GR0)–(GR3).
Then

\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)\subset\mathbb{C}R/K
is a finite indecomposable set
satisfying (GR0), (GR1), (WGR2) and (GR3).

Proof.

It is clear that
\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)\subset\mathbb{C}R/K

is an indecomposable set
satisfying (GR0), (GR1), (WGR2) and (GR3). It remains to verify
that
\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)
is finite. Suppose that
R
is infinite.
Since
\operatorname{cl}(\Delta^{\operatorname{re}})
is a finite set,
one has
\operatorname{cl}(\alpha)=\operatorname{cl}(\gamma)

for some
\alpha,\gamma\in R
, so
\delta\in\mathbb{C}R
.
Then
\delta\in K
, so
\operatorname{cl}_{K}
can be decomposed
as
\operatorname{cl}_{K}=\operatorname{cl}_{K}^{\prime}\circ\operatorname{cl}
, where

\operatorname{cl}_{K}^{\prime}:\mathbb{C}R/\mathbb{C}\delta\to\mathbb{C}R/K

is the canonical map. Since
\operatorname{cl}(R)\subset\operatorname{cl}(\Delta^{\operatorname{re}})
is a finite set,
\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)
is finite.
∎

B.10.2.

By Lemma
B.10.1
,

\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)
is one of the sets
described in
\lx@sectionsign

B.3.2
, i.e.

\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)
lies in the list:

A(m-1|n-1)\text{ for }m\not=n,\ B(m|n),D(m|n),C(m|n),BC(m|n),D(2|1,a),G(3),F(4),

where
m,n\geq 1
,
or
\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)\cong\Delta(\mathfrak{psl}(n|n))
for
n\geq 2
.
(Recall that
A(n-1|n-1)

stands for
\Delta(\mathfrak{gl}(n|n))
).
For
D(2|1,a),G(3),F(4)
we set
n=1
and
m=1,2,3
respectively.
If
\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)\not=D(2|1,a),F(4)
, we view
\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)

as a subset of
L_{m,n}
(see
\lx@sectionsign

B.2.1
for notation).

B.11.
Case
\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)\not=C(1|1)

Our goal is to show that in this case

R\cong\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})
.
The cases when
\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)
is
A(1|0)
,
B(1|1)
,
BC(1|1)

were treated in
\lx@sectionsign

B.9
. Now we consider the remaining cases,
i.e.
mn\geq 2
(and
mn\geq 3
for
A(m|n)
, since
A(1|0)

corresponds to
m=2
,
n=1
).

B.11.1.

If
\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)\not=D(2|1,a),F(4)
we view
\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)

as a subset of
L_{m,n}
and set

E^{\prime}:=\{\sum_{i=1}^{m}x_{i}\varepsilon_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}y_{j}\delta_{j}|\ \sum_{i=1}^{m}x_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}y_{j}=0,x_{i},y_{j}\in\mathbb{C}\}.

For
\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)=D(2|1,a)
take
E^{\prime}
spanned by two isotropic roots
in
\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)
, and
for
\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)=F(4)
take
E^{\prime}
spanned by

(\delta_{1}-\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2}-\varepsilon_{3})/2
,

\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2}
and
\varepsilon_{2}-\varepsilon_{3}
.

One readily sees that if
\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)\not\cong\Delta(\mathfrak{psl}(n|n))
,
then

\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)\cap E^{\prime}\cong A(m-1|n-1)

and this is a maximal root subsystem of type

A(i|j)
contained in
\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)
. For
\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)\cong\Delta(\mathfrak{psl}(n|n))

we have
\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)\cap E^{\prime}=\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)
.

Let
\{\alpha^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m+n-1}

be as in
\lx@sectionsign

B.9.6
. Fix
\alpha_{i}\in R

such that
\operatorname{cl}_{K}(\alpha_{i})=\alpha^{\prime}_{i}

and let
E
be the span of
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{m+n}
.
By
\lx@sectionsign

B.9.6
,
R\cap E

contains
R^{\prime}
such that

R^{\prime}\cong A(m-1|n-1).

B.11.2.

Lemma.

We have

(i)

K\cap\mathbb{C}R^{\prime}=0

if

\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)\not\cong\Delta(\mathfrak{psl}(n|n))
for
n\geq 3
;

(ii)

\{\gamma\in R|\ \operatorname{cl}_{K}(\gamma)\in E^{\prime}\}=R^{\prime}+\mathbb{Z}a\delta\ \ \text{ for some }a\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

For
m\not=n
,
R^{\prime}
isomorphic to

A(m-1|n-1)
and the restriction of
(-,-)
to

R^{\prime}
is non-degenerate, so
K\cap\mathbb{C}R^{\prime}=0
.
Consider the case
m=n
. Let
\beta_{i}\in R^{\prime}
be such that

\operatorname{cl}_{K}(\beta_{i})=\varepsilon_{i}-\delta_{i}
.
The kernel of the restriction of
(-,-)
to
\mathbb{C}R^{\prime}

is spanned by

\mu:=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\beta_{i}
.
Therefore
K\cap\mathbb{C}R^{\prime}=K\cap\mathbb{C}\mu
.
Hence the restriction of
\operatorname{cl}_{K}
to
R^{\prime}
is injective
(since
n>1
, so
\alpha-\beta\not\in\mathbb{C}\mu

if
\alpha,\beta\in R^{\prime}
and
\alpha\not=\beta
).

If

\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)\not\cong\Delta(\mathfrak{psl}(n|n))
, then

\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)
is
D(n|n)
or
B(n|n)
, so

2\delta_{1}\in\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)
. Taking

\gamma\in R
such that
\operatorname{cl}_{K}(\gamma)=2\delta_{1}

we obtain
(\gamma,\beta_{i})=(2\delta_{1},\varepsilon_{1}-\delta_{1})\not=0

and
(\gamma,\beta_{i})=0
for
i>1
, that is

(\mu,\gamma)\not=0
, so
\mu\not\in K
.
Hence
\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)\not\cong\Delta(\mathfrak{psl}(n|n))
forces

K\cap\mathbb{C}R^{\prime}=0
. This completes the proof of (i).

For (ii) recall that
\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)\cap E^{\prime}

is isomorphic to
A(m-1|n-1)

if
\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)\not\cong\Delta(\mathfrak{psl}(n|n))

and
\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)\cap E^{\prime}=\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)
if

\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)\cong\Delta(\mathfrak{psl}(n|n))
.
Since
R^{\prime}\cong A(m-1|n-1)
, we have
\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R^{\prime})=\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)
.
Assume that

\{\gamma\in R|\ \operatorname{cl}_{K}(\gamma)\in\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R^{\prime})\}\not=R^{\prime}
.
Take
\gamma\in R

such that
\operatorname{cl}_{K}(\gamma)\in E^{\prime}
and
\gamma\not\in R^{\prime}
.
Since
\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R^{\prime})=\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)
,

\operatorname{cl}_{K}(\gamma)=\operatorname{cl}_{K}(\alpha)
for some
\alpha\in R^{\prime}
.
Set
\delta_{0}:=\gamma-\alpha
and define

F(\alpha)
as in
\lx@sectionsign

B.9
. Since
\gamma\in R

we have
1\ F(\alpha)
, so

\mathbb{Z}\subset F(\alpha)
by
\lx@sectionsign

B.9.3
.
Therefore
\operatorname{cl}(\alpha+\mathbb{Z}\delta_{0})=\operatorname{cl}(\alpha)+\mathbb{Z}\operatorname{cl}(\delta_{0})

lies in
\operatorname{cl}(R)\subset\operatorname{cl}(\Delta^{\operatorname{re}})
which is finite.
Hence
\operatorname{cl}(\delta_{0})=0
, so
\delta_{0}\in\mathbb{C}\delta
.
This establishes (ii).
∎

B.11.3.

Consider the case
\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)=A(m-1|n-1)
.
Since
R^{\prime}\cong A(m-1|n-1)
and
mn\geq 2
,
the restriction of
\operatorname{cl}_{K}

to
R^{\prime}
is injective.
Since
R^{\prime}
and
\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)
contain
the same number of elements, we have

\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)=\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R^{\prime})
. By Lemma
B.11.2
(ii),
we obtain

R=R^{\prime}+\mathbb{Z}j\delta
, so

R
is isomorphic to
\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})

for
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
of type
A(m-1|n-1)
.

B.11.4.

Now consider the cases when
\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)\not\cong A(m-1|n-1)
.

Retain notation of
B.11.1
. Recall that
R^{\prime}\cong A(m-1|n-1)
.
By Lemma
B.11.2
(i),
K\cap\mathbb{C}R^{\prime}=0
, so the span of

\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R^{\prime})
is of dimension
m+n-1
. Since

\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)\not\cong A(m-1|n-1)
,
the span of
\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)
is of dimension
m+n
.
In each case we choose
\alpha^{\prime}_{m+n}\in\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)

which does not lie in
\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R^{\prime})
:
if
\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)
is
B(m|n)
or
BC(m|n)

we take
\alpha^{\prime}_{m+n}:=\delta_{n}
;
in other cases we take
\alpha^{\prime}_{m+n}
arbitrary.

Then we choose
\alpha_{m+n}

such that
\operatorname{cl}_{K}(\alpha_{m+n})=\alpha^{\prime}_{m+n}
.
We denote by
V
the span of
\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m+n}
.
We set

\dot{R}:=V\cap R.

Since
\{\alpha^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m+n}
is a basis of
\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)

we identify
\mathbb{C}R/K
with
V
. Then

\dot{R}\subset\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)
.

We have
\dim V=m+n
and

\dot{R}\subset V
satisfies (GR0)–(GR2)
(since
R
satisfies these properties).
The Gram matrices of
\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m+n}

and of
\{\alpha^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m+n}
coincide
and the latter is the Gram matrix of a distinguished base
of a root system which is not of type
A(m-1|n-1)
.
Thus this matrix is invertible, so the restriction
of
(-,-)
to
V
is non-degenerate. By Serganova’s classification (
\lx@sectionsign

B.3.2
)

\dot{R}
is the root system of a finite-dimensional Kac-Moody
superalgebra. Recall that

(B.6)

\dot{R}\subset R,\ \ A(m-1|n-1)\cong R^{\prime}\subsetneq\dot{R}\subset\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R),\ \ \ \mathbb{C}\dot{R}=\mathbb{C}\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)

and that
\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)
is not the types

A(i|j)
or
\Delta(\mathfrak{psl}(i|i))
for all
i,
.
If
\dot{R}\not=\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)
, then
\dot{R}\subset\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)

is one of the following inclusions:

D(m|n)\subset B(m|n),\ \ D(m|n)\subset C(m|n),\ \ D(m|n)\subset BC(m|n),\ \ B(m|n)\subset BC(m|n).

If
\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)
is
B(m|n)
or
BC(m|n)
,
\dot{R}

contains
\alpha_{m+n}=\delta_{n}
, so
\dot{R}

is not of the type
D(m|n)
.
Hence
\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)=\dot{R}

if
\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)\not=C(m|n)
,
BC(m|n)
,

\dot{R}=D(m|n)
if
\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)\not=C(m|n)
,
and
\dot{R}=B(m|n)
if
BC(m|n)
.

B.11.5.

Take a non-zero
\delta_{0}\in K
.
By Lemma
B.2.9
, any isotropic root in
\beta\in\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)

is
W[\dot{R}]
-conjugate to a root
in
R^{\prime}
. By Lemma
B.9.1
,
F(\beta)=0
if
\delta_{0}\not\in\mathbb{C}\delta

and

F(\beta)=\mathbb{Z}a
for
\delta_{0}=\delta
, where
a

is as in Lemma
B.11.2
(ii).

If
\gamma\in R
is anisotropic, then, by Lemma
B.9.1
,

F(\gamma)=\mathbb{Z}c\delta_{0}
for some
c\in\mathbb{C}
.
Since
\operatorname{cl}(R)\subset\operatorname{cl}(\Delta^{\operatorname{re}})

is finite,
\operatorname{cl}(c\delta_{0})=0
.
We conclude that
F(\alpha)=0
for each
\alpha\in R
if

\delta_{0}\in\mathbb{C}\delta
. Since
\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)\subset V=\mathbb{C}\dot{R}

we obtain

R\subset(V+\mathbb{C}\delta).

B.11.6.

Corollary.

Let
\dot{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}

be the finite-dimensional Kac-Moody superalgebra
with the root system
\dot{R}
.
If
\dot{R}=\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)
, then

R\cong\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}({\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}})
, where
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
is either

\dot{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}
, or
the affinization of
\dot{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}
,
or
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}\cong D(m+1|n)^{(2)}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
B.2.9
,
\dot{R}
contains an isotropic

\beta
such that
(\beta,\gamma)\not=0
.
By
\lx@sectionsign\lx@sectionsign

B.9.3
,
B.9.4
we have

c=\pm a
if
\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)\cap(\mathbb{C}\beta+\mathbb{C}\gamma)

is of type
A(1|0)
, and
c\in\{\pm a,\pm a/2\}

if this is of type
B(1|1)
.

If
c=\pm a
, then

R=\dot{R}+\mathbb{Z}a\delta
, and

R=\dot{R}\cong\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}(\dot{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}})

if
a=0
and
R\cong\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}({\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}})

where
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
is the affinization of
\dot{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}

if
a\not=0
.

If
a\not=0
and

c\not=\pm a
, then
\dot{R}=B(m|n)
or
G(3)
.
In this case
\lx@sectionsign

B.9.4
gives

F(\alpha_{s})=\mathbb{Z}\frac{a}{2}
if
\alpha_{s}\in\dot{R}

is a short root and
F(\alpha)=\mathbb{Z}a

otherwise. If
\dot{R}=\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)

is of type
B(m|n)
, this gives
R\cong\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}({\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}})

where
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}=D(m+1|n)^{(2)}
.
If
\dot{R}=G(3)
, then, for a long root
\alpha_{l}

in
G(2)
we have
\langle\alpha_{l},\alpha_{s}^{\vee}\rangle=\pm 3
,
so Lemma
B.9.1

(ii) gives
3F(\alpha_{s})\subset F(\alpha_{l})

which contradicts to
F(\alpha_{s})=\mathbb{Z}\frac{a}{2}

and
F(\alpha_{l})=\mathbb{Z}a
.
∎

B.11.7.
Case
C(m|n)
with
mn\geq 2

Without loss of generality we can (and will) assume
n\geq 2
.
Recall that
R\subset V\oplus\mathbb{C}\delta
.
Set
\delta_{0}:=\delta
.
By Lemma
B.9.1
,
F(\alpha)=\mathbb{Z}a

for any
\alpha\in W[\dot{R}]R^{\prime}
that is
for all
\alpha\in\dot{R}
with
\alpha\not=\pm 2\delta_{j}

for
i=1,\ldots,m
.

Set

\beta=\delta_{1}-\varepsilon_{1}.

Applying Lemma
B.9.1
(ii) to the pair
\beta

and
2\delta_{1}
,
we obtain
F(2\delta_{1})\subset\mathbb{Z}a
.
Since
\langle 2\delta_{1},(\delta_{1}-\delta_{2})^{\vee}\rangle=2

we get
2\mathbb{Z}a\subset F(2\delta_{1})
.
Hence
F(2\delta_{1})
is
\mathbb{Z}a
or
2\mathbb{Z}a
.

Set

Y:=\{y\in\mathbb{C}|\ 2\varepsilon_{1}+y\delta\in R\}.

Since
\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)=C(m|n)
and
R\subset V+\mathbb{C}\delta
,
Y

is not empty. For
y\in Y
one has

s_{2\varepsilon_{1}+y\delta}(\beta_{1})=\beta_{1}+(2\varepsilon_{1}+y\delta)=\delta_{1}+\varepsilon_{1}+y\delta\in R

so
y\in\mathbb{Z}a
(since
F(\delta_{1}+\varepsilon_{1})=\mathbb{Z}a
).
Thus
Y
is a non-empty subset of
\mathbb{Z}a
.
For each
i\in\mathbb{Z}
,

R
contains
\varepsilon_{1}-\delta_{1}

and
\varepsilon_{1}+\delta_{1}+ai\delta
, so the set

\{2\varepsilon_{1}+ai\delta,2\delta_{1}+ai\delta\}\cap R

has the cardinality one. Therefore

\mathbb{Z}a=F(2\delta_{1})\coprod Y.

Since
F(2\delta_{1})
is
\mathbb{Z}a
or
2\mathbb{Z}a
,
and
Y
is non-empty, we obtain

F(2\delta_{1})=2\mathbb{Z}a
,

Y=a+2\mathbb{Z}a
and
a\not=0
.

Acting by
W[\dot{R}]

we obtain

F(2\delta_{j})=2\mathbb{Z}a,\ \ \ \ \{y\in\mathbb{C}|\ 2\varepsilon_{i}+y\delta\in R\}a+2\mathbb{Z}a

for
j=1,\ldots,n

and
i=1,\ldots,m
. This gives

R=\{\alpha+\mathbb{Z}a\delta|\ \alpha\in D(m|n),\ \alpha\not=\pm 2\delta_{j}\}\cup\{\pm 2\delta_{j}+\mathbb{Z}a\delta,\pm 2\varepsilon_{i}+a\delta+2\mathbb{Z}a\delta\}

(where
i=1,\ldots,m
and

j=1,\ldots,n
). Hence

R\cong\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})

for
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}=A(2m-1|2n-1)^{(2)}
.

B.11.8.
Case
BC(m|n)
with
mn\geq 2

Without loss of generality we can (and will) assume
n\geq 2
.
By
\lx@sectionsign\lx@sectionsign

B.11.4
,
B.11.5
,
R
contains
\dot{R}\cong B(m|n)

and
R\subset(\mathbb{C}\dot{R}\oplus\mathbb{C}\delta)
.
Set
\delta_{0}:=\delta
. Using Lemma
B.11.2
(ii)
and the fact
that
F(w\alpha)=F(\alpha)
for

w\in W[R]
and
\alpha\in R

we get

F(\pm\varepsilon_{i}\pm\delta_{j})=F(\pm\varepsilon_{i}\pm\varepsilon_{i_{1}})=F(\pm\delta_{j}\pm\delta_{j_{1}})=\mathbb{Z}a

for
1\leq i\not=i_{1}\leq m
and
1\leq j\not=j_{1}\leq n
.
Now
\lx@sectionsign

B.9.5
gives the following possibilities

\begin{array}[]{lllll}(1)&F(2\delta_{j})=2\mathbb{Z}a,&F(\varepsilon_{i})=F(\delta_{i})=\mathbb{Z}a&2\varepsilon_{i}+\delta\in R,&F(2\varepsilon_{i}+\delta)=2\mathbb{Z}a\\
(2)&F(2\delta_{j})=2\mathbb{Z}a,&F(\varepsilon_{i})=F(\delta_{j})=\mathbb{Z}a/2&2\varepsilon_{i}+2\delta\in R,&F(2\varepsilon_{i}+\delta)=2\mathbb{Z}a.\\
\end{array}

Hence
R\cong\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})
, where

\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
is of type
A(2m+1|2n)^{(2)}
for (1),
and of type
A(2m|2n)^{(4)}
for (2).

B.12.
Case
\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)=C(1|1)

Take
\alpha_{1}
,
\alpha_{2}
, and
\beta
in
R
such that
\operatorname{cl}_{K}(\alpha_{1})=-2\varepsilon_{1}
,

\operatorname{cl}_{K}(\alpha_{2})=2\delta_{1}
, and

\operatorname{cl}_{K}(\beta)=\varepsilon_{1}-\delta_{1}
.
We normalize the form
(-,-)
by the condition

(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{1})=2
.
Let
V
be the span of
\alpha_{1}
,
\alpha_{2}
and
\beta
. Set

\xi:=2\beta+\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}.

B.12.1.

Lemma.

The vectors
\alpha_{1}
,
\alpha_{2}
,
\beta
are linearly independent.
If
\xi
is not proportional to
\delta
, then

\alpha_{1}
,
\alpha_{2}
,

\beta
and
\delta
are linearly independent.

Proof.

Since
R
contains
\beta
and
s_{\alpha_{2}}\beta=\beta+\alpha_{2}
,
one has
2\beta+\alpha_{2}\not\in R
(by (GR2)), so

\xi\not=0
.

Notice that the kernel of the Gram matrix of
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\beta,\delta

is spanned by
\xi
and
\delta
. Since
\xi\not=0
, the vectors

\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\beta
are linearly independent.
If
\xi
is not proportional to
\delta
, then

\alpha_{1}
,
\alpha_{2}
,

\beta
and
\delta
are linearly independent.
∎

B.12.2.

The non-isotropic elements in
\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)
are
\pm\operatorname{cl}_{K}(\alpha_{i})

for
i=1,2
. Using Lemma
B.9.1
we obtain

(B.7)

R^{\operatorname{an}}=\{\pm\alpha_{1}+\mathbb{Z}a_{1}\delta\}\coprod\{\pm\alpha_{1}+\mathbb{Z}a_{1}\delta\}

for some
a_{1},a_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.Let
W^{\prime}
be the image of
W[R]
in
GL(\mathbb{C}R)
.
By Lemma
B.2.9
(ii),

R^{\operatorname{iso}}=-W^{\prime}\beta\cup W^{\prime}\beta.

Since
s_{\alpha_{i}}s_{\alpha_{i}+s\delta}\beta=\beta+s\delta

for any
s\in\mathbb{Z}

we have

(B.8)

W^{\prime}\beta=\{\beta+\mathbb{Z}a\delta,\beta+\alpha_{1}+\mathbb{Z}a\delta,\beta+\alpha_{2}+\mathbb{Z}a\delta,\beta+\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\mathbb{Z}a\delta\},

where
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
is
such that
\mathbb{Z}a_{1}+\mathbb{Z}a_{2}=\mathbb{Z}a

(i.e.,
a=0
if
a_{1}=a_{2}=0
, and

a
is the greatest common divisor of
a_{1},a_{2}
otherwise).

Since
R
contains
\beta+\alpha_{1}
,
\beta+\alpha_{2}
and

\beta+aj\delta\in R

for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, (GR2) gives

(B.9)

|\{\alpha_{1}-aj\delta,\xi-\alpha_{2}+aj\delta\}\cap R=|\{\alpha_{2}-aj\delta,\xi-\alpha_{1}+aj\delta\}\cap R|=1

where
|X|
stands for the cardinality of
X
.

B.12.3.
Case
\xi\not\in\mathbb{C}\delta

By Lemma
B.12.1
,
\beta
,
\alpha_{1}
,
\alpha_{2}
,
\delta

are linearly independent. This implies

W^{\prime}\beta\cap(-W^{\prime}\beta)=\emptyset
.
Moreover,
\xi-\alpha_{i}+aj\delta\not\in R
by (
B.7
)
for
i=1,2
. Combining (
B.7
) and (
B.9
) we conclude

a\in\mathbb{Z}a_{i}
for
i=1,2
, that is
a=a_{1}=a_{2}
.
Hence

R=\{\pm\alpha_{i}+\mathbb{Z}a\delta,\ \pm\beta+a\delta,\ \pm(\beta+\alpha_{i}+\mathbb{Z}a\delta),\ \pm(\beta+\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2})+\mathbb{Z}a\delta\}_{i=1,2}.

Since
\beta
,
\alpha_{1}
,
\alpha_{2}
,
\delta
are linearly independent, the map given by
\alpha_{1}\mapsto\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2}
,

\beta\mapsto\varepsilon_{2}-\delta_{1}
,

\alpha_{2}\mapsto\delta_{1}-\delta_{2}
,
a\delta\mapsto\delta

induces the isomorphism

R\cong\Delta(\mathfrak{gl}(2|2))=A(1|1)

if
a=0
and
R\cong A(1|1)^{(1)}
if
a\not=0
.

Note that
\operatorname{cl}(R)\cong A(1|1)
(
\cong\Delta(\mathfrak{gl}(2|2))
).

B.12.4.
Case
a=0

In this case
a_{1}=a_{2}=0
.
By Lemma
B.12.1
,
\beta
,
\alpha_{1}
,
\alpha_{2}

are linearly independent. This implies

W^{\prime}\beta\cap(-W^{\prime}\beta)=\emptyset
and

R=\{\pm\alpha_{i},\pm\beta,\pm(\beta+\alpha_{i}),\pm(\beta+\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2})\}_{i=1,2}\cong A(1|1),

where the isomorphism is the same as in
\lx@sectionsign

B.12.3
.
Note that
\operatorname{cl}(R)\cong A(1|1)
(
\cong\Delta(\mathfrak{gl}(2|2))
).

B.12.5.
Case
\xi\in\mathbb{C}\delta
,
a\not=0

Then
\xi=d\delta
for some
d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\not=0}
.

Recall that
a_{1},a_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
a_{1}+a_{2}\not=0
,
and
a

is the greatest common divisor of
a_{1},a_{2}
.
Combining (
B.7
) and (
B.9
) we conclude
that for each
j\in\mathbb{Z}
either
aj\in\mathbb{Z}a_{2}

or
aj+d\in\mathbb{Z}a_{1}
, and, similarly,
either
aj\in\mathbb{Z}a_{1}

or
aj+d\in\mathbb{Z}a_{2}
. Taking
j=0
we obtain

d\not\in\mathbb{Z}a_{i}
for
i=1,2
.
Then
aa_{1}+d\not\in\mathbb{Z}a_{1}
, so
aa_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}a_{2}
.
Similarly,
aa_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}a_{1}
. Therefore
a_{1},a_{2}\not=0
,
so
k_{1}:=\frac{a_{1}}{a}
,
k_{2}:=\frac{a_{2}}{a}
are coprime
positive integers.
By above,
ak_{1}|k_{2}
and
ak_{2}|k_{1}
, which implies
k_{1}=k_{2}=1
.
Hence
a_{1}=a_{2}=a
and
a\not|d
.

Let us show that

-W^{\prime}\beta\cap W^{\prime}\beta=\emptyset
.
We will use formula (
B.8
).
Take
\gamma\in-W^{\prime}\beta\cap W^{\prime}\beta
.
Then
\operatorname{cl}(\gamma)=\operatorname{cl}(w\beta)
for some
w\in W^{\prime}

and
w^{-1}\gamma\in-W^{\prime}\beta\cap W^{\prime}\beta
.
Thus, without loss of generality, we can assume that

\operatorname{cl}(\gamma)=\operatorname{cl}(\beta)
. In this case

\gamma=\beta+j_{1}a\delta=-\beta+\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+j_{2}a\delta

so

2\beta+\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}=d\delta=(j_{2}-j_{1})a\delta
which contradicts to
a\not|d
.

In the light of
\lx@sectionsign

B.12.2
we conclude that
R=R(a,d)

where

(B.10)

R(a,d)=\{\pm\alpha_{i}+\mathbb{Z}a\delta,\ \pm\beta+a\delta,\ \pm(\beta+\alpha_{i}+\mathbb{Z}a\delta),\ \pm(\beta+\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2})+\mathbb{Z}a\delta\}_{i=1,2}

where
\beta=\frac{d\delta-\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}}{2}

and
a\not|d
. One has
R(a,d)\cong R(a,d\pm 2a)

(the isomorphism is given by
\beta\mapsto\beta+a\delta
),

R(a,d)\cong R(a,-d)
(the isomorphism is given by

\beta\mapsto-\beta
,
\alpha_{i}\mapsto-\alpha_{i}
),
so we can assume that
a\geq 2d
.

One has

R(2d,d)\cong A(1|1)^{(2)}
(with the isomorphism given
by
\alpha_{1}\mapsto\delta-2\varepsilon_{1}
,
\beta\mapsto\varepsilon_{1}-\delta_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}\mapsto 2\delta_{1}
).

For
a\not=2d
, one has
R(a,d)\not\cong A(1|1)^{(2)}

(and
R
is not a bijective quotient of
\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})

for any
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
), since
for any isotropic root
\gamma\in R
and any non-zero
\delta_{0}\in K

we have

F(\gamma)=\mathbb{Z}xa\cup(\mathbb{Z}a-d)x,

where
\delta_{0}=x\delta
, and
F(\gamma)

forms a subgroup of
\mathbb{C}

if
a=2d
(or if
R
is a bijective quotient of
\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})
),
and does not form a subgroup if
a\not=2d
.

B.12.6.

Corollary.

Let
R\subset\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}(\mathfrak{g})
be such that
\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)\cong C(1|1)

(
\cong\Delta(\mathfrak{psl}(2|2))
. Then we have the following possibilities:

\begin{array}[]{lll}R\cong A(1|1)(=\Delta(\mathfrak{gl}(2|2))&\operatorname{cl}(R)=R,\\
R\cong A(1|1)^{(1)}&\operatorname{cl}(R)\cong A(1|1)\\
R\cong A(1|1)^{(2)}&\operatorname{cl}(R)=\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)\cong C(1|1)\\
R\cong R(a,d),a,d\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0},a>2d&\operatorname{cl}(R)=\operatorname{cl}_{K}(R)\cong C(1|1).\end{array}

This completes the proof of Proposition
B.6.7
(i).

B.13.
Proof of Proposition
B.6.7
(ii)

Now assume that
\Delta_{\lambda}
has an indecomposable component

R
such that
\operatorname{cl}(R)=C(1|1)
.

B.13.1.

Since
\operatorname{cl}(R)
does not satisfy (GR2),

\operatorname{cl}(\Delta^{\operatorname{re}})
does not satisfy (GR2), so

\operatorname{cl}(\Delta^{\operatorname{re}})
is isomorphic either to
BC(m|n)

for
mn\geq 1
or to
C(m|n)
for
mn>1
, and

\operatorname{cl}(R)=\{\pm 2\varepsilon_{i},\pm\varepsilon_{i}\pm\delta_{j},\pm 2\delta_{j}\}

for some
i,j
(
1\leq i\leq m
,
1\leq j\leq n
).

Take
\gamma\in R
such that
\operatorname{cl}(\gamma)=2\delta_{j}
.
Note that
\gamma/2\not\in R
(since
\operatorname{cl}(\gamma/2)=\delta_{i}\not\in\operatorname{cl}(R)
), so

\gamma/2\not\in\Delta_{\lambda}
.
By Corollary
4.1.6
,
\gamma/2\not\in\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}
.

If
\operatorname{cl}(\Delta^{\operatorname{re}})\cong BC(m|n)
, then

\operatorname{cl}(\mathbb{C}R\cap\Delta^{\operatorname{re}})\cong BC(1|1)
, so

\mathbb{C}R\cap\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}
is one of the real root systems
(i.e.,
A(2|1)^{(2)}
,
A(2|2)^{(4)}
)
described in
\lx@sectionsign

B.9.5
. For these root
system for any root
\gamma
satisfying
\operatorname{cl}(\gamma)=2\delta_{j}
,
\gamma/2
is a root
(see the observation in the end of
\lx@sectionsign

B.9.5
),
so
\gamma/2\in\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}
, a contradiction.

Hence
\operatorname{cl}(\Delta^{\operatorname{re}})=C(m|n)
, so, by
\lx@sectionsign

B.11.7
,

\mathfrak{g}
is of the type
A(2m-1|2n-1)^{(2)}
.

B.13.2.

Set
k:=(\lambda,\delta)
.
Since
(\alpha,\alpha)\in\{0,\pm 2\}
for any
\alpha\in R
,
we have
(\lambda,\alpha)\in\mathbb{Z}
for all
\alpha\in R_{\lambda}
,
so
(\lambda,\alpha)\in\mathbb{Z}
for all
\alpha\in R
.

Take a non-isotropic
\alpha\in R
.
Then
(\alpha,\alpha)=\pm 2
, so

\alpha+s\delta\in\Delta_{\lambda}

if and only if
\alpha+s\delta\in\Delta^{\operatorname{re}}

and
(\lambda,\alpha+s\delta)\in\mathbb{Z}
.
The first condition gives
2|s
and the second condition
gives
sk\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Recall that
R=R(a,d)
for some
a,d\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
,

a\geq 2d
. By (
B.10
),
\alpha+s\delta\in R

if and only if
a|s
.
We conclude that
a|s
is equivalent to
2|s

and
sk\in\mathbb{Z}
. Therefore
a
is even and

k=\frac{p}{q}
where
p,q
are coprime and either
q
is odd and

a=2q
or
q
is even and
a=q
.
Since
d\delta\in\mathbb{Z}R
we have

(\lambda,d\delta)=dk\in\mathbb{Z}
. Thus
d
is divisible by
q
.
Hence
a=2q
and
d=q
. Thus
R(a,d)\cong A(1|1)^{(2)}

and
q
is odd. This completes the proof of Proposition
B.6.7
(ii).
